Boundary layers and incompressible Navier-Stokes-Fourier limit of the Boltzmann Equation in Bounded Domain (I)
Ning Jiang, Nader Masmoudi

Abstract
. We establish the incompressible Navier-Stokes-Fourier limit for solutions to the Boltzmann equation with a general cut-off collision kernel in a bounded domain. Appropriately scaled families of DiPerna-Lions-(Mischler) renormalized solutions with Maxwell reflection boundary conditions are shown to have fluctuations that converge as the Knudsen number goes to zero. Every limit point is a weak solution to the Navier-Stokes-Fourier system with different types of boundary conditions depending on the ratio between the accommodation coefficient and the Knudsen number. The main new result of the paper is that this convergence is strong in the case of Dirichlet boundary condition. Indeed, we prove that the acoustic waves are damped immediately, namely they are damped in a boundary layer in time. This damping is due to the presence of viscous and kinetic boundary layers in space. As a consequence, we also justify the first correction to the infinitesimal Maxwellian that one obtains from the Chapman-Enskog expansion with Navier-Stokes scaling. This extends the work of Golse and Saint-Raymond [ 19 , 20 ] and Levermore and Masmoudi [ 27 ] to the case of a bounded domain. The case of a bounded domain was considered by Masmoudi and Saint-Raymond [ 33 ] for linear Stokes-Fourier limit and Saint-Raymond [ 40 ] for Navier-Stokes limit for hard potential kernels. Both [ 33 ] and [ 40 ] didn’t study the damping of the acoustic waves. This paper extends the result of [ 33 ] and [ 40 ] to the nonlinear case and includes soft potential kernels. More importantly, for the Dirichlet boundary condition, this work strengthens the convergence so as to make the boundary layer visible. This answers an open problem proposed by Ukai [ 45 ] .



1.
Introduction

The hydrodynamic limits from the Boltzmann equation got a lot of
interest in the previous two decades. Hydrodynamic regimes are those where the Knudsen number

\varepsilon
is small. The Knudsen number is the ratio of the mean free path and
the macroscopic length scales. The incompressible Navier-Stokes-Fourier (NSF) system can
be formally derived from the Boltzmann equation through a scaling in
which the fluctuations of the number density
F
about an absolute
Maxwellian
M
are scaled to be on the order
\varepsilon
, see
[
2
]
.

The program that justifies the hydrodynamic limits from the
Boltzmann equation in the framework of DiPerna-Lions
[
12
]
was
initiated by Bardos-Golse-Levermore
[
2
,
3
]
in late 80’s. Since then,
there has been lots of contributions to this program
[
4
,
13
,
19
,
20
,
23
,
27
,
30
,
31
,
33
,
39
]
. In particular the
work of Golse and Saint-Raymond
[
19
]
is the first complete rigorous justification of
NSF limit from the Boltzmann equation in a class of
bounded collision kernels, without making any nonlinear weak
compactness hypothesis. They have recently extended their result to
the case of hard potentials
[
20
]
. With some new nonlinear
estimates, Levermore and Masmoudi
[
27
]
treated a broader class
of collision kernels which includes all hard potential cases and,
for the first time in this program, soft potential cases.

All of the above mentioned works were carried out in either the
periodic spatial domain or the whole space, except for
[
33
]
and
[
40
]
.
In
[
33
]
, the linear Stokes-Fourier system was recovered with the same
collision kernels assumption as in
[
13
]
, while in
[
40
]
, the Navier-Stokes limit was derived with the same kernels assumption as in
[
20
]
, i.e. hard potential kernels. In
[
33
]
and
[
40
]
, the fluctuations of renormalized solutions to the Boltzmann equation in a bounded domain
(see
[
37
]
) was proved to pass to the limit and recovered
fluid boundary conditions, either Dirichlet, or Navier slip boundary condition, depending on the relative sizes of the
accommodation coefficient and the Knudsen number.

The dependance of the boundary conditions of the limiting fluid equations on the relative importance of the accommodation coefficient and the Knudsen number was observed by Sone and his collaborators. Their results, mostly formal, are presented in Chapter 3 and 4 in
[
44
]
for several types of kinetic boundary conditions. The work
[
33
]
and
[
40
]
rigorously justified the incompressible Stokes and Navier-Stokes equations from Boltzmann equation imposed with Maxwell reflection boundary condition.

In his survey paper
[
45
]
, Ukai proposed the following question:
“As far as the Boltzmann equation in a bounded domain is concerned, some progress has been made recently. In [37] , the convergence of the Boltzmann equation to the (linear) Stokes-Fourier equation was proved together with the convergence of the boundary conditions. It is a big challenging problem to extend the result to the nonlinear case and to strength the convergence so as to make visible the boundary layer.”
(In the above citation of Ukai’s survey, the reference
[37]
is the Saint-Raymond and Masmoudi’s paper
[
33
]
.)

In this paper and a forthcoming one, we study the incompressible NSF limit in a bounded domain from the Boltzmann equation with the Maxwell reflection boundary condition in which the accommodation might depend on the Knudsen number. We consider a bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
,
{\mathrm{D}}\geq 2
, with
boundary
{\partial\Omega}\in C^{2}
. The NSF system governs the
fluctuations of mass density, bulk velocity, and temperature
(\rho,\mathrm{u},\theta)
about their spatially homogeneous equilibrium
values in a Boussinesq regime. Specifically, after a suitable choice
of units, these dimensionless fluctuations satisfy the incompressibility and
Boussinesq relations

\nabla_{\!\!x}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}=0\,,\quad\rho+\theta=0\,,

(1.1)

while their evolution is determined by the Navier-Stokes and heat equations

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{u}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}+\nabla_{\!x}p=\nu\Delta_{\!x}\mathrm{u}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}|_{t=0}=\mathrm{u}_{0}\,,

(1.2)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\theta+\mathrm{u}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\theta=\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\kappa\Delta_{x}\theta\,,

\displaystyle\theta|_{t=0}=\theta_{0}\,,

where
\nu>0
is the kinematic viscosity and
\kappa>0
is the heat thermal conductivity.

Traditionally, two types of natural physical boundary conditions could be imposed for the incompressible NSF system
(
1.2
). The first is the homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition, namely,

\mathrm{u}=0\,,\quad\theta=0\!\quad\mbox{on}\!\!\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times{\partial\Omega}\,.

(1.3)

The other is the so-called Navier slip boundary condition, which was proposed by Navier
[
38
]
:

\displaystyle[2\nu d(\mathrm{u})\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}+\chi\mathrm{u}]^{\mathrm{tan}}=0\,,\quad\mathrm{u}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=0\quad\mbox{on}\!\!\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\partial\Omega\,,

(1.4)

\displaystyle\kappa\partial_{\mathrm{n}}\theta+\chi\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\theta=0\quad\mbox{on}\!\!\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\partial\Omega\,,

where
d(\mathrm{u})=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}+\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}^{\top})
denotes the symmetric part of the stress tensor and
{\partial}_{\mathrm{n}}

denotes the directional derivative along the outer normal vector

\mathrm{n}(x),x\in{\partial\Omega}
. In the above Navier boundary condition,
\chi>0
is
the reciprocal of the slip length which depends on the material of
the container.

In the current work, for general cut-off collision kernels, namely in the framework of
[
27
]
, we justify the NSF system. Regarding the
weak
convergence results, our proof is basically the same as in
[
33
]
and
[
40
]
: the boundary conditions of the limiting NSF system depend on the ratio of the accommodation coefficient and the Kundsen number, namely when
\frac{\alpha_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\infty
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, Dirichlet condition is derived, while when
\frac{\alpha_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\sqrt{2\pi}\chi
, the Navier-slip boundary condition is derived. The main difference is that
[
40
]
used the same renormalizations of
[
20
]
, applicable for hard potentials, while in the current work, we use the renormalization of
[
27
]
, which works for more general cut-off kernels, including soft potentials.

The main novelty of the current work
is the treatment of the Dirichlet boundary condition case.
Indeed, we prove that when
\frac{\alpha_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\infty
,
the convergence is
strong
. Furthermore, as a consequence of this strong convergence, the first correction to the infinitesimal Maxwellian, which is a quadratic term obtained from the Chapman-Enskog expansion with the Navier-Stokes scaling, is rigorously justified. We point out that in all the previous
works mentioned above, the convergence is in
w\mbox{-}L^{1}
, unless
the initial data is well-prepared, i.e. is hydrodynamic and satisfies the Boussinesq and
incompressibility relations. This weak convergence is caused by the
persistence of fast acoustic waves. In the Navier-Stokes regime, the
Reynold number
Re
is order
O(1)
, then the von
Kámán relation
\varepsilon=\frac{Ma}{Re}

implies that in the fluid limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, the Mach number

Ma
must go to zero. As is well know physically, one
expects that as
Ma\rightarrow 0
, fast acoustic waves are
generated and carry the energy of the potential part of the flow. For
the periodic flows, or for some particular boundary conditions such
as Navier condition (
1.4
), these waves subsist
forever and their frequency grows with
\varepsilon
. Mathematically,
this means that the convergence is only
weak
. This phenomenon
happens in many singular limits of fluid equations among which we only mention
[
28
,
29
]
.

One of the ingredients of the convergence proof is the treatment of the
acoustic waves which are highly oscillating. A compensated
compactness type argument was used by Lions and Masmoudi
[
30
]

to prove that these acoustic waves have no contribution on the equation satisfied by
the weak limit. This argument was previously used in the
compressible incompressible limit
[
29
]
.

In
[
10
]
, a striking phenomenon, namely the damping of acoustic waves
caused by the Dirichlet boundary condition was found by
Desjardins, Grenier, Lions, and Masmoudi in considering the
incompressible limit of the isentropic compressible Navier-Stokes equations. In
the case of a viscous flow in a bounded domain with Dirichlet
boundary condition, and under a generic assumption on the domain
(related to the so-called Schiffer’s conjecture and the Pompeiu
problem
[
9
]
), they showed that the acoustic waves are
instantaneously (asymptotically) damped, due to the formation of a
thin boundary layer in time. This layer is caused by a boundary
layer in space and dissipates the energy carried by the acoustic
waves. From a mathematical point of view, strong convergence
was obtained.

Inspired by the idea of
[
10
]
, the current paper considers the much more involved kinetic-fluid coupled case. We prove that if the accommodation coefficient is bigger than the Knudsen number, there is
no need for the argument in
[
29
]
since we can prove that the
acoustic waves are damped instantaneously. Our work is based on the
construction of viscous and kinetic Knudsen boundary layers of
size
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon
. The main idea is to use a family of test functions
which solve approximately a scaled stationary linearized Boltzmann equation and can
capture the propagation of the fast acoustic waves. These test
functions are constructed through considering a family of
approximate eigenfunctions of a
dual
operator with a
dual
kinetic boundary condition with respect to the original
Boltzmann equation. The approximate eigenvalue is the sum of several terms with different order of
\varepsilon
: the leading term is purely imaginary, which
describes the acoustic mode, and the real part of the next order term is strictly
negative
which gives the strict dissipation when applying the test functions to the
renormalized Boltzmann equation.

In contrast to
[
10
]
, the approximate eigenfunctions include interior part and two boundary layers: fluid viscous layer and kinetic Knudsen layer, while in
[
10
]
, only a fluid
boundary layer was necessary. Another important difference is that a generic
assumption on the domain had to be made in
[
10
]
(in
particular there are modes which are not damped in the disc), while in the current work,
this assumption is not needed. The reason is that we deal with the full acoustic system, namely including the temperature. The NSF system has also some dissipation in the temperature equation which is ignored
in the isentropic model. (in particular this dissipation property holds in the case of the ball). This was also considered in
[
24
]
in which we reinforced the result of
[
10
]
.

When the accommodation coefficient
\alpha_{\varepsilon}
is asymptotically larger than the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
in the sense that
\frac{\alpha_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\infty
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, the fluid limit is the NSF equations with Dirichlet boundary condition. For example, we can assume

\alpha_{\epsilon}=\chi\varepsilon^{\beta}
with
0\leq\beta<1
. We found that
\beta=\frac{1}{2}
is a threshold in the sense that the kinetic-fluid coupled boundary layers behave differently for
0\leq\beta<\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{1}{2}\leq\beta<1
, but for both cases the kinetic-fluid layers have damping effect. The current paper focuses on the threshold case
\beta=\frac{1}{2}
and we leave the other cases for a separate paper due to the more complex construction of the boundary layers.

One of the difficulties of the construction happens in the case the Laplace operator
-\Delta_{\!x}
with Neumann boundary condition has multiple eigenvalues. As a consequence, the dimension of the null space of the the operator
{\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}
is greater than one, where
{\mathcal{A}}
denotes the acoustic operator, and
\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}[\lambda^{k}_{0}]^{2}
are eigenvalues for
k\in\mathbb{N}
(for details see Section 5.2). Thus, as each stage of the construction of boundary layers, the terms in the null space of
{\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}
can not be determined uniquely. To completely determine all the terms in the ansaza of boundary layers, we have to add some orthogonality conditions. Surprisingly, all these orthogonality conditions are consistent, at least for the threshold case
\beta=\frac{1}{2}
treated in the current paper. Similar idea has been used in
[
24
]
which can be applied to the compressible-incompressible limit of the full Navier-Stokes-Fourier system in a bounded domain.

A key role is played by the linearized kinetic boundary layer equation in the coupling of viscous and kinetic layers. More specifically, its solvability provides the boundary conditions of the fluid variables in the interior and viscous boundary layers which satisfy the acoustic systems with source terms and second order ordinary differential equations respectively. This linearized kinetic boundary layer equation has been studied extensively (see
[
1
,
8
,
16
,
15
,
46
]
). Applying the boundary layer equations to construct the two layer eigenfunctions is the main novelty of the current paper. To the best of our knowledge these two layer eigenfunctions are new even in the applied literature.

The paper is organized as follows: the next section contains
preliminary material regarding the Boltzmann equation in a bounded
domain. We state the main theorems in Section 3 which include the
weak convergence for the Navier slip boundary and strong convergence
for the Dirichlet boundary. In Section 4, we list some differential
geometry properties of the boundary
{\partial\Omega}
as a submanifold of

\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
. Section 5 provides an introduction to the acoustic modes while
Section 6 is about the analysis of the kinetic boundary layer
equation whose solvability provides the boundary conditions of the
fluid variables. In Section 7, we present the constructions of the
test functions used in the proof of the Main Theorem. The proof of
the main proposition on the boundary layers is given in Sections 8 and 9.
In Section 10, we establish the weak convergence result of
the main theorem. Section 11 contains the proof of the strong
convergence in the Dirichlet boundary case using the test functions constructed in
Section 7.

2.
Boltzmann Equation in Bounded Domain

Here we introduce the Boltzmann equation in a bounded domain,
only so far as to set our notations, which are essentially those of

[
3
]
and
[
33
]
. More complete introduction to the
Boltzmann equation can be found in
[
6
,
7
,
17
,
44
]
.

2.1.
Maxwell Boundary Condition

We consider
\Omega
, a smooth bounded domain of
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
, and

\mathcal{O}=\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
, the space-velocity domain. Let

\mathrm{n}(x)
be the outward unit normal vector at
x\in{\partial\Omega}
and let
\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}
be
the Lebesgue measure on the boundary

{\partial\Omega}
. We define the outgoing and incoming sets
\Sigma_{+}
and

\Sigma_{-}
by

\Sigma_{\pm}=\{(x,v)\in\Sigma:\pm\mathrm{n}(x)\!\cdot\!v>0\}\quad\mbox{where}\quad\Sigma={\partial\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\,.

Denoted by
\gamma F
the trace of
F
over
\Sigma
, the boundary
condition takes the form of a balance between the values of the
outgoing and incoming parts of
\gamma F
, namely

\gamma_{\pm}F=\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{\pm}}\gamma F
. In order to
describe the interaction between particles and the wall, Maxwell

[
34
]
proposed in 1879 the following phenomenological law
which splits into a local reflection and a diffuse
reflection

\gamma_{-}F=(1-\alpha)L\gamma_{+}F+\alpha K\gamma_{+}F\quad\mbox{on}\quad\Sigma_{-}\,,

(2.1)

where
\alpha\in[0\,,1]
is a constant, called the “accommodation coefficient.” The local reflection operator
L
is given by

L\phi(x\,,v)=\phi(x\,,R_{x}v)\,,

(2.2)

where
R_{x}v=v-2\left[\mathrm{n}(x)\!\cdot\!v\right]\mathrm{n}(x)

is the velocity before the collision with the wall. The diffuse
reflection operator
K
is given by

K\phi(x\,,v)=\sqrt{2\pi}\tilde{\phi}(x)M(v)\,,

where
\tilde{\phi}
is the outgoing mass flux

\tilde{\phi}(x)=\int\limits_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)>0}\phi(x\,,v)v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}v\,,

and
M
is the absolute Maxwellian
M(v)=\tfrac{1}{(2\pi)^{{\mathrm{D}}/2}}\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}|v|^{2}\right)
,
that corresponds to the spatially homogeneous fluid state with
density and temperature equal to 1 and bulk velocity equals to 0. Furthermore, We notice that

\int\limits_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)>0}v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)\sqrt{2\pi}M(v)\,\mathrm{d}v=\int\limits_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)<0}|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\sqrt{2\pi}M(v)\,\mathrm{d}v=1\,,

which expresses the conservation of mass at the boundary. Here we take the temperature of the wall to be constant and equal to 1.

2.2.
Nondimensionalized Form of the Boltzmann Equation

We consider a sequence of renormalized solutions
F_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
to the rescaled Boltzmann equation

\displaystyle\varepsilon{\partial}_{t}{F_{\epsilon}}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}{F_{\epsilon}}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{B}({F_{\epsilon}}\,,{F_{\epsilon}})

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathcal{O}\,,

(2.3)

\displaystyle{F_{\epsilon}}(0\,,x\,,v)={F_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}}(x\,,v)\geq 0

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathcal{O}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}{F_{\epsilon}}=(1-\alpha)L\gamma_{+}{F_{\epsilon}}+\alpha K\gamma_{+}{F_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\Sigma_{-}\,.

The Boltzmann collision operator
\mathcal{B}
acts only on the
v
argument of

F
and is formally given by

\mathcal{B}(F\,,F)=\iint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{D-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}(F^{\prime}_{1}F^{\prime}-F_{1}F)b(\omega\,,v_{1}-v)\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,,

where
v_{1}
ranges over
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
endowed with its Lebesgue measure

\mathrm{d}v_{1}
, while
\omega
ranges over the unit sphere

\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}=\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}:|\omega|=1\}
endowed with its rotationally
invariant unit measure
\mathrm{d}\omega
. The
F^{\prime}_{1}\,,F^{\prime}\,,F_{1}\,,

and
F
appearing in the integrand designate
F(t\,,x\,,\cdot)

evaluated at the velocities
v^{\prime}_{1}\,,v^{\prime}\,,v_{1}
and
v
, respectively,
where the primed velocities are defined by

v^{\prime}_{1}=v_{1}-\omega[\omega\cdot(v_{1}-v)]\,,\quad v^{\prime}=v+\omega[\omega\cdot(v_{1}-v)]\,,

for any given
(\omega\,,v_{1}\,,v)\in\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
. This
expresses the conservation of momentum and energy for particle pairs
after a collision, namely,

v+v_{1}=v^{\prime}+v^{\prime}_{1}\,,\quad|v|^{2}+|v_{1}|^{2}=|v^{\prime}|^{2}+|v^{\prime}_{1}|^{2}\,.

The collision kernel
b
is a positive, locally integrable function and has
the classical form

b(\omega\,,v)=|v|\Sigma(|\omega\cdot\hat{v}|\,,|v|)\,,

where
\hat{v}=v/|v|
and
\Sigma
is the specific differential
cross section. This symmetry implies that the quantity
\int b(\omega\,,v)\,\mathrm{d}\omega
is a function of
|v|
only. The
DiPerna-Lions theory requires that
b
satisfies

\lim\limits_{|v|\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{1+|v|^{2}}\iint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times K}b(\omega\,,v_{1}-v)\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}=0

(2.4)

for any compact set

K\subset\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
. There are some additional assumptions on
b
needed
in
[
27
]
. For the convenience
of the reader, we list these assumptions here.

A major role will be played by the attenuation coefficient
a(v)
,
which is defined as

a(v)=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\bar{b}(v_{1}-v)M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}=\iint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}b(\omega,v_{1}-v)\,\mathrm{d}\omega M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,.

A few facts about
a(v)
are readily evident from what we have already assumed. Because (
2.4
) holds, one can show that

\lim\limits_{|v|\rightarrow\infty}\frac{a(v)}{1+|v|^{2}}=0\,.

(2.5)

Our
second assumption
regarding the
collision kernel
b
is that
a(v)
satisfies a lower bound of the form

C_{a}(1+|v|)^{\alpha}\leq a(v)\,,

(2.6)

for some
constant
C_{a}>0
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. The
third
assumption
is that there exists
s\in(1\,,\infty]
and

C_{b}\in(0\,,\infty)
such that

\left(\,\,\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\left|\frac{\bar{b}(v_{1}-v)}{a(v_{1})a(v)}\right|^{s}a(v_{1})M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}\leq C_{b}\,.

(2.7)

Another major role in what follows will be played by the linearized
around the global Maxwellian
M
collision operator
\mathcal{L}
,
which is defined by

\mathcal{L}\tilde{g}=\iint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}(\tilde{g}+\tilde{g}_{1}-\tilde{g}^{\prime}-\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{1})b(\omega\,,v_{1}-v)\,\mathrm{d}\omega M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,.

(2.8)

One has the decomposition

\frac{1}{a}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{K}^{-}-2\mathcal{K}^{+}\,,

where the loss operator
\mathcal{K}^{-}
and the gain operator

\mathcal{K}^{+}
are defined by

\mathcal{K}^{-}\tilde{g}=\frac{1}{a}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\tilde{g}_{1}\bar{b}(v_{1}-v)M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,,

\mathcal{K}^{+}\tilde{g}=\frac{1}{a}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}(\tilde{g}^{\prime}+\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{1})b(\omega,v_{1}-v)\,\mathrm{d}\omega M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,.

The
fourth assumption
regarding the
collision kernel
b
is that

\mathcal{K}^{+}:L^{2}(aM\mathrm{d}v)\rightarrow L^{2}(aM\mathrm{d}v)\quad\mbox{is
compact}\,.

(2.9)

Combining the gain operator assumption

(
2.9
)
and the loss operator assumption
(
2.7
)
, we
conclude that

\frac{1}{a}\mathcal{L}:L^{p}(aM\mathrm{d}v)\rightarrow L^{p}(aM\mathrm{d}v)\quad\mbox{is Fredholm}

for every
p\in(1\,,\infty)
. From this Fredholm property we can define the
psuedo-inverse of
\mathcal{L}
, called
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
:

\mathcal{L}^{-1}:L^{p}(a^{1-p}M\mathrm{d}v)\cap\mbox{Null}^{\perp}({\mathcal{L}})\rightarrow L^{p}(aM\mathrm{d}v)\,.

Moreover,
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
is a bounded operator.

The
fifth assumption
regarding
b
is that for every
\delta>0
there exists
C_{\delta}
such that
\bar{b}
satisfies

\frac{\bar{b}(v_{1}-v)}{1+\delta\frac{\bar{b}(v_{1}-v)}{1+|v_{1}-v|^{2}}}\leq C_{\delta}(1+a(v_{1}))(1+a(v))\quad\mbox{for every}\quad\!v_{1},v\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\,.

(2.10)

It is well known that the null space of the linearized Boltzmann operator
{\mathcal{L}}
is given by Null
({\mathcal{L}})\equiv\mbox{span}\{1,v_{1}\,,\cdots\,,v_{{\mathrm{D}}}\,,|v|^{2}\}
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}(M\mathrm{d}v)
onto Null
({\mathcal{L}})
, namely,

{\mathcal{P}}\tilde{g}=\langle\tilde{g}\rangle+v\cdot\langle\tilde{g}\rangle+(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})\langle(\tfrac{1}{{\mathrm{D}}}|v|^{2}-1)\tilde{g}\rangle\,,

(2.11)

where the notation
\langle\cdot\rangle
is defined below in (
2.16
). Furthermore, we define
{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}=\mathcal{I}-{\mathcal{P}}
. The matrix-valued function

\mathrm{A}(v)
and the vector-valued function
\mathrm{B}(v)

are defined by

\mathrm{A}(v)=v\otimes v-\tfrac{1}{{\mathrm{D}}}|v|^{2}\mathrm{I}\,,\quad\mathrm{B}(v)=\tfrac{1}{2}|v|^{2}v-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2}v\,.

(2.12)

We also define a scalar-valued function
\mathrm{C}(v)
by

\mathrm{C}(v)=\tfrac{1}{4}|v|^{4}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2}|v|^{2}+\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}({\mathrm{D}}+2)}{4}\,.

(2.13)

It is easy to see that each entry of
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{B}
and
\mathrm{C}

are in
L^{2}(a^{-1}M\,\mathrm{d}v)\cap\mbox{Null}^{\perp}({\mathcal{L}})
. Furthermore,
\mathrm{C}
is perpendicular to each entry of
\mathrm{A}
and
\mathrm{B}
. We also introduce

\widehat{\mathrm{A}}\in L^{2}(aM\mathrm{d}v;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}\times{\mathrm{D}}})
and

\widehat{\mathrm{B}}\in L^{2}(aM\mathrm{d}v;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}})
by

\widehat{\mathrm{A}}={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathrm{A}\,,\quad\widehat{\mathrm{B}}={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathrm{B}\,.

(2.14)

Next, for the sake of simplicity, we take the following normalizations:

\displaystyle\iiint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}b(\omega\,,v_{1}-v)\,\mathrm{d}\omega M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v=1\,,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega=1\,,\quad\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}M\mathrm{d}v=1\,,\quad\int\limits_{\Omega}\,\mathrm{d}x=1\,,

associated with the domains
\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
,
\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}
,
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
and
\Omega
respectively, and

\iint\limits_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}F^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}v=1\,,

(2.15)

associated with the initial data
F^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}
.

Because
M\mathrm{d}v
is a positive unit measure on
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
, we denote by
\langle\xi\rangle
the average over
this measure of any integrable function
\xi=\xi(v)
,

\langle\xi\rangle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\xi(v)M\mathrm{d}v\,,

(2.16)

and the inner product on
L^{2}(M\mathrm{d}v)

\langle\xi\,,\eta\rangle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\xi(v)\overline{\eta(v)}M\mathrm{d}v\,,

where
\overline{\eta}
denotes the complex conjugate of
\eta
. Moreover, we also use the following average on the boundary

\langle\xi\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}}=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\xi(v)\left[\mathrm{n}(x)\!\cdot\!v\right]\sqrt{2\pi}M\mathrm{d}v\,,

(2.17)

from which we have

\langle\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}}=-\langle\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{-}}\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}}=1\,.

Because
\mathrm{d}\mu=b(\omega\,,v_{1}-v)\,\mathrm{d}\omega M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}M\,\mathrm{d}v
is a positive unit measure on

\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
, we denote by

\langle\!\langle\Xi\rangle\!\rangle
the average over this measure
of any integrable function
\Xi=\Xi(\omega\,,v_{1}\,,v)

\langle\!\langle\Xi\rangle\!\rangle=\iiint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\Xi(\omega\,,v_{1}\,,v)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,.

The measure
\mathrm{d}\mu
is invariant under the coordinate transformations

(\omega\,,v_{1}\,,v)\mapsto(\omega\,,v\,,v_{1})\,,\quad(\omega\,,v_{1}\,,v)\mapsto(\omega\,,v^{\prime}_{1}\,,v^{\prime})\,.

These are called
d\mu\mbox{-}symmetries
.

2.3.
Navier-Stokes Scaling

The incompressible NSF system can be formally derived from the
Boltzmann equation through a scaling in which the fluctuations of
the kinetic densities
F_{\varepsilon}
about the absolute Maxwellian M are
scaled to be of order
\varepsilon
. More precisely, we take

F_{\varepsilon}=MG_{\varepsilon}=M(1+\varepsilon g_{\varepsilon})\,.

(2.18)

Rewriting equation
(
2.3
)
for
G_{\varepsilon}
yields

\displaystyle\varepsilon{\partial}_{t}{G_{\epsilon}}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}{G_{\epsilon}}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{Q}({G_{\epsilon}}\,,{G_{\epsilon}})

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathcal{O}\,,

(2.19)

\displaystyle{G_{\epsilon}}(0\,,x\,,v)={G_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}}(x\,,v)

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathcal{O}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}{G_{\epsilon}}=(1-\alpha)L\gamma_{+}{G_{\epsilon}}+\alpha\langle\gamma_{\!+}G_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\!{\partial\Omega}}

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\Sigma_{-}\,,

where the collision kernel
\mathcal{Q}
is now given by

\mathcal{Q}(G\,,G)=\iint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}(G^{\prime}_{1}G^{\prime}-G_{1}G)b(\omega\,,v_{1}-v)\mathrm{d}\omega M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,.

In terms of
g_{\varepsilon}
the system
(
2.3
)
finally reads

\displaystyle\varepsilon{\partial}_{t}g_{\varepsilon}+v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}g_{\varepsilon}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{L}g_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{Q}(g_{\varepsilon}\,,g_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathcal{O}\,,

(2.20)

\displaystyle g_{\varepsilon}(0\,,x\,,v)=g_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(x\,,v)

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathcal{O}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}g_{\varepsilon}=(1-\alpha)L\gamma_{+}g_{\varepsilon}+\alpha\langle\gamma_{+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\Sigma_{-}\,.

2.4.
A Priori
Estimates

Due to the presence of the boundary, the classical
a priori

estimates for the Boltzmann equation, namely the entropy and energy
bounds, are modified. First, because all particles arriving at the
boundary are reflected or diffused, we have conservation of mass,
which can be written as

\int_{\Omega}\langle{G_{\epsilon}}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\langle{G_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x=1\,.

Multiplying the equation (
2.19
) by
\log({G_{\epsilon}})
and integrating in
x
and
v
, we get formally

\displaystyle\varepsilon{\partial}_{t}\int_{\Omega}\langle{G_{\epsilon}}\log({G_{\epsilon}})-{G_{\epsilon}}+1\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x+\iint_{\Sigma}({G_{\epsilon}}\log({G_{\epsilon}})-{G_{\epsilon}}+1)v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}M\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\log({G_{\epsilon}})\mathcal{Q}({G_{\epsilon}}\,,{G_{\epsilon}})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

By denoting
h(z)=(1+z)\log(1+z)-z
, for
z>-1
and using that it is a convex function, we can compute the boundary term in the following way:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\varepsilon}(\gamma_{\!+}{G_{\epsilon}})=

\displaystyle\iint_{\Sigma}[{G_{\epsilon}}\log({G_{\epsilon}})-{G_{\epsilon}}+1]v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}M\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}\left[h(\varepsilon\gamma_{\!+}g_{\epsilon})-h\left((1-\alpha_{\epsilon})\varepsilon\gamma_{\!+}g_{\epsilon}+\alpha_{\epsilon}\langle\varepsilon\gamma_{\!+}g_{\epsilon}\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}}\right)\right]v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)\,M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}\left[h(\varepsilon\gamma_{\!+}g_{\epsilon})-(1-\alpha_{\epsilon})h(\varepsilon\gamma_{\!+}g_{\epsilon})-\alpha_{\epsilon}h(\langle\varepsilon\gamma_{\!+}g_{\epsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega})\right]v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)\,M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathcal{E}(\gamma_{\!+}{G_{\epsilon}})\,,

where
\mathcal{E}(\gamma_{\!+}{G_{\epsilon}})
, the so-called Darrozès-Guiraud information, is given by

\mathcal{E}(\gamma_{\!+}{G_{\epsilon}})=\int_{\partial\Omega}\left[\langle h(\varepsilon\gamma_{\!+}g_{\epsilon})\rangle_{\partial\Omega}-h(\varepsilon\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\epsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega})\right]\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,.

Jensen’s inequality implies that
\mathcal{E}(\gamma_{\!+}{G_{\epsilon}})\geq 0
.
Noticing that
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\varepsilon}(\gamma_{\!+}{G_{\epsilon}})\geq\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathcal{E}(\gamma_{\!+}{G_{\epsilon}})
, we get the entropy inequality

H({G_{\epsilon}}(t))+\int^{t}_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}R({G_{\epsilon}}(s))+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\varepsilon}(\gamma_{\!+}{G_{\epsilon}}(s))\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq H({G_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}})\,,

(2.21)

where
H(G)
is the relative entropy functional

H(G)=\int_{\Omega}\langle G\log(G)-G+1\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,,

and
R(G)
is the entropy dissipation rate functional

R(G)=\int_{\Omega}\left\langle\!\!\!\left\langle\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\frac{G^{\prime}_{1}G^{\prime}}{G_{1}G}\right)(G^{\prime}_{1}G^{\prime}-G_{1}G)\right\rangle\!\!\!\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

2.5.
DiPerna-Lions-(Mischler) Solutions

We will work in the setting of renormalized solutions which were initially constructed by DiPerna and Lions

[
12
]
over the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
for any initial data
satisfying natural physical bounds. Recently, their result was extended
to the case of a bounded domain by Mischler
[
35
,
36
,
37
]
with general Maxwell boundary conditions

(
2.1
)
.

The DiPerna-Lions-(Mishler) theory does not yield solutions that are known to
solve the Boltzmann equation in the usual weak sense. Rather, it
gives the existence of a global weak solution to a class of formally
equivalent initial value problems that are obtained by multiplying

(
2.19
)
by
\Gamma^{\prime}(G_{\varepsilon})
:

\displaystyle(\varepsilon{\partial}_{t}+v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x})\Gamma({G_{\epsilon}})=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})\mathcal{Q}({G_{\epsilon}}\,,{G_{\epsilon}})

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathcal{O}\,,

(2.22)

\displaystyle{G_{\epsilon}}(0\,,\cdot\,,\cdot)={G_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\mbox{on}\quad\mathcal{O}\,.

Here the admissible function
\Gamma:[0\,,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is continuously differentiable and for some constant
C_{\Gamma}<\infty

its derivative satisfies

|\Gamma^{\prime}(z)|\sqrt{1+z}\leq C_{\Gamma}\,.

(2.23)

The weak formulation of the renormalized Boltzmann equation (
2.22
) is given by

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\Gamma({G_{\epsilon}}(t_{2}))Y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x-\varepsilon\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\Gamma({G_{\epsilon}}(t_{1}))Y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x

(2.24)

\displaystyle-\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\Gamma({G_{\epsilon}})v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}Y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int\limits_{\partial\Omega}\langle\Gamma(\gamma{G_{\epsilon}})Y[\mathrm{n}(x)\cdot v]\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})\mathcal{Q}({G_{\epsilon}}\,,{G_{\epsilon}})Y\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,,

for every
Y\in C^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\bar{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}})
and every

[t_{1}\,,t_{2}]\subset[0\,,\infty]
. Moreover, the boundary condition is also understood in the renormalized sense:

\Gamma(\gamma_{\!-}{G_{\epsilon}})=\Gamma\left((1-\alpha)L\gamma_{\!+}{G_{\epsilon}}+\alpha\widetilde{{F_{\epsilon}}}\right)\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\Sigma_{-}\,,

(2.25)

where the equality holds almost everywhere and in the sense of distribution.

Proposition 2.1
.

(Renormalized solutions in bounded domain
[
37
]
) Let
b
satisfy the
condition
(
2.4
)
. Given any initial data
{G_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}}
satisfying

\iint\limits_{\mathcal{O}}G_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(1+|v|^{2}+|\log G_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}|)\,M\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}x<+\infty\,,

(2.26)

there exists at least one

{G_{\epsilon}}\geq 0
in
C([0\,,\infty);\,L^{1}(\,M\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}x))
such that

(
2.24
)
and
(
2.25
)
hold for all admissible
functions
\Gamma
. Moreover,
{G_{\epsilon}}
satisfies the following global
entropy inequality for all
t>0
:

H({G_{\epsilon}}(t))+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int^{t}_{0}R({G_{\epsilon}}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{t}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\varepsilon}(\gamma_{+}{G_{\epsilon}}(s))\leq H({G_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}})\,.

(2.27)

3.
Statement of the Main Results

In this section we state our main results on justifying the incompressible NSF limits with different boundary conditions depending on the quotient between the accommodation coefficients
\alpha_{\varepsilon}
and the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
.

3.1.
Dirichlet Boundary Condition

The main theorem of this paper is the following strong convergence
to the NSF system with Dirichlet boundary
condition when the accommodation coefficient
\alpha_{\epsilon}
is much larger
than the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
, i.e.
\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\infty

as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Theorem 3.1
.

(Dirichlet Boundary Condition) Let
b
be a collision kernel
that satisfies conditions (
2.5
), (
2.6
),
(
2.7
), (
2.9
) and (
2.10
). Let
G^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}
be
any family of non-negative measurable functions of
(x,v)
satisfying (
2.26
) and the renormalization
(
2.15
). Let
g^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}
be the associated
family of fluctuations given by
G^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon g^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}
. Assume that the families
G^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}
and
g^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}

satisfy

H(G^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon})\leq C^{\mathrm{in}}\varepsilon^{2}\,,

(3.1)

and

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\left(\langle g^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,,\langle vg^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,,\langle(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}}-1)g^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\right)=(\rho^{\mathrm{in}},\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{in}},\theta^{\mathrm{in}})\,,

(3.2)

in the sense of distributions for some
(\rho^{\mathrm{in}},\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{in}},\theta^{\mathrm{in}})\in L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x\,;\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}}\times\mathbb{R})
. Let
G_{\varepsilon}
be any family of DiPerna-Lions renormalized solutions to the
Boltzmann equation (
2.19
) that have
G^{\mathrm{in}}_{\varepsilon}
as initial values, and the accommodation
coefficient
\alpha_{\epsilon}
satisfies

\alpha_{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{2\pi}\chi\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,.

(3.3)

Then the family of fluctuations
g_{\varepsilon}
given by (
2.18
) is relatively compact in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))\,.
Every limit point
g
of
g_{\varepsilon}
has
the infinitesimal Maxwellian form

g=v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}+\left(\tfrac{1}{2}|v|^{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2}\right)\theta\,,

(3.4)

where
(\mathrm{u},\theta)\in C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x\,;\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}}\times\mathbb{R}))\cap L^{2}(\mathrm{d}t\,;H^{1}(\mathrm{d}x\,;\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}}\times\mathbb{R}))
with mean zero over
\Omega
,
and it satisfies the NSF system with Dirichlet
boundary condition (
1.1
), (
1.2
), and
(
1.3
), where kinematic viscosity
\nu
and thermal
conductivity
\kappa
are given by

\nu=\tfrac{1}{({\mathrm{D}}-1)({\mathrm{D}}+2)}\langle{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}\!:\!{\mathcal{L}}{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}\rangle\,,\quad\kappa=\tfrac{1}{{\mathrm{D}}}\langle{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}\!\cdot\!{\mathcal{L}}{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}\rangle\,.

(3.5)

The initial data is given by

\mathrm{u}^{0}=\mathbb{P}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{in}}\,,\quad\theta^{0}=\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\theta^{\mathrm{in}}-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\rho^{\mathrm{in}}\,.

(3.6)

Here the operator
\mathbb{P}
is the Leray’s projection on the space of divergence free vector fields. Moreover, every subsequence
g_{\varepsilon_{k}}
of
g_{\varepsilon}
that converges to
g
as
\varepsilon_{k}\rightarrow 0
also satisfies

\displaystyle\langle vg_{\varepsilon_{k}}\rangle\rightarrow\mathrm{u}

\displaystyle\hbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{p}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}))\,,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\langle(\tfrac{1}{{\mathrm{D}}}|v|^{2}-1)g_{\varepsilon_{k}}\rangle\rightarrow\theta

\displaystyle\hbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{p}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x;\mathbb{R}))\quad\mbox{for every}\quad 1\leq p<\infty\,.

Furthermore,
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}g_{\varepsilon}
is
relatively compact in
w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
. For every subsequence
\varepsilon_{k}
so that

g_{\varepsilon_{k}}
converges to
g
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}g_{\varepsilon_{k}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{A}:\mathrm{u}\otimes\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{B}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}\theta+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{C}\theta^{2}

(3.8)

\displaystyle-\widehat{\mathrm{A}}:\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}-\widehat{\mathrm{B}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\theta\,,\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))\,,

as
\varepsilon_{k}\rightarrow 0
, where
\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C}
and
{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}},{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}
are defined in (
2.12
), (
2.13
) and (
2.14
).

Remark:
In the formal Chapman-Enskog expansion,

g_{\varepsilon}=g+\varepsilon\mathcal{P}^{\perp}g_{1}+\varepsilon\mathcal{P}g_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}g_{2}+\cdots\,,

where
g
is given by (
3.4
) and
\mathcal{P}^{\perp}g_{1}
is the righthand side term in (
3.8
). In previous works
[
19
,
20
,
27
]
, under the assumptions (
3.1
) and
(
3.2
), the convergence to (
3.4
) and
(
3.7
) are only in
w\mbox{-}L^{1}
. So
the convergence to the quadratic term (
3.8
), which is the
first correction to the infinitesimal Maxwellian that one obtains
from the Chapman-Enskog expansion with the Navier-Stokes scaling,
could not be obtained. In Theorem
3.1
, by showing the
acoustic waves are instantaneously damped, we justify not only the
strong convergence to the leading order term
g
, but also weak
convergence to the kinetic part of the next order corrector (
3.8
).

3.2.
Navier Boundary Condition

The second result is about Navier boundary condition. For this case, although the coupled viscous boundary layer and the
Knudsen layer still have dissipative effect, however, the damping
happens a longer time scale
O(1)
. Consequently, unlike the Dirichlet
boundary condition case, the fast acoustic waves can be damped, but

not instantaneously
. Nevertheless, we can show the weak
convergence result, thus justify the NSF limit with slip Navier boundary
condition, while the linear Stokes-Fourier limit was justified in
[
33
]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

(Navier Boundary Condition) With the same assumptions with Theorem
3.1
, except that the accommodation coefficients satisfy

\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon}\rightarrow\chi\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\,.

(3.9)

Then the family
g_{\varepsilon}
is relatively compact in

w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))\,.

Every limit point
g
of
g_{\varepsilon}
in
w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
has the infinitesimal
Maxwellian form as (
3.4
) in which
(\mathrm{u},\theta)\in C([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x\,;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}))\cap L^{2}(\mathrm{d}t;H^{1}(\mathrm{d}x\,;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}))

is a Larey solution of the
NSF system with Navier boundary condition
(
1.1
), (
1.2
), and
(
1.4
), where kinematic viscosity
\nu
and thermal
conductivity
\kappa
are given by (
3.5
), the initial
data is given by (
3.6
).

Moreover, every subsequence
g_{\varepsilon_{k}}
of
g_{\varepsilon}
that converges to
g
as
\varepsilon_{k}\rightarrow 0
also satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\langle vg_{\varepsilon_{k}}\rangle\rightarrow\mathrm{u}

\displaystyle\hbox{in}\!\!\quad C([0,\infty)\,;\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega\,;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}))\,,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\langle(\tfrac{1}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}|v|^{2}-1)g_{\varepsilon_{k}}\rangle\rightarrow\theta

\displaystyle\hbox{in}\!\!\quad C([0,\infty)\,;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\Omega\,;\mathbb{R}))\,.

Remark:
For the Navier-slip boundary condition case, since the convergence is weak, the convergence (
3.8
), i.e. the justification of the first correction to the infinitesimal Maxwellian in the Chapman-Enskog expansion can not be obtained.

4.
Geometry of the boundary
{\partial\Omega}

In this section, we collect some differential geometry properties
related to the boundary
{\partial\Omega}
which can be considered as a
({\mathrm{D}}-1)

dimension Riemannian manifold with a metric induced from the
standard Euclidian metric of
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
. From the following classical result in geometry (for the proof, see
[
42
]
), there is a tubular neighborhood
\Omega_{\delta}=\{x\in\Omega:\mbox{dist}(x,{\partial\Omega})<\delta\}
of
{\partial\Omega}
such that the nearest point
projection map is well defined.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
{\partial\Omega}
is a compact
C^{k}
submanifold of dimension
{\mathrm{D}}-1
embedded
in
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
, then there is
\delta=\delta_{{\partial\Omega}}>0
and a map
\pi\in C^{k-1}(\Omega^{\delta}\,;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}})

such that the following properties hold:

(i)
: for all
x\in\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
with

\mathrm{dist}(x\,,{\partial\Omega})<\delta\,;

\pi(x)\in{\partial\Omega}\,,\quad x-\pi(x)\in T^{\perp}_{\Pi(x)}({\partial\Omega})\,,\quad|x-\pi(x)|=\mathrm{dist}(x\,,{\partial\Omega})\,,and

|z-x|>\mathrm{dist}(x\,,{\partial\Omega})\quad\mbox{for any}\quad\!z\in{\partial\Omega}\setminus\{\pi(x)\}\,;

(ii)
:

\pi(x+z)\equiv x\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\!x\in{\partial\Omega}\,,z\in T_{x}({\partial\Omega})^{\perp}\,,|z|<\delta\,,

(iii)
: Let
\mathrm{Hess}\Pi^{x}
denote the Hessian of
\pi
at

x
, then

\mathrm{Hess}\pi^{x}(V_{1}\,,V_{2})=\mathrm{h}_{x}(V_{1}\,,V_{2})\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\!x\in{\partial\Omega}\quad\!V_{1}\,,V_{2}\in T_{x}({\partial\Omega})\,,

where
\mathrm{h}_{x}
is the second fundamental form of
{\partial\Omega}
at
x
.

The viscous boundary layer has significantly different behavior over the tangential and normal directions near the boundary. This inspire us to consider the following new coordinate system, which we call the curvilinear coordinate for the tubular neighborhood
\Omega^{\delta}
defined in Lemma
4.1
. Because
{\partial\Omega}
is a
({\mathrm{D}}-1)
dimensional manifold, so locally

\pi(x)
can be represented as

\pi(x)=(\pi^{1}(x)\,,\cdots\,,\pi^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}(x))\,.

(4.1)

More precisely, the representation (
4.1
) could be understood in the following sense: we can introduce a new coordinate system
(\xi^{1}\,,\cdots\,,\xi^{\mathrm{D}})
by
a homeomorphism which locally defined as
\xi:\xi(x)=(\xi^{\prime}(x)\,,\xi^{\mathrm{D}}(x))
where
\xi^{\prime}=(\xi^{1}\,,\cdots\,,\xi^{{\mathrm{D}}-1})
, such that
\xi(\pi(x))=(\xi^{\prime},\,0)
and
\mathrm{d}(x)=\xi^{\mathrm{D}}
, where
\mathrm{d}(x)
is the distance function to
the boundary
\partial\Omega
, i.e.

\mathrm{d}(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x\,,{\partial\Omega})=|x-\pi(x)|\,.

(4.2)

For the simplicity of notation, we denote
``\xi^{\prime}(x)=\pi(x)"
which is the meaning of (
4.1
).

It is easy to see that
\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
is perpendicular to the
level surface of the distance function
\mathrm{d}
, i.e. the set

S^{z}=\{x\in\Omega:\mathrm{d}(x)=z\}
. In particular, on the
boundary,
\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
is perpendicular to
S_{0}={\partial\Omega}
.
Without loss of generality, we can normalize the distance
function so that
\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}(x)=-\mathrm{n}(x)
when
x\in{\partial\Omega}
. By the definition of the projection
\Pi
, we have

\pi(x+t\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}(x))=\pi(x)\quad\mbox{for}\quad\!\!\!t\quad\!\!\!\mbox{small}\,,

(4.3)

and consequently,
\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}=0
, for

\alpha=1\,,\cdots\,,{\mathrm{D}}-1
. In particular, for
t
small enough,

\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}(x)\in T_{x}({\partial\Omega})
when
x\in{\partial\Omega}
.

Next, we calculate the induced Riemannian metric from
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
on

{\partial\Omega}
. In a local coordinate system, these Riemannian metric can be
represented as

g=g_{\alpha\beta}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\alpha}\otimes\mathrm{d}\pi^{\beta}\,,

where
g_{\alpha\beta}=\langle\tfrac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\pi^{\alpha}}}\,,\tfrac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\pi^{\beta}}}\rangle
. Noticing that
\tfrac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{x^{i}}}=\tfrac{{\partial}\pi^{\alpha}}{{\partial}{x^{i}}}\tfrac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\pi^{\alpha}}}
, and

\langle\tfrac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{x^{i}}},\tfrac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{x^{j}}}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
, the
metric
g_{\alpha\beta}
can be determined by

g_{\alpha\beta}\tfrac{{\partial}\pi^{\alpha}}{{\partial}{x^{i}}}\tfrac{{\partial}\pi^{\beta}}{{\partial}{x^{i}}}=1\,.

5.
Acoustic Modes

5.1.
Acoustic Operator
{\mathcal{A}}

Recall that the Leray’s projection
{\mathbb{P}}
on the space of
divergence-free vector fields and
\mathbb{Q}
on the space of
gradients are defined by

{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{I}}-\mathbb{Q}\,,

where
\mathbb{Q}\mathrm{u}=\nabla_{\!x}q
and
q
solves

\displaystyle\Delta_{\!x}q

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\!\!x}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega\,,

(5.1)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!x}q\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{u}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\partial\Omega\,,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{\Omega}q\,\mathrm{d}x=0\,.

We define Hilbert spaces

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}

\displaystyle=\left\{U=(\rho\,,\mathrm{u}\,,\theta)\in L^{2}(\mathrm{d}{x};\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{\mathrm{D}}\times\mathbb{C})\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}

\displaystyle=\left\{U\in\mathbb{H}:\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\!x}U|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\right\}\,,

endowed with inner product

\langle U_{1}\,,U_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\int_{\Omega}(\rho_{1}\overline{\rho}_{2}+\mathrm{u}_{1}\cdot\overline{\mathrm{u}}_{2}+\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}\theta_{1}\overline{\theta}_{2})\,\mathrm{d}x\,,

(5.2)

where
\overline{f}
denotes the complex conjugate of the complex-valued function
f
. Next, we define the acoustic operator
{\mathcal{A}}
:

{\mathcal{A}}\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
\mathrm{u}\\
\theta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\!\!x}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}\\
\nabla_{x}(\rho+\theta)\\
\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\nabla_{\!\!x}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(5.3)

over the domain

\mathrm{Dom}({\mathcal{A}})=\left\{U=(\rho\,,\mathrm{u}\,,\theta)\in\mathbb{V}:\mathrm{u}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=0\ \hbox{on}\ \partial\Omega\right\}\,.

The null space of
\mathcal{A}
and its orthogonal with respect to
the inner product (
5.2
) are characterized as

\mbox{Null}(\mathcal{A})=\{(-\varphi\,,\mathrm{w}\,,\varphi)\in\mathbb{V}:\nabla_{\!\!x}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{w}=0\quad\!\mbox{and}\quad\!\mathrm{w}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=0\quad\!\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\}\,,

(5.4)

and

\mbox{Null}(\mathcal{A})^{\perp}=\{(\rho\,,\mathrm{u}\,,\theta)\in\mathbb{V}:\theta=\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\rho\,,\mathrm{u}=\nabla_{\!x}\phi\,,\quad\!\!\mbox{for
some}\quad\!\!\phi\in H^{1}{(\Omega)}\}\,,

(5.5)

respectively. Because Null
({\mathcal{A}})
includes the incompressibility and Boussinesq relations,
we call it
incompressible
regime. We will see in the next subsection that Null
({\mathcal{A}})^{\perp}
is spanned by the eigenspaces of the acoustic operator
{\mathcal{A}}
, so we call it
acoustic
regime.

For any
U=(\rho\,,\mathrm{u}\,,\theta)\in\mathbb{H}
, we can
define
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\perp}
the projections to the incompressible
regime
\mbox{Null}(\mathcal{A})
and acoustic regime

\mbox{Null}(\mathcal{A})^{\perp}
respectively as follows:

\displaystyle\Pi U

\displaystyle=\left(\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\rho-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\theta\,,\mathbb{P}\mathrm{u}\,,\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\theta-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\rho\right)\,,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\perp}U

\displaystyle=\left(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}(\rho+\theta)\,,\mathbb{Q}\mathrm{u}\,,\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}(\rho+\theta)\right)\,.

5.2.
Eigenspaces of
{\mathcal{A}}

The eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the acoustic operator
{\mathcal{A}}
in a
bounded domain can be constructed from those of the Laplace operator
with Neumann boundary condition in the following way: Let
\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}[\lambda^{k}]^{2}\,,\lambda^{k}>0\,,k\in\mathbb{N}
be the nondecreasing sequence of eigenvalues of the Laplace operator
-\Delta_{\mathrm{N}}
with
homogeneous Neumann boundary condition, and

\Psi^{k}
be the corresponding orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
eigenfunctions:

-\Delta_{\!x}\Psi^{k}=\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}[\lambda^{k}]^{2}\Psi^{k}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega,\quad\nabla_{\!x}\Psi^{k}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=0\,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\partial\Omega\,.

(5.6)

More specifically,

0<\lambda^{1}\leq\lambda^{2}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda^{k}\rightarrow+\infty\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\!k\rightarrow\infty\,.

Let
\tau
denote either
+
or
-
, and
\lambda^{\tau,k}=\tau\lambda^{k}
. It can be verified that
i\lambda^{\tau,k}
are non-zero eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{A}}
and

U^{\tau,k}=\sqrt{\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2{\mathrm{D}}}}\left(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\Psi^{k}\,,\tfrac{\nabla_{x}\Psi^{k}}{i\lambda^{\tau,k}}\,,\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\Psi^{k}\right)^{\top}

(5.7)

are the corresponding normalized eigenvectors, i.e.

{\mathcal{A}}U^{\tau,k}=i\lambda^{\tau,k}U^{\tau,k}\,,

(5.8)

and furthermore,
U^{\tau,k}
span Null
(\mathcal{A})^{\perp}
under the inner product (
5.2
). Consequently we have an orthonormal basis of
the acoustic modes, i.e.

\mbox{Null}(\mathcal{A})^{\perp}=\overline{\mbox{Span}\left\{U^{\tau,k}|k\in\mathbb{N}\,,\tau=\pm\right\}}^{L^{2}}\,.

Moreover, we can use the components of
U^{\tau,k}
to construct the infinitesimal Maxwellians
g^{\tau,k}
which are in the null space of
{\mathcal{L}}
:

g^{\tau,k}=\sqrt{\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2{\mathrm{D}}}}\left\{\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\Psi^{k}+v\!\cdot\tfrac{\nabla_{x}\Psi^{k}}{i\lambda^{\tau,k}}+\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\Psi^{k}(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})\right\}\,.

(5.9)

These infinitesimal Maxwellians will be the building blocks of the
approximate eigenfunctions of
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{L}}-v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}
.

5.3.
Conditions on
\Psi^{k}

Note that
\Psi^{k}
,
k\geq 1
are solutions to the Neumann boundary condition equation (
5.6
), so some
orthogonality condition
is required for the eigenfunctions associated to the eigenvalues with multiplicity greater
than 1. Assume that
\lambda^{2}
is an eigenvalue of (
5.6
) and denote by
\mathrm{H}_{0}=\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
the eigenspace associated to
\lambda^{2}
, i.e.

\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)=\{\Psi\in\mathrm{Dom}(-\Delta_{\!x}):-\Delta_{\!x}\Psi=\lambda^{2}\Psi\quad\!\!\mbox{in}\quad\!\!\Omega,\tfrac{{\partial}\Psi}{{\partial}\mathrm{n}}=0\quad\!\!\mbox{on}\quad\!\!{\partial\Omega}\}

(5.10)

where
\mathrm{Dom}(-\Delta_{\!x})=H^{2}(\Omega)\cap\{\Psi|\tfrac{{\partial}\Psi}{{\partial}\mathrm{n}}=0\quad\!\!\mbox{on}\quad\!\!{\partial\Omega}\}
denotes the domain of
-\Delta_{x}
with Neumann boundary condition. On the finite dimensional space
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
, we can define a quadratic form
Q_{1}
. Its associated bilinear form that we still denote
Q_{1}
and a symmetric operator
L_{1}=L^{\lambda}_{1}
by

Q_{1}(\Psi,\Phi)=\int_{\Omega}L_{1}(\Psi)\Phi\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}L_{1}(\Phi)\Psi\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

(5.11)

The eigenspace
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
is endows with an orthogonality condition

Q_{1}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l})=0\,,\quad\mbox{if}\quad\!\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)\quad\!\mbox{and}\quad\!k\neq l\,.

(5.12)

This condition means that the eigenvectors
\Psi^{k}
for
\lambda^{k}=\lambda
are orthogonal for the symmetric operator
L^{\lambda}_{1}
. Of course, since
L^{2}(\Omega)
is the direct sum of the spaces
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
for different
\lambda^{\prime}s
. From the definition of
L^{\lambda}_{1}
on each eigenspace
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
, we can define an operator
L_{1}
on
L^{2}(\Omega)
which leaves each eigenspace
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
invariant.
But this is not necessary, so we will think of
L_{1}=L^{\lambda}_{1}
as acting on
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
for a fixed multiple eigenvalue
\lambda
.

The orthogonality condition (
5.12
) turns out to be enough for the construction of the boundary layer if the eigenvalues of
L_{1}
are
simple, namely, if
\lambda^{k}_{1}\neq\lambda^{l}_{1}
for all
k\neq l
such that
\lambda^{k}_{0}=\lambda^{l}_{0}=\lambda
. However, if
\lambda_{1}
is an eigenvalue of
L_{1}
with multiplicity greater than or equal to
2
, then we need an extra orthogonality condition. Let
\mathrm{H}_{1}=\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})
be defined by

\mathrm{H}_{1}=\{\Psi\in\mathrm{H}_{0}:L_{1}\Psi=\lambda_{1}\Psi\}\,.

(5.13)

On the finite dimensional space
\mathrm{H}_{1}
, there exists a quadratic form
Q_{2}
and a symmetric operator
L_{2}
(see the definition below), the extra condition is

Q_{2}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l})=0\,,\quad\mbox{if}\quad\!\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda)\quad\!\mbox{and}\quad\!k\neq l\,.

(5.14)

This condition is enough if
L_{2}
has only simple eigenvalue on the vector space
H_{1}
. This process can be continued inductively.

Let us now explain more precisely the condition we have to impose on the eigenvectors of
-\Delta_{x}
. We can construct recursively, on each eigenspace
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
of
-\Delta_{x}
, a sequence of symmetric operators
L_{q},q\in\mathbb{N}
in the following way: Let
L_{0}=-\Delta_{x}
, we define
L_{1}
on each one of the eigenspace
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
of
L_{0}
by (
5.11
). Assume that the operators
L_{p}
were constructed for
p\leq q-1,q\geq 2
in such a way that each operator
L_{p}
leaves invariant the eigenspaces of the operators
L_{p^{\prime}}
for
p^{\prime}<p
. Now, to construct
L_{q}
, it is enough to construct
L_{q}
on each eigenspace
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathrm{H}_{2}(\lambda_{2})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{q-1}(\lambda_{q-1})
, where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{q-1}
are eigenvalues of
L_{1},L_{2},\cdots,L_{q-1}
respectively. This is done by constructing a quadratic form
Q_{q}
on each space
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathrm{H}_{2}(\lambda_{2})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{q-1}(\lambda_{q-1})
and defining
L_{q}
by

Q_{q}(\Psi,\Phi)=\int_{\Omega}L_{q}(\Psi)\Phi\,\mathrm{d}x\,,\quad\!\mbox{for all}\quad\!\Psi,\Phi\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathrm{H}_{2}(\lambda_{2})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{q-1}(\lambda_{q-1})\,.

The precise construction of the quadratic form
Q_{q}
on the space
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathrm{H}_{2}(\lambda_{2})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{q-1}(\lambda_{q-1})
will be done in the proof.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be an integer. This is the integer that will appear in the order of the approximation in the Proposition
7.1
. The eigenvectors
\Psi^{k}
for
\lambda^{k}_{0}=\lambda
should be chosen in such a way that they are eigenvectors for all the operators
L_{n}
at least for
n\leq N+2
. This implies that they are orthogonal to all the operators
L_{n}
for
n\leq N+2
, which means that

Q_{n}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l})=\int_{\Omega}L_{n}(\Psi^{k})\Psi^{l}=0\,,

(5.15)

if
\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathrm{H}_{2}(\lambda_{2})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{n-1}(\lambda_{n-1})
and
k\neq l
.

Remark:
The precise construction of the quadratic form
Q_{q}
will be done in the proof of Proposition
7.1
.

5.4.
The operator
{\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}

Later on, in the construction of the boundary layers, for each acoustic mode
k\geq 1
and

\tau=+
or
-
, we will frequently solve the following linear hyperbolic system for
V^{\tau,k}=(\rho^{\tau,k},\mathrm{v}^{\tau,k},\theta^{\tau,k})^{\top}
:

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k})V^{\tau,k}

\displaystyle=i\mu^{\tau,k}U^{\tau,k}+F^{\tau,k}\,,

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{v}^{\tau,k}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=g^{\tau,k}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\,.

where
\mu^{\tau,k}
,
F^{\tau,k}
and
g^{\tau,k}
are given, and
U^{\tau,k}
is defined in
(
5.7
).

Remark:
Strictly speaking, (
5.16
) is not
rigorous because
\mathrm{v}^{\tau,k}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}
is
non-zero, so
V^{\tau,k}
is not in the domain of
{\mathcal{A}}
. For
notational simplicity, we still use
{\mathcal{A}}
in (
5.16
) and
later on, just mean the expression of
{\mathcal{A}}
in (
5.3
)
regardless of the domain.

To solve the system (
5.16
), the main difficulty is that the kernel of
{\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}
is nontrivial. It will be more involved when the
eigenvalues have multiplicity greater than
1
. It can be characterized that the kernel and the orthogonal of
{\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}
with respect to the inner product (
5.2
) are

\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)=\mathrm{Span}\big\{U^{\tau,l}:\mbox{for all}\quad\!\!l\in\mathbb{N}\quad\!\!\mbox{such that}\quad\!\!\lambda^{l}=\lambda^{k}\big\}\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{\perp}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Span}\big\{U^{\delta,l}:\mbox{for all}\quad\!\!\delta=\pm\quad\!\!\mbox{and}\quad\!\!l\in\mathbb{N}\quad\!\!\mbox{such that}\quad\!\!\lambda^{l}\neq\lambda^{k}\big\}

\displaystyle\oplus\mathrm{Span}\big\{U^{-\tau,l}:\lambda^{l}=\lambda^{k}\big\}\oplus\mbox{Null}({\mathcal{A}})\,.

Next, we define a bounded pseudo inverse of
{\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}

\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{-1}:\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{\perp}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{\perp}\,,

by

\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{-1}U^{\delta,l}=\tfrac{1}{i\lambda^{\delta,l}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}}U^{\delta,l}\,,\quad\!\mbox{for any}\quad\!U^{\delta,l}\quad\!\mbox{with}\quad\!\lambda^{l}\neq\lambda^{k}\,,

(5.17)

\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{-1}U^{-\tau,l}=\tfrac{1}{-2i\lambda^{\tau,k}}U^{-\tau,l}\,,\quad\!\mbox{for
any}\quad\!U^{-\tau,l}\quad\!\mbox{with}\quad\!\lambda^{l}=\lambda^{k}\,,

(5.18)

and

\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{-1}(\rho,\mathrm{v},-\rho)^{T}=\tfrac{1}{i\lambda^{\tau,k}}(\rho,\mathrm{v},-\rho)^{T}\,,

(5.19)

for any
(\rho,\mathrm{v},-\rho)^{T}\in\mbox{Null}({\mathcal{A}})
and

\tau,\delta\in\{+,-\}
. It is obvious that this pseudo inverse operator
is a bounded operator. Consequently, the solutions to the system (
5.16
) are stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

For each fixed acoustic modes
k\geq 1
and
\tau\in\{+,-\}
, the solvability conditions of the system (
5.16
) are:

(i)
If
\lambda^{k}
is a simple eigenvalue of (
5.6
), then the only solvability condition is that
i\mu^{\tau,k}
must satisfy

i\mu^{\tau,k}=\int_{{\partial\Omega}}g^{\tau,k}\Psi^{k}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{\!x}-\langle F^{\tau,k}|U^{\tau,k}\rangle\,.

(5.20)

Under this condition, the solutions to (
5.16
)
V^{\tau,k}
can be solved uniquely as

V^{\tau,k}=V^{\tau,k}_{1}\,,

(5.21)

where
V^{\tau,k}_{1}\in\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{\perp}
.

(ii)
If
\lambda^{k}
is not a simple eigenvalue, then besides (
5.20
), further compatibility condition is needed:
F^{\tau,k}
must satisfy:

\int_{{\partial\Omega}}g^{\tau,k}\Psi^{l}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{\!x}=\langle F^{\tau,k}|U^{\tau,l}\rangle\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\!\lambda^{l}=\lambda^{k}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\!k\neq l\,.

(5.22)

For this case, under these two conditions (
5.20
)-(
5.22
), the solutions to (
5.16
)

V^{\tau,k}
can be determined modulo
\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)
. In other words,
V^{\tau,k}
can be uniquely
represented as

V^{\tau,k}=\sum\limits_{\lambda^{k}=\lambda^{l}}\langle V^{\tau,k}\,|\,U^{\tau,l}\rangle U^{\tau,l}+V^{\tau,k}_{1}\,,

(5.23)

where
V^{\tau,k}_{1}\in\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{\perp}
.

Proof.

For any
g^{\tau,k}\in H^{\frac{1}{2}}({\partial\Omega})
, there exists

\tilde{\mathrm{v}}^{\tau,k}\in H^{1}(\Omega\,;\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}})
, such that

\gamma\tilde{\mathrm{v}}^{\tau,k}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=g^{\tau,k}
, where
\gamma
is the usual trace operator from

H^{1}(\Omega\,;\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}})
to
H^{\frac{1}{2}}({\partial\Omega})
. We define

\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}=V^{\tau,k}-(0,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}^{\tau,k},0)^{T}\,.

(5.24)

Then
\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}
has zero the normal velocity on the
boundary
{\partial\Omega}
, thus is in the domain of
{\mathcal{A}}
. From
(
5.16
),
\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}
satisfies

({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k})\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}=-({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k})(0,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}^{\tau,k},0)+i\mu^{\tau,k}U^{\tau,k}+F^{\tau,k}\,.

(5.25)

The solvability of (
5.25
) is that the righthand side must be in

\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{\perp}
. Thus, the inner
product of (
5.25
) with
U^{\tau,k}
is zero, which gives (
5.20
), while the inner product
with
U^{\tau,l}
with
\lambda^{k}=\lambda^{l},k\neq l
gives
(
5.22
). Under these conditions, by applying the
psedu inverse operator
\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{-1}
defined in (
5.17
)-(
5.19
),
we can uniquely solve
\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}
in

\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{\perp}
, denoted by

\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}_{1}
. However, the projection of
\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}
on

\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)
is
not
determined. In other words,

\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}=\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}_{1}+\sum\limits_{\lambda^{k}=\lambda^{l}}\langle\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}\,|\,U^{\tau,l}\rangle U^{\tau,l}\,.

Using (
5.24
), we get (
5.23
),
where

V^{\tau,k}_{1}=\widetilde{V}^{\tau,k}_{1}+(0,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}^{\tau,k},0)^{T}-\sum\limits_{\lambda^{k}=\lambda^{l}}\langle(0,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}^{\tau,k},0)^{T}\,|\,U^{\tau,l}\rangle U^{\tau,l}\,.

In (
5.23
), the projection of
V^{\tau,k}
on

\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)
, i.e. the first
term in the righthand side of (
5.23
), can
not
be
determined. It is easy to see that the projection of
V^{\tau,k}
on

\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k}\big)^{\perp}
, i.e.
V^{\tau,k}_{1}
, is uniquely determined,
although the lifting of the trace
g^{\tau,k}
is not unique.

∎

6.
Analysis of the Kinetic Boundary Layer Equation

In this section, we collect three results in kinetic equations which
will be frequently used in this paper. The first two results are
standard in kinetic theory:

Lemma 6.1
.

The solvability condition for the linear kinetic equation
{\mathcal{L}}g=f

is

\langle f\,,\zeta(v)\rangle=0\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\zeta\in\mbox{Span}\{1\,,v\,,|v|^{2}\}\,.

(6.1)

The second result we will use is quoted from Lemma 4.4 in
[
3
]
.

Lemma 6.2
.

The components of
\langle\mathrm{A}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{A}}\rangle
and

\langle\mathrm{B}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{B}}\rangle
satisfy the following
identities:

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{A}_{ij}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{kl}\rangle

\displaystyle=\nu\left(\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{B}_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2}\kappa\delta_{ij}\,,

where
\nu
and
\kappa
are given by (
3.5
).

The next result is about the linear kinetic boundary layer equation which
will be used to determine the boundary conditions of the fluid
variables. We define the kinetic boundary layer operator
{\mathcal{L}}^{BL}
, reflection boundary operator
L^{\mathcal{R}}
and diffusive boundary operator
L^{\mathcal{D}}
acting on functions
\{g^{\mathrm{bb}}(x,v,\xi):(x,v,\xi)\in\Omega^{\delta}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
as follows:

{\mathcal{L}}^{BL}g^{\mathrm{bb}}:=-(v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}){\partial}_{\xi}g^{\mathrm{bb}}+{\mathcal{L}}g^{\mathrm{bb}}\,,

(6.2)

where
{\mathcal{L}}
is the linearized Boltzmann operator defined in (
2.8
).

L^{\mathcal{R}}g^{\mathrm{bb}}:=\gamma_{+}g^{\mathrm{bb}}-L\gamma_{-}g^{\mathrm{bb}}\,,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\!L^{\mathcal{D}}g^{\mathrm{bb}}:=\sqrt{2\pi}\chi\left[\langle\gamma_{-}g^{\mathrm{bb}}\rangle_{{\partial}\Omega}-L\gamma_{-}g^{\mathrm{bb}}\right]\,.

Lemma 6.3
.

Considering the following linear kinetic boundary layer equation of
g^{\mathrm{bb}}(x,v,\xi)
in
half space:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{BL}g^{\mathrm{bb}}

\displaystyle=S^{\mathrm{bb}}\,,\quad\mbox{in}\quad\xi>0\,,

(6.3)

\displaystyle g^{\mathrm{bb}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\xi\rightarrow\infty\,,

with boundary condition

L^{\mathcal{R}}g^{\mathrm{bb}}=H^{\mathrm{bb}}\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\xi=0\,,\quad v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0\,.

(6.4)

In the above equations, the boundary source term
H^{\mathrm{bb}}
is taken of the following form:

H^{\mathrm{bb}}=-L^{\mathcal{R}}g+L^{\mathcal{D}}f\,,

(6.5)

where
g
and
f
are of the forms:

\displaystyle g=

\displaystyle\rho_{g}+\mathrm{u}_{g}\cdot v+\theta_{g}\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}\right)

(6.6)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle({\partial}_{\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\otimes\!\mathrm{n}:\widehat{\mathrm{A}}+{\partial}_{\zeta}\theta^{\mathrm{b}}\mathrm{n}\!\cdot\!\widehat{\mathrm{B}})+({\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}\tilde{\mathrm{u}}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\otimes\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}:\widehat{\mathrm{A}}+{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}\tilde{\theta}^{\mathrm{b}}\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\!\cdot\!\widehat{\mathrm{B}})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}}:\widehat{\mathrm{A}}+\nabla_{\!x}\theta^{\mathrm{int}}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{B}})+S_{g}\,,

and

f=\rho_{f}+\mathrm{u}_{f}\cdot v+\theta_{f}\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}\right)+S_{f}\,,

(6.7)

and where
S_{g}\,,S_{f}\in\mbox{Null}({\mathcal{L}})^{\perp}
are source terms.

Then there exists a solution
g^{\mathrm{bb}}(x,v,\xi)
of the equation (
6.3
) if and only if the following boundary
conditions are satisfied by the fluid variables:

(i)
On the boundary
{\partial\Omega}
, the normal components of velocity is

\mathrm{u}_{g}\cdot\mathrm{n}=\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle S^{\mathrm{bb}}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

(6.8)

(ii)
On the boundary
{\partial\Omega}
, the tangential components of
velocities and temperature satisfy

\displaystyle\left[\mathrm{u}_{f}\right]^{\mathrm{tan}}=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\left[{\partial}_{\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\right]^{\mathrm{tan}}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\left[2d(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}})\cdot\mathrm{n}\right]^{\mathrm{tan}}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\nabla_{\!\pi}[\tilde{\mathrm{u}}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}]

(6.9)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left[\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{D}}S_{f})v(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})M\,\mathrm{d}v\right]^{\mathrm{tan}}-\tfrac{1}{\chi}\langle(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})vS_{g}\rangle^{\mathrm{tan}}+\tfrac{1}{\chi}\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle S^{\mathrm{bb}}v\rangle^{\mathrm{tan}}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,,

and

\displaystyle\theta_{f}=

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}{\partial}_{\zeta}\theta^{\mathrm{b}}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}{\partial}_{\mathrm{n}}\theta^{\mathrm{int}}+\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{2({\mathrm{D}}+1)}\mathrm{u}_{f}\cdot\mathrm{n}+\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{D}}S_{f})|v|^{2}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})M\,\mathrm{d}v

(6.10)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{({\mathrm{D}}+1)\chi}\langle(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})|v|^{2}S_{g}\rangle+\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{1}{\chi}\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle S^{\mathrm{bb}}(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,,

where kinematic viscosity
\nu
and thermal conductivity
\kappa

are given by (
3.5
),
\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{tan}}
denotes the
tangential components of the vector
\mathrm{u}
, and
\nabla_{\pi}
denotes the tangential derivative.

Proof.

The solvability conditions of the linear boundary layer equation
(
6.3
) with boundary condition (
6.4
) are
given by

\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}H^{\mathrm{bb}}\eta(v)(v\cdot\mathrm{n})M\,\mathrm{d}v=-\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle S^{\mathrm{bb}}\eta\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,,

(6.11)

for all
\eta(v)\in
Null
({\mathcal{L}})
satisfying the condition:

\eta(R_{x}v)=\eta(v)\,.

(6.12)

It is obvious that
1
and
|v|^{2}
satisfy (
6.12
). If
\eta(v)=\sum^{\mathrm{D}}_{1}a_{i}v_{i}
satisfies (
6.12
), then necessarily

(v\cdot\mathrm{n})\sum^{\mathrm{D}}_{i=1}a_{i}\mathrm{n}_{i}=0\,,

(6.13)

which implies that the vector
\mathrm{a}=(a_{1}\,,\cdots\,,a_{{\mathrm{D}}})^{\top}
is perpendicular to the outer normal vector
\mathrm{n}
.

The formula (
6.8
) can be derived by taking
\eta=1
in
(
6.11
). Simple calculations show that

\displaystyle\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}H^{\mathrm{bb}}(v)(v\cdot\mathrm{n})M\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=-\langle v\gamma g\rangle\cdot\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{u}_{g}\cdot\mathrm{n}-\langle vS_{g}\rangle\cdot\mathrm{n}\,.

Note that
S_{g}\in
Null
({\mathcal{L}})^{\perp}
, hence (
6.8
) follows.

To prove (
6.9
), by taking
\eta=\sum^{\mathrm{D}}_{1}a_{i}v_{i}
in
(
6.11
), we have

\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}H^{\mathrm{bb}}(a_{i}v_{i})(\mathrm{n}_{j}v_{j})M\,\mathrm{d}v=-\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle(a_{i}v_{i})S^{\mathrm{bb}}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

In other words,

\displaystyle-\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{R}}g)(a_{i}v_{i})(\mathrm{n}_{j}v_{j})M\,\mathrm{d}v+\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{D}}f)(a_{i}v_{i})(\mathrm{n}_{j}v_{j})M\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle S^{\mathrm{bb}}(a_{i}v_{i})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

Simple calculations yield that

\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{R}}g)(a_{i}v_{i})(\mathrm{n}_{j}v_{j})M\,\mathrm{d}v=\langle v_{i}v_{j}\gamma g\rangle a_{i}\mathrm{n}_{j}=\langle\mathrm{A}_{ij}\gamma g\rangle a_{i}\mathrm{n}_{j}\,.

Using the definition of the viscosity
\nu
in (
3.5
) and
Lemma
6.2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{R}}g)(a_{i}v_{i})(\mathrm{n}_{j}v_{j})M\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\nu[{\partial}_{\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}]\cdot\mathrm{a}+\nu\left[(\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}}+(\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}})^{\top})\cdot\mathrm{n}\right]\cdot\mathrm{a}+\nu\nabla_{\pi}[\tilde{\mathrm{u}}\cdot\mathrm{n}]\cdot\mathrm{a}+\langle S_{g}\mathrm{A}\mathrm{n}\rangle\cdot\mathrm{a}\,.

Next, it can be calculated that

L^{\mathcal{D}}f=\big(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{2}\theta_{f}-\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{2}\mathrm{u}_{f}\cdot\mathrm{n}\big)-\mathrm{u}_{f}\!\cdot\!v+2(\mathrm{u}_{f}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})\mathrm{n}\!\cdot\!v-\tfrac{1}{2}\theta_{f}|v|^{2}\\
+L^{\mathcal{D}}S_{f}\,,

(6.14)

where
R_{x}\mathrm{u}_{f}=\mathrm{u}_{f}-2(\mathrm{u}_{f}\cdot\mathrm{n})\mathrm{n}
is the reflection of
\mathrm{u}_{f}
with
respect to the normal
\mathrm{n}(x)
. Thus

\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{D}}f)(a_{i}v_{i})(v\cdot\mathrm{n})M\,\mathrm{d}v=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}(R_{x}\mathrm{u}_{f})\cdot\mathrm{a}+\left[\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{D}}S_{f})\mathrm{A}\cdot\mathrm{n}M\,\mathrm{d}v\right]\cdot\mathrm{a}\,.

To prove (
6.10
), taking
\eta=|v|^{2}
in
(
6.11
), we have

\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}H^{\mathrm{bb}}|v|^{2}(v\cdot\mathrm{n})M\,\mathrm{d}v=-\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle|v|^{2}S^{\mathrm{bb}}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

It can be calculated that

\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{R}}g)(v\cdot\mathrm{n})|v|^{2}M\,\mathrm{d}v=2\langle\gamma g\mathrm{B}\rangle\cdot\mathrm{n}+({\mathrm{D}}+2)\langle(v\cdot\mathrm{n})\gamma g\rangle\,.

Using the definition of the thermal conductivity
\kappa
in
(
3.5
) and Lemma
6.2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}L^{\mathcal{R}}g(v\cdot\mathrm{n})|v|^{2}M\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-({\mathrm{D}}+2)\kappa{\partial}_{\zeta}\theta^{b}+({\mathrm{D}}+2)\kappa\nabla_{\!x}\theta^{\mathrm{int}}\cdot\mathrm{n}+({\mathrm{D}}+2)\mathrm{u}_{g}\cdot\mathrm{n}+\langle(v\cdot\mathrm{n})|v|^{2}S_{g}\rangle\,.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{D}}f)(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})|v|^{2}M\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{u}_{f}\cdot\mathrm{n}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\theta_{f}+\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}(L^{\mathcal{D}}S_{f})(v\cdot\mathrm{n})|v|^{2}M\,\mathrm{d}v\,.

As showed in (
6.13
), the vector
\mathrm{a}
is
perpendicular to
\mathrm{n}
. Thus the resulting vector of inner
product with
\mathrm{a}
is the tangential part. Finally, noticing

[R_{x}\mathrm{u}_{f}]^{\mathrm{tan}}=[\mathrm{u}_{f}]^{\mathrm{tan}}
,
we then finish the proof of the Lemma
6.3
.
∎

7.
Approximate Eigenfunctions-Eigenvalues

7.1.
Motivation

We define the operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\varepsilon}
as

{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{L}}-v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{L}}+v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\,.

Formally,
{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\varepsilon}
are “dual” in the following sense:

\langle{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\varepsilon}g^{*}\,,g\rangle=\langle g^{*}\,,{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}g\rangle\,,

(7.1)

provided that
g^{*}
satisfies the Maxwell reflection boundary condition

\gamma_{-}g^{*}=(1-\alpha)L\gamma_{+}g^{*}+\alpha\langle\gamma_{+}g^{*}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\Sigma_{-}\,,

(7.2)

and
g
satisfies the dual boundary condition

\gamma_{+}g=(1-\alpha)L\gamma_{-}g+\alpha\langle\gamma_{-}g\rangle_{\partial\Omega}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\Sigma_{+}\,.

(7.3)

If
g_{\varepsilon}
is the fluctuation defined in (
2.18
), then
g_{\varepsilon}
obeys the scaled Boltzmann equation (
2.20
) in which
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\varepsilon}g_{\varepsilon}
appears and
g_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the boundary condition (
7.2
). Then from (
7.1
),
{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
appears in the weak formulation of the Boltzmann equation if we take
g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
as a test function. Thus, it is natural to construct eigenfunctions and eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}
satisfying the dual boundary condition (
7.3
). Specifically, we consider the kinetic eigenvalue problem:

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}=-i\lambda^{BL}_{\varepsilon}g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}\,,

(7.4)

with
g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
satisfying the dual Maxwell boundary condition (
7.3
),
where the accommodation coefficient
\alpha
takes the value
\alpha_{\epsilon}=\sqrt{2\pi}\chi\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. By doing so, formally the equation (
2.20
) becomes an ordinary differential equation of
b_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\Omega}\langle g_{\varepsilon}\,,g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x
:

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}b_{\varepsilon}+\tfrac{i\lambda^{BL}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}b_{\varepsilon}=c_{\varepsilon}\,.

To solve the eigenvalue problem (
7.4
) and (
7.3
), a key observation is that the solutions must include interior and two boundary layer terms: the
fluid viscous boundary layer
with thickness
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, and the
kinetic Knudsen layer
with thickness
\varepsilon
. We make the ansatz of
g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
and
\lambda^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
as

g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}=\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\left[g^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}(x,v)+g^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}(\pi(x),\tfrac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}},v)\right]\varepsilon^{\frac{m}{2}}+\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}g^{\mathrm{bb}}_{m}(\pi(x),\tfrac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\varepsilon},v)\varepsilon^{\frac{m}{2}}\,,

(7.5)
