and

\lambda^{BL}_{\varepsilon}=\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}\lambda_{m}\varepsilon^{\frac{m}{2}}\,.

(7.6)

Each
g^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}
and
g^{\mathrm{bb}}_{m}
are defined in
\Omega^{\delta}
, the

\delta\mbox{-}
tubular neighborhood of
{\partial\Omega}
in
\Omega
, where

\delta>0
is the small number defined in Lemma
4.1
,
the projection
\pi
is defined in (
4.1
). After rescaling by

\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon
respectively,

g^{\mathrm{b}}_{m},g^{\mathrm{bb}}_{m}:({\partial\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\,.

Both
g^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}
and
g^{\mathrm{bb}}_{m}
will vanish in the
outside of
\Omega^{\delta}
. Thus
g^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}
and
g^{\mathrm{bb}}_{m}

are required to be rapidly decreasing to 0 in the
\zeta
and
\xi

respectively, which are defined by
\zeta=\frac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

and
\xi=\frac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\varepsilon}
.

In the ansatz (
7.5
),
g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
consists three types of terms: the interior terms
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}
,
the fluid viscous boundary layer terms
g^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}
, and the kinetic Knudsen layer terms

g^{\mathrm{bb}}_{m}
. They are coupled through the boundary condition (
7.3
).

7.2.
Statement of the Proposition

Now we state the proposition which can be considered as a kinetic analogue of the
Proposition 2 in
[
10
]
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a
C^{2}
bounded domain of
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
and the accommodation coefficient
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{2\pi}\chi\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. Then, for every acoustic mode
k\geq 1
, non-negative integer
N
, and each
\tau\in\{+\,,-\}
, there exists approximate
eigenfunctions
g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}
and eigenvalues

-i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}
, and error terms

R^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}
and
r^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}
respectively,
such that

{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}=-i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}+R^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}\,,

(7.7)

and
g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}
satisfy the approximate dual Maxwell boundary condition:

L^{\mathcal{R}}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}=\sqrt{\varepsilon}L^{\mathcal{D}}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}+r^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!\Sigma_{+}\,.

(7.8)

Moreover, there exits complex numbers
\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1}
, such that
i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}
has the following expansions:

i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}=i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{0}+i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1}\sqrt{\varepsilon}+O(\varepsilon)\,,\quad with\quad\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})<0\,.

(7.9)

Furthermore, for all
1<r,p\leq\infty
, we have error estimates:

\|R^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}\|_{L^{r}(\mathrm{d}x,L^{p}(a^{1-p}M\mathrm{d}v))}=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{N-1})\,,

(7.10)

and

\|g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}-g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\|_{L^{r}(\mathrm{d}x,L^{p}(a^{1-p}M\mathrm{d}v))}=O(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2r}})\,.

(7.11)

where
g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
is defined in
(
5.9
). We also have the boundary error estimates:

\|r^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,N}\|_{L^{r}(\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x},L^{p}(a^{1-p}M\mathrm{d}v))}=O\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{N+1}\right)\,.

(7.12)

7.3.
Main Idea of the Proof

For each non-negative integer
m
,
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}
is decomposed as hydrodynamic part
{\mathcal{P}}g^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}
, i.e. the projection on Null
({\mathcal{L}})
, and kinetic part
{\mathcal{P}}^{\!\perp}g^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}
, the projection on Null
({\mathcal{L}})^{\!\perp}
. The hydrodynamic part is given by

{\mathcal{P}}g^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}=\rho^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}+v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}+\big(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}\big)\theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}=(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}\,,

where
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}=(\rho^{\mathrm{int}}_{m},\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}}_{m},\theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{m})^{\top}
is called the fluid variables of
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}
. It can be shown that the coefficients of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\!\perp}g^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}
are in terms of
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m^{\prime}}
and their derivatives for
m^{\prime}<m
. Thus, we need only to solve
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{n}
for all integers
n\leq m
to determine
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}
. Similar notations will be used for
g^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}
, and for the same reason we also need only to solve
U^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}
.

We put the ansatz into the equation (
7.4
), then collect the same order terms. The leading order term
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
is hydrodynamic, which means
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
is completely determined by
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
. we can derive that
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
satisfies the equation

{\mathcal{A}}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}=i\lambda_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,.

(7.13)

For (
7.13
) there are two cases:

•

Case 1
:
\lambda_{0}\neq 0
, by comparing the
equations (
7.13
) and (
5.8
),

i\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue of the acoustic operator

{\mathcal{A}}
, i.e.
\lambda_{0}=\lambda^{\tau,k}
and

U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}=U^{\tau,k}
, where
k\geq 0
is the acoustic modes, and
\tau
denotes either
+
or
-
. Starting from here, we can construct the boundary layer
g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
which we call the boundary layer in the
acoustic modes
.

•

Case 2
:
\lambda_{0}=0
,
which implies that
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}\in\mbox{Ker}({\mathcal{A}})
, i.e.
\rho^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}+\theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}=0
and
\nabla_{\!\!x}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}=0
. Starting from here, we can construct the boundary layer
g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
which we call the boundary layer in the
incompressible modes
.

Because the main goal of the current paper is about how the acoustic waves and the boundary layers interact in the incompressible Navier-Stokes limit of the Boltzmann equation, we only consider the
Case 1
,
the kinetic-fluid boundary layers in the acoustic modes for
each
k\geq 0
and each
\tau
. Consequently, we add superscript
\tau,k
for each term in the ansatz.
In the forthcoming paper
[
25
]
, we will investigate the higher order acoustic limit of the Boltzmann equation, where we need to analyze the boundary layers in incompressible modes, i.e.
Case 2
.

The basic strategy to solve all terms in the ansatz is the following: (for the simplicity of notation, we don’t write the upper index
\tau
)

1,

g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}
satisfies the linear kinetic boundary layer equation (
6.3
). Applying Lemma
6.3
, the solvability conditions for
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}
give the normal boundary condition
[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}+\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}]\cdot\mathrm{n}
and the tangential boundary condition
[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}]^{\mathrm{tan}}+[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1}]^{\mathrm{tan}}
and
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1}\,;

2,

U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}
satisfies the ODE system like (
8.96
), where the normal boundary acoustic operator
{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}}
is defined in (
8.1
). Solving
U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}
includes two steps: First, projecting the ODE system of
U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}
on Null
{}^{\perp}({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})
to get the first order ODE satisfied by
\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}
and
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
(thus only need one boundary condition at
\zeta=\infty
). The next, projecting on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})
, to solve
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi
and
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}
which satisfy second ODE. Thus besides the condition at
\zeta=\infty
, another boundary condition at
\zeta=0
is needed. This is given by the solvability condition of
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}
in
1
.

3,

U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}
and
i\lambda^{k}_{m}
can be solved by applying Lemma
5.1
, where only the boundary condition in the normal
direction
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}
is needed which can be known from
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}
since their summation
[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}+\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}]\cdot\mathrm{n}
is found in
(1)
, and
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}
is already known in
2
;

4,
If the multiplicity of the eigenvalue
\lambda^{k}_{0}
is greater than 1, applying Lemma
5.1
,
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}
can be only solved modulo
\mathrm{Ker}({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})
. The components of
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}
in
\mathrm{Ker}({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})
will be solved by applying Lemma (
5.1
) again in later rounds.

8.
Proof of Proposition
7.1
: Construction of Boundary Layers

In this section, we construct the kinetic-fluid boundary layers corresponding to the accommodation coefficient
\alpha_{\epsilon}=\sqrt{2\pi}\chi\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. As mentioned in the previous section, we make the ansatz for
g^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
and
\lambda^{BL}_{\varepsilon}
as in (
7.5
) and (
7.6
). Then we formally
plug the ansatz (
7.5
) into the equation (
7.4
) and then collect the terms with the same order of
\varepsilon
in the interior, the viscous boundary layer, and the Knudsen layer respectively. The following calculations will be frequently used:

\displaystyle v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}g^{\mathrm{b}}(\pi(x),\tfrac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle=(v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}){\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}g^{\mathrm{b}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}(v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}){\partial}_{\zeta}g^{\mathrm{b}}\,,

\displaystyle v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}g^{\mathrm{bb}}(\pi(x),\tfrac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=(v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}){\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}g^{\mathrm{bb}}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}){\partial}_{\xi}g^{\mathrm{bb}}\,.

8.1.
Normal Boundary Acoustic Operator

A key role played in the analysis of the viscous boundary layer is the so-called normal boundary acoustic operator
{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}}
of the viscous boundary fluid variables
U^{\mathrm{b}}=(\rho^{\mathrm{b}}\,,\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\,,\theta^{\mathrm{b}})^{\top}
:

\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{d}}U^{\mathrm{b}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\!\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})\\
\partial_{\!\zeta}(\rho^{\mathrm{b}}+\theta^{\mathrm{b}})\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\\
\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\partial_{\!\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})\end{pmatrix}\,.

(8.1)

The null space of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{b}}
and its orthogonal space are

\displaystyle\mathrm{Null}({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})=\{(\rho^{\mathrm{b}}\,,\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\,,\theta^{\mathrm{b}})^{\top}\in L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x\,;\Omega^{\delta}):\rho^{b}+\theta^{\mathrm{b}}=0\,,\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}=0\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})=\{(\rho^{\mathrm{b}}\,,\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\,,\theta^{\mathrm{b}})^{\top}\in L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x\,;\Omega^{\delta}):\theta^{\mathrm{b}}=\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\rho^{\mathrm{b}}\,,\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi=0\}\,,

where the orthogonality is with respect to the inner product endowed on
L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x\,;\Omega_{\delta})
:

\langle\widetilde{U}^{\mathrm{b}}\,,U^{\mathrm{b}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\delta})}:=\int_{U^{\delta}}(\tilde{\rho}^{\mathrm{b}}\overline{\rho^{b}}+\tilde{\mathrm{u}}^{\mathrm{b}}\cdot\overline{\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}}+\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}\tilde{\theta}^{\mathrm{b}}\overline{\theta^{b}})\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

The projections from
L^{2}(\Omega_{\delta})
to Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})
and Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})^{\perp}
are defined as

\displaystyle U^{\mathrm{b}}

\displaystyle=\Pi^{\mathrm{b}}U^{\mathrm{b}}+(\mathrm{I}-\Pi^{\mathrm{b}})U^{\mathrm{b}}

(8.2)

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\rho^{b}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\theta^{\mathrm{b}}\\
(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha})\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\\
\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\theta^{\mathrm{b}}-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\rho^{b}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}(\rho^{\mathrm{b}}+\theta^{\mathrm{b}})\\
(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\\
\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}(\rho^{\mathrm{b}}+\theta^{\mathrm{b}})\end{pmatrix}\,.

We remark that the normal boundary acoustic operator
{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}}
and its Null and Null orthogonal spaces appear in other places to play a key role. For example, the zero viscosity limit of compressible NSF equations with boundary, see
[
11
]
.

8.2.
Preparations

Before we start the induction, we solve
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
,
g^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}

and
i\lambda_{0}
. First, the terms of order
O(\varepsilon^{-1})
in the interior and viscous boundary layers
in the equation
(
7.4
)
yield

{\mathcal{L}}g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathcal{L}}g^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}=0\,,

which imply that
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
and

g^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}
are hydrodynamic, i.e.

g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}(x,v)=\rho^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}+v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}+\big(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}\big)\theta^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}=(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,

and

\displaystyle g^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}(\pi(x),\zeta,v)

\displaystyle=\rho^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}+v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}+\big(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}\big)\theta^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}=(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,.

We denote above expressions as
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}=I_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0})
and
g^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}=B_{0}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})
. Here as operators,
I_{0}=B_{0}
, we use different notations to emphasize that one is for interior variable, the other for viscous boundary variable. The fluid variables
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
and
U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}
are to be determined. To solve them we need to know the equations satisfied by them and their boundary conditions. It is easy to know from the order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-1})
of the interior part that
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}
is also hydrodynamic, i.e.
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}=I_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1})\,.

8.3.
Induction: Round 0

Now we start our induction arguments, each round includes considering the kinetic boundary layer, viscous boundary layer and interior terms alternately.

Step 1: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-2})
in the kinetic boundary layer.

The order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-2})
in the boundary condition

(
7.3
)
gives

L^{\mathcal{R}}\tilde{g}_{0}=0\,,

(8.3)

where we use the notation

\tilde{g}=g^{\mathrm{int}}+g^{\mathrm{b}}
.
Because
g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
and
g^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}
are
hydrodynamic, then

\big[\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}+\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\big]\cdot\mathrm{n}=0\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\,.

(8.4)

Step 2: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-1})
in the viscous boundary layer.

Next, the order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-1})
in the viscous boundary layer reads

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{L}}g^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}=v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!\!x}\mathrm{d}\partial_{\zeta}g^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}

(8.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\otimes\!\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}:\!\mathrm{A}+\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{B}+{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}}U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})\,.

Lemma
6.1
implies that the solvability condition for equation
(
8.5
)
is
{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}}U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}=0
, i.e.
U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}
lies in the kernel of the normal boundary acoustic operator:

\partial_{\!\zeta}(\rho^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}+\theta^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\!\partial_{\!\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})=0\,.

from which we deduce that
\rho^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}+\theta^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}
is constant in
\zeta
. Since
\rho^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}+\theta^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\rightarrow 0
as

\zeta\rightarrow\infty
, then

\rho^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}+\theta^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}=0\,.

(8.6)

Similarly, we have

\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}=0\,,

(8.7)

which also gives that on the boundary
\partial\Omega
,

\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}(x,\zeta\!=\!0)\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=0\,.

(8.8)

Combining (
8.4
) with the condition (
8.8
), we deduce that

\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=0\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\,.

(8.9)

Under these conditions,
g^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle g^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}

\displaystyle=B_{0}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1})+B_{1}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})

(8.10)

\displaystyle:=(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}+\{\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\otimes\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!:\!{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!\cdot\!{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}\}\,.

Note that
B_{1}
is a linear operator.

Step 3: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0})
in the interior.

To find the equation satisfied by

U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
, we consider the order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0})

in the interior part:

{\mathcal{L}}g^{\mathrm{int}}_{2}=v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!\!x}g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}-i\lambda_{0}g^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,.

(8.11)

Using the boundary condition (
8.9
) which means that

U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
is in the domain of the acoustic
operator
{\mathcal{A}}
, the solvability of (
8.11
) gives

{\mathcal{A}}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}=i\lambda_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,

(8.12)

which is a first order linear hyperbolic system with the boundary condition (
8.9
). If

i\lambda_{0}=0
, (
8.12
) is the so-called acoustic system whose solutions
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
satisfies the incompressible and Boussinesq relations.
This case will be treated in a separate paper
[
25
]
.

If
i\lambda_{0}\neq 0
,
then from the discussions in section
5
, especially (
5.8
), we know that
the system (
8.12
) has a family of solutions: for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
are eigenvectors of
{\mathcal{A}}
, and can be
constructed from the eigenvectors of the Laplace operator with
Neumann boundary condition, i.e.

U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}=U^{\tau,k}_{0}\,,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lambda_{0}=\tau\lambda^{k}\,,\quad k=1,2,\cdots

(8.13)

where
\tau
denotes the signs either
+
or
-
, see the discussion of the spectrum of
{\mathcal{A}}
in section

5
, in particular (
5.6
),
(
5.7
) and (
5.8
). Consequently, every term in the ansatz (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) depends on the choice of
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tau
.

Remark:
(
8.13
) is the building-block of
the construction of the approximate eigenvector

g^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}
and eigenvalues

\lambda^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}
for any
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
. It means that the leading order term of
(g^{k}_{\varepsilon,N},\lambda^{k}_{\varepsilon,N})
is in acoustic modes. For this reason, we call
g^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}
the boundary layer in
acoustic
regime.

Now we can represent

g^{\mathrm{int}}_{2}
as

\displaystyle g^{\mathrm{int}}_{2}

\displaystyle=I_{0}(U^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{2})+I_{2}(U^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})

(8.14)

\displaystyle:=U^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}\!\cdot\!(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})+\{\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\!:\!\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(v)+\nabla_{\!x}\theta^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(v)\}\,,

where
\mathrm{u}^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}=\sqrt{\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2{\mathrm{D}}}}\frac{\nabla_{\!x}\Psi^{k}}{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{0}}
and

\theta^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}({\mathrm{D}}+2)}}\Psi^{k}
, and
\Psi^{k}
is defined in (
5.6
).

Remark:
For the notational simplicity, from now on we drop
\tau
on the subscript, unless specifically mentioned.

8.4.
Induction: Round 1

Now we move to round 1 which studies the kinetic boundary layer, viscous boundary layer and interior terms
alternately
.

Step 1: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-1})
in the kinetic boundary layer.

The order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-1})
of the kinetic boundary layer in the ansatz gives that
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(x,v,\xi)
obeys the following linear kinetic boundary layer equation in
\Omega^{\delta}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
: (recalling the definition of
{\mathcal{L}}^{BL}
in (
6.2
))

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{BL}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad\mbox{in}\quad\xi>0\,,

(8.15)

\displaystyle g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\xi\rightarrow\infty\,,

with boundary condition at
\xi=0

L^{\mathcal{R}}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}=H^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\xi=0\,,\quad v\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)>0\,,

(8.16)

where
H^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}
is of the form: for
x\in{\partial\Omega}
,
v\cdot\mathrm{n}(x)>0,

\displaystyle H^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(x,v)

\displaystyle=-L^{\mathcal{R}}\tilde{g}^{k}_{1}+L^{\mathcal{D}}\tilde{g}^{k}_{0}

(8.17)

\displaystyle:=\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1})+\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})\,.

Here
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1})
is a linear function of
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}
and
U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}
, and
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})
is a linear function of
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}
and
U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}
. More specifically,

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}):=

\displaystyle-L^{\mathcal{R}}(I_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1})+B_{0}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}))

(8.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})(\tilde{\mathrm{u}}^{k}_{1}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})\,,

and

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}):=

\displaystyle-L^{\mathcal{R}}B_{1}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})+L^{\mathcal{D}}(I_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0})+B_{0}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}))

(8.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L^{\mathcal{R}}(\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\otimes\!\mathrm{n}\!:\!{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\mathrm{n}\!\cdot\!{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}})

\displaystyle-v\cdot[\tilde{\mathrm{u}}^{k}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}]\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}+\big(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{2}-\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}\big)\tilde{\theta}^{k}_{0}+\big(\mathrm{n}\cdot v-\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{2}\big)(\tilde{\mathrm{u}}^{k}_{0}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})\,.

The boundary conditions for the tangential components of
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
and
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
can be derived from the solvability condition of the above kinetic boundary layer equations. The formulas (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) of Lemma
6.3
give that on the boundary

{\partial\Omega}
,

[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}]^{\mathrm{tan}}=-[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}]^{\mathrm{tan}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}=-\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,

(8.20)

from which we can deduce the boundary conditions for

[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}]^{\mathrm{tan}}
and
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
because

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
and

\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
are already determined, thus their boundary values are known. Furthermore, from (
6.8
) of Lemma
6.3
, we also have

[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}+\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}]\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=0\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\,,

(8.21)

which implies
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1})=0
. Furthermore, from the boundary condition (
8.20
)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})=

\displaystyle L^{\mathcal{R}}(\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\otimes\!\mathrm{n}\!:\!{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\mathrm{n}\!\cdot\!{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}})

(8.22)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}v\cdot[\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}]^{\mathrm{tan}}+\big(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{2}-\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}\big)\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,,

which implies that although
formally
,
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}
depends on
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}
and
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}
which have not been fully determined at this stage, it in fact depends only on the boundary values of
U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
, thus once we solve
U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
, we can solve
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}

completely
. This will be finished at the end of the
Step 2
, see (
8.37
).

Step 2: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0})
in the viscous boundary layer.

The equations satisfied by
[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}]^{\mathrm{tan}}
and

\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
can be derived by considering the order

O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0})
of the viscous boundary layer:

{\mathcal{L}}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}=v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!\!x}\mathrm{d}\partial_{\!\zeta}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}+v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,.

(8.23)

Lemma
6.1
implies that the projection of the righthand
side of (
8.23
) must be in the null space of
{\mathcal{L}}
. We first use
the expression (
8.10
) of
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}
to calculate
the projection of

v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!\!x}\mathrm{d}\partial_{\!\zeta}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}
onto
Null
({\mathcal{L}})
. It is easy to see that components of

{\mathcal{P}}(\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{m}\mathrm{d}\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})_{l}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{ml})
on
1
and
\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\frac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}

are zeros, here
\mathcal{P}
is defined in (
2.11
). Applying Lemma
6.2
, we get

{\mathcal{P}}\left(\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{m}\mathrm{d}\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})_{l}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{ml}\right)=\left[\nu\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}+\nu(1-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}})\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}\big(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\big)\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\right]\cdot v\,.

Similarly, the components of

{\mathcal{P}}(\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{j}\mathrm{d}\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}\theta^{k,b}_{0}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}_{j})

on
1
and
v
are zeros. Then applying Lemma
6.2
again, we have

{\mathcal{P}}\left(\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{j}\mathrm{d}\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}\theta^{k,b}_{0}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}_{j}\right)=\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\kappa\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}\theta^{k,b}_{0}\big(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}\big)\,,

where kinematic viscosity
\nu
and thermal conductivity
\kappa
are given by (
3.5
). Based on above calculations the solvability conditions for
(
8.23
)
are a system of second order ordinary differential equations in
\zeta
:

-{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}}U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}=({\mathcal{A}}^{\pi}+\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{d}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,,

(8.24)

where the tangential acoustic operator
{\mathcal{A}}^{\pi}
and the normal diffusive operator
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{d}}
are
defined as

\mathcal{A}^{\pi}U^{\mathrm{b}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{div}_{\pi}(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi)\\
{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}(\rho^{\mathrm{b}}+\theta^{\mathrm{b}})\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\\
\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\mathrm{div}_{\pi}(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi)\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{d}}U^{\mathrm{b}}:=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\nu{\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}+\nu(1-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}){\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\\
\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\kappa{\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}\theta^{\mathrm{b}}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(8.25)

for
U^{\mathrm{b}}=(\rho^{\mathrm{b}}\,,\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\,,\theta^{\mathrm{b}})^{\top}
. Here we use the notation
\mathrm{div}_{\pi}(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi)={\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}(\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha})
. Recall the normal acoustic operator
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{d}}
is defined in (
8.1
).

The ODE system (
8.24
) can be solved as follows: projecting the system (
8.24
) on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})
and Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})^{\perp}
respectively, the projection on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})
gives the first order equations of
\rho^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}+\theta^{b}_{1}
and
\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
which can be solved by using the vanishing condition at
\zeta=\infty
, while the projection on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})^{\perp}
gives the second order equations of
\mathrm{u}^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi
and
\theta^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}
which can be solved by using the vanishing condition at
\zeta=\infty
and the Robin boundary condition at
\zeta=0
, i.e. (
8.20
).

1.
Solve
\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}
:

We first project the system (
8.24
) on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})^{\perp}
, the
\mathrm{u}\mbox{-}
component of is

{\partial}_{\!\zeta}(\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1})=0\,,\quad\mbox{hence}\quad\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}=0\,.

(8.26)

The
\rho\mbox{-}
component (or equivalently the
\theta\mbox{-}
component) of the projection on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})^{\perp}
is

-\partial_{\!\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})=\mathrm{div}_{\pi}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi)+\kappa\partial^{2}_{\!\zeta\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,,

(8.27)

to solve which we need to first solve
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi
and
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
. Note that in the derivation of (
8.26
) and (
8.27
) the relations (
8.6
) and (
8.7
) are used.

2.
Solve
[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}]^{\mathrm{tan}}
:
We next project the system (
8.24
) on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})
, the
\mathrm{u}\mbox{-}
component gives the equation for
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle(\nu{\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}]

\displaystyle=0\,,

(8.28)

\displaystyle[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}](\zeta=0)\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}(\pi(x))\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\zeta\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0\,.

where the boundary condition in the second line of (
8.28
)
follows from the fact that
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}
is the tangential components of

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
because
\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}
is
tangential to
{\partial\Omega}
, see the arguments after (
4.3
). The solution to ODE
(
8.28
)
is

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}(\pi(x),\zeta)\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}=\tfrac{1}{c_{\chi}\sqrt{\nu}-1}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}(\pi(x))\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha})\exp\big(-(1+\tau i)\sqrt{\tfrac{\lambda^{k}_{0}}{2\nu}}\zeta\big)\,,

(8.29)

where
\tau=+
or
-
,
c_{\chi}=-\tfrac{1+\tau i}{2\chi}\sqrt{2\lambda^{k}_{0}}
. We denote the solution (
8.29
) by

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}(\pi(x),\zeta)\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi=\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{0}(\zeta,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

(8.30)

where
\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{0}(\zeta,\cdot)
is a linear function. Note that in the righthand side of (
8.29
),
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
should be understood as its value on the boundary, i.e.
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}(\pi(x))
.

3.
Solve
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
:
The
\rho\mbox{-}
component (or equivalently the
\theta\mbox{-}
component) of the projection Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})
yields
the equation for
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
:

\displaystyle(\kappa{\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(8.31)

\displaystyle[\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}](\zeta=0)

\displaystyle=-\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}(\pi(x))\,,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\zeta\rightarrow\infty}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}

\displaystyle=0\,.

The solution to
(
8.31
)
is

\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}(\pi(x),\zeta)=\tfrac{1}{c_{\chi}\sqrt{\tilde{\kappa}}-1}\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}(\pi(x))\exp\left(-(1+\tau i)\sqrt{\tfrac{\lambda^{k}_{0}}{2\kappa}}\zeta\right)\,,

(8.32)

where
\tilde{\kappa}=(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1})^{2}\kappa
. We denote the solution (
8.32
) by

\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}(\pi(x),\zeta)=\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{0}(\zeta,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

(8.33)

where
\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{0}(\zeta,\cdot)
is a linear function.

4.
Solve
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
:

Now the equation (
8.27
) becomes

{\partial}_{\!\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})=-{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha})-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,.

(8.34)

By integrating the equation
(
8.34
)
from
\zeta
to
\infty
, it gives

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}=\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(\zeta,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

where
\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b}}_{1,0}(\zeta,\cdot)
is linear. In particular, letting
\zeta=0
gives the value of
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}
on the boundary
\partial\Omega
:

\displaystyle-\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1-\tau i}{\sqrt{2\lambda^{k}_{0}}}\left(\mathrm{div}_{\pi}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi)\tfrac{\sqrt{\nu}}{c_{\chi}\sqrt{\nu}-1}+\tau i\lambda^{k}_{0}\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\tfrac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{c_{\chi}\sqrt{\tilde{\kappa}}-1}\right)

(8.35)

\displaystyle=Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})=\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(0,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

where
Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(\cdot)
is a linear function. Consequently, (
8.21
) gives the boundary value

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{n}=Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,.

Finally we can represent
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}
from (
8.23
):

\displaystyle g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}=

\displaystyle(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}+\partial_{\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\otimes\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}:\!{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+\partial_{\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!\cdot\!{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}

(8.36)

\displaystyle+{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\otimes\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\cdot{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}\left(\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{j}\mathrm{d}\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})_{k}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{jk}+\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{j}\mathrm{d}\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}\theta^{k,b}_{0}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{0}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{2})+B_{1}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1})+B_{2}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})\,,

where

B_{2}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}):={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}\left((v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})\partial_{\!\zeta}B_{1}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})+(v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha})\partial_{\!\pi^{\alpha}}B_{0}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})\right)\,.

Solve
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}
:

Now we can represent (
8.17
) as,

\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(U^{k}_{0})

\displaystyle=h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})

(8.37)

\displaystyle=L^{\mathcal{R}}(\partial_{\!\zeta}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{0}(0,U^{k}_{0})\nabla_{\!x}\pi\!\otimes\!\mathrm{n}\!:\!{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+\partial_{\!\zeta}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{0}(0,U^{k}_{0})\mathrm{n}\!\cdot\!{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}})

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}v\cdot\partial_{\!\zeta}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{0}(0,U^{k}_{0})\nabla_{\!x}\pi+\left(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{2}-\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}\right)\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{0}(0,U^{k}_{0})\,,

which is completely determined. Thus we can solve
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}
which we denote by

g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(\pi(x),\xi,v)=K_{1}(\xi,v,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}(\pi(x)))\,,

where
K_{1}(\xi,v,\cdot)
is a linear function.

We summarize that in
Step 2
by considering the order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0})
in the viscous boundary layer, we determine:

•

\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}
;

•

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi
and
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
, thus
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
;

•

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
and hence the boundary value of
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{n}
when we take
\zeta=0
, and consequently
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{n}
which will be used in
Step 3
;

•

expression of
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}
;

•

g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}
.

Step 3: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{1})
in the interior.

The order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{1})
in the interior part yields

{\mathcal{L}}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{3}=v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}-i\lambda^{k}_{1}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,

(8.38)

and the solvability condition of which is

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}

\displaystyle=i\lambda^{k}_{1}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,\quad\mbox{in}\quad\!\Omega\,,

(8.39)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\,.

To solve (
8.39
), we apply Lemme
5.1
. The formula (
5.20
) gives

i\lambda^{k}_{1}=\int_{\partial\Omega}[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{n}]\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}=\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,.

(8.40)

Note that

\nabla_{\!x}\Psi^{k}=g^{\gamma\beta}\tfrac{{\partial}\Psi^{k}}{{\partial}\pi^{\beta}}\tfrac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\pi^{\gamma}}
, and
\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}=g^{\alpha\delta}\tfrac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\pi^{\delta}}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}(\nabla_{\!x}\Psi^{k}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha})\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}=

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial\Omega}g_{\gamma\delta}g^{\alpha\delta}g^{\beta\gamma}\tfrac{{\partial}\Psi^{k}}{{\partial}\pi^{\alpha}}\tfrac{{\partial}\Psi^{k}}{{\partial}\pi^{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial\Omega}|\nabla_{\!\pi}\Psi^{k}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,,

where
\nabla_{\!\pi}
is the tangential gradient on
{\partial\Omega}
. Thus

i\lambda^{k}_{1}=\Lambda_{1}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\nabla_{\!\pi}\Psi^{k}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}+\Lambda_{2}\int_{\partial\Omega}\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}(\lambda^{k}_{0})^{2}|\Psi^{k}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,,

(8.41)

where

\Lambda_{1}=-\tfrac{\sqrt{\nu}}{\sqrt{2(\lambda^{k}_{0})^{3}}}\tfrac{(2a+1)+\tau i}{(a+1)^{2}+a^{2}}\sqrt{\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}}}\,,\quad\!\Lambda_{2}=-\tfrac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{2(\lambda^{k}_{0})^{3}}}\tfrac{(2b+1)+\tau i}{(b+1)^{2}+b^{2}}\sqrt{\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}}}\,,

a=\tfrac{\sqrt{2\lambda^{k}_{0}\nu}}{2\chi}\,,\quad b=\tfrac{\sqrt{2\lambda^{k}_{0}\kappa}}{2\chi}\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\,.

From the expression (
8.41
),
i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1}

has an important property (
no matter
\tau=+
or
-
!
):

\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})<0\,.

(8.42)

Proof.

From (
8.41
), we can only conclude
\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{k}_{1})\leq 0
. The strict negativity comes from the following argument. Indeed, assume that
\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{k}_{1})=0
. This would imply that
\nabla_{\!x}\Psi^{k}=0
and
\Psi^{k}=0
on the boundary
{\partial\Omega}
. Hence, extending
\Psi^{k}
by
0
outside of
\Omega
and denoting by
\tilde{\Psi}^{k}
this extension, we see that
\tilde{\Psi}^{k}
is an eigenvector of
-\Delta_{\!x}
on the whole
space with compact support which is impossible. Hence
(
8.42
) holds.
∎

Remark:
The above formula
(
8.41
)
and the strict inequality (
8.42
) is
crucial in this paper, because it gives dissipativity, which will be
used later in proving the damping of the acoustic waves in the
Navier-Stokes limit.

Case 1
: If
\frac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}[\lambda^{k}_{0}]^{2}
is a
simple
eigenvalue of
-\Delta_{\!x}
with Neumann boundary condition, see (
5.6
). By Lemma
5.1
, (
8.40
) is the only solvability condition under which the system (
8.39
) can be solved uniquely as

U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}=Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

(8.43)

where
Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\in\mathrm{Null}({\mathcal{A}})^{\perp}
. Note that the system (
8.39
) is linear and the boundary data
Z^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}
is also linear in
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
. So
Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
also linearly depends on
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
, i.e.
Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(\cdot)
is a linear function.

Case 2
: If the eigenvalues
\lambda^{k}_{0}
is
not simple
, an additional compatibility condition is needed, which is given by the formula (
5.22
):

\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}=0\,,\quad\mbox{if}\quad\lambda^{k}_{0}=\lambda^{l}_{0}\quad\mbox{and}\quad k\neq l\,.

(8.44)

Specifically, this condition reads as

\Lambda_{1}\int_{\partial\Omega}\nabla_{\!\pi}\Psi^{k}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!\pi}\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}+\Lambda_{2}\int_{\partial\Omega}\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}(\lambda^{k}_{0})^{2}\Psi^{k}\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}=0\,,\quad\mbox{if}\quad\lambda^{k}_{0}=\lambda^{l}_{0}\quad\mbox{and}\quad k\neq l\,.

We can define the quadratic form
Q_{1}
and the symmetric operator
L_{1}
on
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
as

Q_{1}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l})=\int_{{\partial\Omega}}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,,

(8.45)

and

L_{1}\Psi^{k}=i\lambda^{k}_{1}\Psi^{k}\,,

(8.46)

and the orthogonality condition (
8.44
) is

Q_{1}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l})=0\,,\quad\mbox{if}\quad\!\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\!l\neq k\,.

(8.47)

Under these conditions, applying Lemma

5.1
, we solve
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}
modulo

\mathrm{Ker}\big({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\big)
, i.e.

U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}=Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})+P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\,,

(8.48)

where
P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}
is defined as

P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}=\sum\limits_{l\neq k\,,\lambda^{l}_{0}=\lambda^{k}_{0}}a^{kl}_{1}U^{l,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,

(8.49)

where
a^{kl}_{1}=\langle U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\,|\,U^{l,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\rangle
will be determined later. Finally we can represent
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{3}
as

\displaystyle g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{3}

\displaystyle=(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{3}+\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\!:\!\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(v)+\nabla_{\!x}\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(v)

(8.50)

\displaystyle=I_{0}(U^{k}_{3})+I_{2}(P_{0}U^{k}_{1})+I_{3}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,.

If
\lambda^{k}_{0}
is a
simple
eigenvalue, the
P_{0}U^{k}_{1}
term vanishes. Thus we finish the
Round 1
in the induction.

Remark:
The orthogonality condition (
8.44
) are given on the eigenfunctions of
-\Delta_{x}
with Neumann boundary condition with respect to the eigenvalue
[\lambda^{k}_{0}]^{2}
. Usually, the eigenfunctions with Neumann boundary conditions are determined up to some constants, so not unique. (
8.44
) is only used to determine the eigenfunctions corresponding to multiple eigenvalue, and it does not give any new assumption on the geometry of the domain
\Omega
.

8.5.
Induction: Round 2

Now we move to the second round in the induction, which also includes three steps by considering terms in the kinetic, viscous boundary layers and interior alternatively.

Step 1: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0})
in the kinetic boundary layer.

The order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{0})
of the kinetic boundary layer in the ansatz gives that
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}
satisfies the linear
boundary layer equation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{BL}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}=0\,,

\displaystyle\mbox{in}\quad\xi>0\,,

(8.51)

\displaystyle g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}\longrightarrow 0\,,

\displaystyle\mbox{as}\quad\xi\rightarrow\infty\,,

with boundary condition at
\xi=0

L^{\mathcal{R}}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}=H^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\xi=0\,,\quad v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0\,,

(8.52)

where
H^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}
is of the form:

\displaystyle H^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}

\displaystyle=-L^{\mathcal{R}}\tilde{g}^{k}_{2}+L^{\mathcal{D}}(\tilde{g}^{k}_{1}+g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1})

(8.53)

\displaystyle=\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{2},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{2})+\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1})+\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{2}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})\,.

Here

\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})=-L^{\mathcal{R}}(I_{2}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0})+B_{2}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}))+L^{\mathcal{D}}(B_{1}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})+K_{1}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}))\,.

(8.54)

Comparing with (
8.17
), we note that the first two terms of (
8.53
) are the same as (
8.17
) replacing by arguments with subscripts higher than 1. In other words,

\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{2},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}):=-2(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})(\tilde{\mathrm{u}}^{k}_{2}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})\,,

(8.55)

and

\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}):=L^{\mathcal{R}}(\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\otimes\!\mathrm{n}\!:\!{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\mathrm{n}\!\cdot\!{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}})-v\cdot[\tilde{\mathrm{u}}^{k}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}]\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}+\big(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{2}-\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}\big)\tilde{\theta}^{k}_{1}\,.

(8.56)

Note that comparing to (
8.19
), we have used the boundary condition
\mathrm{u}^{k}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{n}=0\,.

Next, the formulas (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) give the boundary conditions

[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}]^{\mathrm{tan}}=-[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}]^{\mathrm{tan}}+V^{\mathrm{u}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

(8.57)

and

\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1,0}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}=-\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}+V^{\theta}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

(8.58)

where

\displaystyle V^{\mathrm{u}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}[2d(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}]^{\mathrm{tan}}+\left[\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}L^{\mathcal{D}}S_{1}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})vM\,\mathrm{d}v\right]^{\mathrm{tan}}\,,

(8.59)

\displaystyle V^{\theta}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}L^{\mathcal{D}}S_{1}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})|v|^{2}M\,\mathrm{d}v\,,

and
S_{1}=-({\partial}_{\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\otimes\!\mathrm{n}:\widehat{\mathrm{A}}+{\partial}_{\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\mathrm{n}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{B}})+K_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
. Here we have used the facts

\displaystyle\left[\mathrm{n}_{i}\mathrm{n}_{j}\mathrm{n}_{l}\partial^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})_{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}(v_{i}\widehat{A}_{jk})v_{l}v_{m}\,M\mathrm{d}v\right]^{\mathrm{tan}}

\displaystyle+\left[\mathrm{n}_{i}\mathrm{n}_{j}\mathrm{n}_{l}\partial^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}(v_{i}\widehat{B}_{j})v_{l}v_{m}\,M\mathrm{d}v\right]^{\mathrm{tan}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}_{i}\mathrm{n}_{j}\mathrm{n}_{l}\partial^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})_{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}(v_{i}\widehat{A}_{jk})v_{l}|v|^{2}\,M\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle+\mathrm{n}_{i}\mathrm{n}_{j}\mathrm{n}_{l}\partial^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}(v_{i}\widehat{B}_{j})v_{l}|v|^{2}\,M\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=0\,,

Thus, (
8.56
) is reduced to

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}

\displaystyle(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1})=L^{\mathcal{R}}(\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\otimes\!\mathrm{n}\!:\!{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\mathrm{n}\!\cdot\!{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}})

(8.60)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}v\cdot[\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}]^{\mathrm{tan}}-v\cdot V^{\mathrm{u}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})+\big(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{2}-\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}\big)\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}+\big(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{2}-\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}\big)V^{\theta}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,.

Furthermore, the formula (
6.8
) gives the boundary condition on the normal direction:

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=-\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\,,

(8.61)

from which we know
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{2},U^{\mathrm{b}}_{2})=0
. Thus from (
8.53
), to solve
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}
we don’t need know
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}
and
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}
, although formally it does. Thus, once we solve
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi
and
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}
, we can solve
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}
.

Step 2: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{1})
in the viscous boundary layer.

The equations of
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi
and

\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}
can be found by analyzing the order

O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{1})
of the viscous boundary layer in
(
7.5
) which gives

{\mathcal{L}}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{3}=(v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!\!x}\mathrm{d})\partial_{\!\zeta}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}+(v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}){\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}-i\lambda^{k}_{1}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,.

(8.62)

The solvability condition for (
8.62
) yields that

-{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}}U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}=({\mathcal{A}}^{\pi}+\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{d}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}+(\mathcal{F}_{1}-i\lambda^{k}_{1})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,,

(8.63)

where the linear operator
\mathcal{F}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})=(\mathcal{F}^{\rho}_{1},\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{u}}_{1},\mathcal{F}^{\theta}_{1})^{\top}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})
is defined as

\mathcal{F}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\!\cdot\!(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}):=\mathcal{P}\left\{v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\partial_{\!\zeta}B_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})+v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi\partial_{\pi}B_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\right\}\,.

The precise form of
\mathcal{F}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})
is tedious and not easy to represent explicitly, however, is not of great importance for the later analysis. The
\rho
component vanishes, while the
\mathrm{u}
and
\theta
components are linear functions of third order
\zeta
derivatives of
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
and
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
respectively.

Similar as in solving (
8.24
), we derive the ODE satisfied by
\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}
from the
\mathrm{u}\mbox{-}
component of the projection of (
8.63
) on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})^{\perp}
:

-{\partial}_{\zeta}(\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2})=\left\{\nu(2-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}){\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\right\}[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}]+\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{u}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\,.

(8.64)

Note that both
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
and
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}
(which is included in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{u}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
) are known from the last round and linear in
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
, so the righthand side of (
8.64
) is known and a linear function of
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
. Integrating (
8.64
) from
\zeta
to
\infty
gives

\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}=Y^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(\zeta,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

(8.65)

where
Y^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(\zeta,\cdot)
is a linear function. We can also derive the ODEs satisfied by
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi
and
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}
:

\left\{\nu{\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\right\}[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi]=i\lambda^{k}_{1}[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi]-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{u}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi\,,

(8.66)

and

\left\{\kappa{\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\right\}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}=i\lambda^{k}_{1}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}-(\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\mathcal{F}^{\rho}_{1}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\mathcal{F}^{\theta}_{1})(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\,,

(8.67)

with boundary conditions (
8.57
) and (
8.58
) respectively. Because of the linearity of the above equations, we can solve (
8.66
) and (
8.67
) as

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{0}(\zeta,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})+\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{1}(\zeta,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

(8.68)

\displaystyle\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{0}(\zeta,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})+\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{1}(\zeta,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

recalling
\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{0}
and
\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{0}
are defined in (
8.29
) and (
8.32
) respectively, and
\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{1}
is the solution of

\displaystyle(\nu{\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})\mathrm{u}

\displaystyle=i\lambda^{k}_{1}[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi]-\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{u}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi\,,

\displaystyle[\mathrm{u}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}](\zeta=0)

\displaystyle=-Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})_{\mathrm{u}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi+V^{\mathrm{u}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi\,,

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{u}

\displaystyle=0\,,

and
\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{1}
is the solution of

\displaystyle(\nu{\partial}^{2}_{\!\zeta\!\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})\theta

\displaystyle=i\lambda^{k}_{1}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}-\mathcal{F}^{\theta}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\,,

\displaystyle[\theta-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}](\zeta=0)

\displaystyle=-Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})_{\theta}+V^{\theta}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta\rightarrow\infty}\theta

\displaystyle=0\,,

where
Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})_{\mathrm{u}}
and
Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})_{\theta}
denote the
\mathrm{u}
and
\theta
components of
Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
respectively.

From the
\rho\mbox{-}
component of the projection of (
8.63
) on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})^{\perp}
we can also derive the equation for
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
:

-{\partial}_{\zeta}[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}]=\mathrm{div}_{\!\pi}[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi]+i\lambda^{k}_{0}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}+i\lambda^{k}_{1,0}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,.

Integrating from
\zeta
to
\infty
, we can solve
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}=\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(\zeta,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})+\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(\zeta,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
, in particular, by taking
\zeta=0

-\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})+Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})=\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}\,,

(8.69)

where the second equality followed from (
8.61
).

Finally,
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{3}
can be represented as

\displaystyle g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{3}=

\displaystyle(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{3}+\partial_{\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}\otimes\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!:\!{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+\partial_{\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{2}\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!\cdot\!{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}

\displaystyle+{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\otimes\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\cdot{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}\left(\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{j}\mathrm{d}\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1})_{k}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{jk}+\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{j}\mathrm{d}\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}\theta^{k,b}_{1}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}\left((\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{j}\mathrm{\pi^{\alpha}}+\partial_{j}\mathrm{d}\partial_{i}\mathrm{\pi^{\alpha}})(\partial^{2}_{\zeta\pi^{\alpha}}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})_{k}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{jk}+\partial^{2}_{\zeta\pi^{\alpha}}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}v_{i}\widehat{B}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}\left(\partial_{i}\mathrm{d}\partial_{j}\mathrm{d}\partial_{k}\mathrm{d}[\partial^{3}_{\!\zeta\zeta\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})_{l}v_{i}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}(v_{j}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{kl})+\partial^{3}_{\!\zeta\zeta\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0,0}v_{i}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}(v_{j}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}_{k})]\right)

\displaystyle-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\left({\partial}_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\otimes\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!:\!{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}+{\partial}_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!\cdot\!{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{0}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{3})+B_{1}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{2})+B_{2}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{1})+B_{3}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})\,,

where
B_{3}(v,U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})
can be represented as

\displaystyle B_{3}(v,U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}\left((v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})\partial_{\!\zeta}B_{2}(v,U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})+[(v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha})\partial_{\!\pi^{\alpha}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}]B_{1}(v,U^{\mathrm{b}}_{0})\right)\,.

After
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{1}
is solved (modulo
P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}
), go back to the linear kinetic boundary layer equations (
8.51
)-(
8.52
) of
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}
. Straightforward calculations by regrouping terms show that

H^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}=h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{1}(P_{0}U^{k}_{1})+h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

where
h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{2}(\cdot)
is linear and whose detailed expression we omit here. Using the linearity of the kinetic boundary layer equation and the boundary conditions, it is easy to solve that

g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{2}=K_{1}(\xi,v,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})+K_{2}(\xi,v,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

where
K_{2}(\xi,v,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
is the solution of the kinetic boundary layer equations (
8.51
)-(
8.52
) with the boundary condition

(\gamma_{+}-L\gamma_{-})K_{2}=h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\xi=0\,,\quad v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0\,.

It is obvious that
K_{2}(\xi,v,\cdot)
is linear.

Step 3: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{2})
in the interior.

The order
O(\varepsilon)
in the interior part of (
7.5
) yields

{\mathcal{L}}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{4}=v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}-i\lambda^{k}_{1}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}-i\lambda^{k}_{2}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,

(8.70)

the solvability condition of which is

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}

\displaystyle=i\lambda^{k}_{1}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}+(i\lambda^{k}_{2}-\mathcal{D})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\!\Omega\,,

(8.71)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}\cdot\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})+Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!\partial\Omega\,,

where
\mathcal{D}
is defined as

\mathcal{D}U=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\nu\mathrm{div}_{\!x}(\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}+\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}^{\top}-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\mathrm{div}_{\!x}\mathrm{u})\\
\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\kappa\Delta_{x}\theta\end{pmatrix}\,.

Remark:
To apply Lemma
5.1
, we require the following orthogonality condition for
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}
:

\langle U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}\,|\,U^{l,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\rangle=0\,,\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\!m\neq 0\quad\!\mbox{or}\quad\!k\neq l\,.

(8.72)

To solve (
8.71
), first we apply the formula (
5.20
) of Lemma
5.1
to deduce

\displaystyle i\lambda^{k}_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}\cdot\mathrm{n}]\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

(8.73)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}+\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(PU^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}+\langle\mathcal{D}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}|U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\rangle\,,

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}+\langle\mathcal{D}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}|U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\rangle\,.

Case 1
: If
\lambda^{k}_{0}
is a
simple
eigenvalue, then
PU^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}=0
, thus
i\lambda^{k}_{1}
is given by (
8.73
).

Otherwise,
\lambda^{k}_{0}
is not a
simple
eigenvalue, note that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(PU^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l\neq k\,,\lambda^{l}_{0}=\lambda^{k}_{0}}a^{kl}_{1}\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(U^{l,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

(8.74)

\displaystyle=0\,,

because of the orthogonality condition (
8.45
) and (
8.47
) . The above two identities illustrate that no matter
\lambda^{k}_{0}
is simple or not,
i\lambda^{k}_{2}
is completely determined, which is given by (
8.73
).

When
\lambda^{k}_{0}
is not simple, the compatibility condition (
5.22
) is needed, which gives

i\lambda^{l}_{1}a^{kl}_{1}+\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}=i\lambda^{k}_{1}a^{kl}_{1}\quad\mbox{if}\quad\lambda^{k}_{0}=\lambda^{l}_{0}\quad\mbox{and}\quad k\neq l\,.

(8.75)

Case 2
: If
\lambda^{k}_{0}
is not a
simple
eigenvalue, but
i\lambda^{k}_{1}
is a
simple
eigenvalue of
L_{1}
which is defined in (
8.46
), then for all
l\neq k
with
i\lambda^{l}_{0}=i\lambda^{k}_{0}
, we have
i\lambda^{l}_{1}\neq i\lambda^{k}_{1}
. For this case
a^{kl}_{1}
can be solved from (
8.75
) as

a^{kl}_{1}=\frac{1}{i\lambda^{k}_{1}-i\lambda^{l}_{1}}\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,.

(8.76)

Thus
P_{0}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})
is completely determined, and no additional conditions on
\mathrm{H}_{0}(\lambda)
rather than (
8.44
), or equivalently (
8.47
) is needed. However,

Case 3
: If
\lambda^{k}_{0}
is not a
simple
eigenvalue, and
i\lambda^{k}_{1}
is also not a
simple
eigenvalue of
L_{1}
, we need more orthogonality condition on

\mathrm{H}_{1}=\mathrm{H}(\lambda_{1})=\{\Psi\in\mathrm{H}_{0}:L_{1}\Psi=i\lambda^{k}_{1}\Psi\}\,.

This orthogonality condition comes from (
8.75
):

\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}=0\,,\quad\mbox{if}\quad\!l\neq k\quad\!\lambda^{l}_{0}=\lambda^{k}_{0}\quad\!\lambda^{l}_{1}=\lambda^{k}_{1}\,.

(8.77)

We can define a quadratic form
Q_{2}
and the symmetric operator
L_{2}
on
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})
as

Q_{2}(\Psi^{k}\,,\Psi^{l})=\int_{\partial\Omega}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}+\langle\mathcal{D}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}|U^{l,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\rangle\,,

(8.78)

and
L_{2}\Psi^{k}=i\lambda^{k}_{2}\Psi^{k}
, which satisfies that

Q_{2}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l})=\int_{\Omega}L_{2}(\Psi^{k})\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Be these definitions, we have
i\lambda^{k}_{2}=Q_{2}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{k})
, and the condition (
8.77
) is

Q_{2}(\Psi^{k}\,,\Psi^{l})=0\,,\quad\mbox{if}\quad\!\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\quad\!\mbox{and}\quad\!l\neq k\,.

Under these conditions, the equation (
8.71
) can be solved in the following way: Let
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}=U^{1}+U^{2}
, where
U^{1}
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})U^{1}

\displaystyle=i\lambda^{k}_{1}P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{1}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})\,,

whose solution in Ker
({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})^{\perp}
is
Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})
, and
U^{2}
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})U^{2}

\displaystyle=i\lambda^{k}_{1}Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})+(i\lambda^{k}_{2}-\mathcal{D})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{2}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

whose solution in Ker
({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})^{\perp}
is
completely
determined, and is denoted by
Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
. In summary, the equation (
8.71
) is

U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}=P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}+Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}(P_{0}U^{k}_{1})+Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,

where
P_{0}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})=(P_{1}+P_{1}^{\perp})(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1})
in which
P_{1}^{\perp}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}
is already completely determined
in (
8.76
) and
P_{1}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}
will be determined later, and
P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}
is defined the same as in (
8.49
), i.e.

P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}=\sum\limits_{l\neq k\,,\lambda^{l}_{0}=\lambda^{k}_{0}}a^{kl}_{2}U^{l,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,

where
a^{kl}_{2}=\langle U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}\,|\,U^{l,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\rangle
will be determined later. Finally we can represent
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{4}
as

\displaystyle g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{4}=

\displaystyle I_{0}(U^{k}_{4})+I_{2}(U^{k}_{2})+I_{4}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})

(8.79)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{4}+\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}\!:\!\widehat{\mathrm{A}}(v)+\nabla_{\!x}\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}\!\cdot\!\widehat{\mathrm{B}}(v)

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}\left(\partial^{2}_{x_{i}x_{j}}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})_{k}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{jk}+\partial^{2}_{x_{i}x_{j}}\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}v_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\left(\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\!:\!{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}+\nabla_{\!x}\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\!\cdot\!{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\widehat{\mathrm{B}}\right)\,,

where
I_{4}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}\left\{(v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})I_{2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\right\}\,.
Thus we conclude the
Round 2
in the induction.

8.6.
General case: Induction hypothesis

For
m\geq 3
, we assume that we have finished the
(m-1)\mbox{-}th
round, i.e. used the information from the kinetic boundary layer, the viscous boundary and the interior till the order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m-3})
,
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m-2})
and
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m-1})
respectively. Before we solve the next round, we write down the hypothesis that summarizes what we were able to construct till now. We write down this in following 10 statements that we need to check for the
m\mbox{-}th
round.

\bf(P^{1}_{m-1}):
For
2\leq j\leq m-1
,
\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}=\sum^{j}_{h=2}Y^{\mathrm{b}}_{h}(\zeta,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j-h})\,;
For
j=0,1
,
\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}=0\,.

\bf(P^{2}_{m-1}):
For
0\leq j\leq m-2
,
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi=\sum^{j}_{h=0}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{h}(\zeta,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j-h})\,;

\bf(P^{3}_{m-1}):
For
0\leq j\leq m-2
,
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}=\sum^{j}_{h=0}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{h}(\zeta,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j-h})\,;

\bf(P^{4}_{m-1}):
For
1\leq j\leq m-1
,
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}=\sum^{j}_{h=1}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b}}_{h}(\zeta,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j-h})\,.
Taking
\zeta=0
, we deduce that on the boundary we have
-\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}\cdot\mathrm{n}=\sum^{j}_{h=1}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{h}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j-h})\,.

\bf(P^{5}_{m-1}):
For
0\leq j\leq m
,
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}=\sum^{j}_{h=0}B_{h}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{j-h})\,,
where
B_{h}
for
h\geq 0
is defined iteratively starting from
B_{0}(U^{\mathrm{b}})=(1,v,\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\frac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{\mathrm{b}}
:

B_{h}(U^{\mathrm{b}})={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}\{v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\partial_{\!\zeta}B_{h-1}(U^{\mathrm{b}})+v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi\partial_{\!\pi}B_{h-1}(U^{\mathrm{b}})-\sum^{h-3}_{l=0}i\lambda^{k}_{l}B_{h-2-l}(U^{\mathrm{b}})\}\,.

(8.80)

\bf(P^{6}_{m-1}):
For
1\leq j\leq m-1
,

g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{j}=\sum^{j}_{h=1}K_{h}(v,\xi,P_{0}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j-h}))\,,

(8.81)

where the linear operator
K_{h}(v,\xi,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
is the solution to the linear kinetic boundary layer equation (
6.3
)-(
6.4
) with the source term
s^{\mathrm{bb}}_{h}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
and the boundary source term
h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{h}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
.

\bf(P^{7}_{m-1}):
For
1\leq j\leq m-1
,
i\lambda^{k}_{j}=Q_{j}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{k})
, where the quadratic form
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are defined in (
8.45
) and (
8.78
) respectively, and
Q_{j}
for
3\leq j\leq m-1
is defined as

Q_{j}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l})=\sqrt{\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2{\mathrm{D}}}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\{Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{j}+V^{\mathrm{n}}_{j}\}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}+\sum^{j}_{h=2}\langle\mathcal{G}_{h}(Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{j-h}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}))|U^{l,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\rangle\,.

(8.82)

Note
\mathcal{G}_{h}
is defined in (
8.110
).

\bf(P^{8}_{m-1}):
For
0\leq j\leq m-1
,
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j}=P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j}+\sum^{j}_{h=1}Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{h}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j-h})\,;

\bf(P^{9}_{m-1}):
For
0\leq j\leq m+1
,
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j}=I_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{j})+I_{2}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{j-2})+\sum^{j}_{h=4}I_{j}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{j-h}),
where where
I_{h}
for
h\geq 0
is defined iteratively starting from
I_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}})=(1,v,\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\frac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})U^{\mathrm{int}}
,
I_{1}=0
:

I_{h}(U^{\mathrm{int}})={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}\{v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}I_{h-2}(U^{\mathrm{int}})-\sum^{h-4}_{l=0}i\lambda^{k}_{l}I_{h-2-m}(U^{\mathrm{int}})\}\,.

(8.83)

\bf(P^{10}_{m-1}):
The last assumption to check deals with the number of orthogonality conditions needed and specifies what is already determined and what is still not determined in the construction. We distinguish between
m
cases:

Case 1:

i\lambda^{k}_{h}
is a simple eigenvalue of
L_{h}
for

0\leq h\leq m-2
. No orthogonality condition is needed, and every term in the expansion is fully determined;

Case j (
2\leq j\leq m
):

i\lambda^{k}_{h}
is a multiple eigenvalue of
L_{h}
for
0\leq h\leq j-2
, but
i\lambda_{j-1}
a simple eigenvalue of
L_{j-1}\,.

(Note: the
case m
means that
all the eigenvalues
i{\lambda^{k}_{h}}
for
0\leq h\leq m-2
are multiple eigenvalues.)

•

We need the orthogonality conditions: For each
0\leq h\leq j-2
,

Q_{h+1}(\Psi^{k}\,,\Psi^{l})=0\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\!\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{0}\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{h}\,,

(8.84)

where for
h\geq 1
, the space
\mathrm{H}_{h}=\mathrm{H}_{h}(\lambda_{h})=\{\Psi\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{h-1}(\lambda_{h-1}):L_{h}\Psi=i\lambda_{h}\Psi\}
.

•

For
1\leq h\leq m-j
,
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{h}
are completely determined. (for the
case m
, no term is completely determined.)

•

For
m-j+1\leq h\leq m-1
,
(P_{0}^{\perp}+\cdots+P_{m-1-h}^{\perp})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{h}
are determined.

•

For
m-j+1\leq h\leq m-1
,
P_{m-1-h}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{h}
are not determined,

where
P_{h-1}
is the orthogonal projection on
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{h-1}(\lambda_{h-1})
, and
P_{h-1}=P_{h}+P^{\perp}_{h}
, where
P^{\perp}_{h}
is the orthogonal projection on
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{h-1}(\lambda_{h-1})\cap\mathrm{H}^{\perp}_{h}(\lambda_{h})\,.

Remark:
Regarding the condition (
8.84
),
actually we have a stronger orthogonality
property which is actually equivalent to (
8.84
), namely :
for each
0\leq h\leq j-2
,

\displaystyle Q_{h+1}(\Psi_{k}\,,\Psi_{l})

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\!l\neq k,\quad\Psi_{k},\Psi_{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{0}\,.

(8.85)

Indeed, we just need to use that the
L_{h}
leave stable the spaces

\mathrm{H}_{h}
. Of course, we have to define
L_{h}

over the whole space
\mathrm{H}_{0}
even if the eigenvalue is simple, but
in this case we just take it to be the identity.

In the next subsection, we are going to prove the 10 hypotheses
\bf(P^{1}_{m})-(P^{10}_{m})
assuming
\bf P_{i-1}
for
i\leq m
.

8.7.
Induction: Round m.

For
m\geq 3
, we assume that we have finished round
m-1
in the induction process. For the round
m
, as before it includes three steps by considering terms in the kinetic, viscous boundary layers and interior alternatively.

Step 1: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m-2})
in the kinetic boundary layer.

The order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m-2})
of the kinetic boundary layer in the ansatz gives that
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}
satisfies the linear boundary layer equation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{BL}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}

\displaystyle=S^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}\,,\quad\mbox{in}\quad\xi>0\,,

(8.86)

\displaystyle g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\xi\rightarrow\infty\,,

with boundary condition at
\xi=0

L^{\mathcal{R}}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}=H^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\xi=0\,,\quad v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0\,,

(8.87)

The source term

\displaystyle S^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}

\displaystyle=\left\{v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\partial_{\pi^{\alpha}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\right\}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m-2}-\sum^{m-3}_{j=1}i\lambda^{k}_{j}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m-2-j}

\displaystyle=\sum^{m}_{j=3}s^{\mathrm{bb}}_{j}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})\,,

where for
3\leq j\leq m-3
,

s^{\mathrm{bb}}_{j}(U^{\mathrm{int}})=\left\{v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi\partial_{\!\pi}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\right\}K_{j-2}(U^{\mathrm{int}})-\sum^{j-3}_{h=1}i\lambda^{k}_{h}K_{j-2-h}(U^{\mathrm{int}})\,,

(8.88)

recalling the functions
K_{j}(U^{\mathrm{int}})
are defined through (
8.81
). The boundary source term is

\displaystyle H^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}

\displaystyle=-L^{\mathcal{R}}\tilde{g}^{k}_{m}+L^{\mathcal{D}}(\tilde{g}^{k}_{m-1}+g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m-1})

(8.89)

\displaystyle=\sum^{m}_{j=0}\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{j}(\widetilde{U}^{k}_{m-j})=\sum^{m}_{j=1}h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{j}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})\,.

Here

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{j}(\widetilde{U}^{k}_{0})=

\displaystyle-L^{\mathcal{R}}\left\{I_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})+B_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\right\}

\displaystyle+L^{\mathcal{D}}\left\{I_{j-1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})+B_{j-1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})+K_{j-1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\right\}\,.

The definition of
h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{j}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})
is the following: represent all
I_{0}\,,\cdots\,,I_{m}
and
B_{0}\,,\cdots\,,B_{m}
in terms of
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,\cdots\,,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1}
, and collect the corresponding terms in
\sum^{m}_{j=0}\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{bb}}_{j}(\widetilde{U}^{k}_{m-j})
, which defines
h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{j}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})
for
j=1\,,\cdots\,,m-1
. Note that there is no
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}
term, since it
formally
only appears in the term
-L^{\mathcal{R}}(I_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m})+B_{0}(U^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}))=-2(v\cdot\mathrm{n})(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}+\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m})\cdot\mathrm{n}
, which only depends on
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\,,\cdots\,,P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1}
, see
\bf(P^{4}_{m-1})
.

The formulas (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) give the boundary conditions

[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}]^{\mathrm{tan}}+[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1}]^{\mathrm{tan}}=\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}V^{\mathrm{u}}_{j}(P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})\,,

(8.90)

and

\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1}=\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}V^{\theta}_{j}(P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})\,,

(8.91)

where

\displaystyle\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}V^{\mathrm{u}}_{j}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})=-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}[2d(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-2})\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}]^{\mathrm{tan}}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\nabla_{\!\pi}[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-2}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}]

(8.92)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}\left[L^{\mathcal{D}}\left\{B_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1-j})+K_{j}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})\right\}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})v\right]^{\mathrm{tan}}M\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\chi}\left\langle(v\cdot\mathrm{n})v\{\sum^{m}_{j=4}I_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})+\sum^{m}_{j=3}B_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-j})\}\right\rangle^{\mathrm{tan}}+\tfrac{1}{\chi}\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle vS^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}\rangle^{\mathrm{tan}}\mathrm{d}\xi\,,

and

\displaystyle\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}V^{\theta}_{j}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})=-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\mathrm{n}}\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-2}+\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{2({\mathrm{D}}+1)}\tilde{\mathrm{u}}^{k}_{m-1}\cdot\mathrm{n}

(8.93)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}\int_{v\cdot\mathrm{n}>0}\left[L^{\mathcal{D}}\left\{B_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1-j})+K_{j}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})\right\}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})|v|^{2}\right]^{\mathrm{tan}}M\,\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{({\mathrm{D}}+1)\chi}\left\langle(v\cdot\mathrm{n})|v|^{2}[\sum^{m}_{j=4}I_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})+\sum^{m}_{j=3}B_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-j})]\right\rangle^{\mathrm{tan}}+\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{1}{\chi}\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1)S^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}\rangle^{\mathrm{tan}}\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

If we express in (
8.92
) and (
8.93
) all
U^{\mathrm{int}}
and
U^{\mathrm{b}}
in terms of
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{1}\,,\cdots\,,P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-2}
, and collect the corresponding terms, then we can define
V^{\mathrm{u}}_{m-1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
and
V^{\theta}_{m-1}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
. Note that
V^{\mathrm{u}}_{1}\,,\cdots\,,V^{\mathrm{u}}_{m-2}
and
V^{\theta}_{1}\,,\cdots\,,V^{\theta}_{m-2}
have been defined in the previous rounds of the induction.

Furthermore, the formula (
6.8
) gives the boundary condition on the normal direction:

[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}+\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}]\cdot\mathrm{n}=\sum^{m}_{j=3}V^{\mathrm{N}}_{j}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\,,

(8.94)

where
