V^{\mathrm{N}}_{m}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})=\int^{\infty}_{0}\left\langle\{v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\partial_{\!\pi^{\alpha}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\}K_{m-2}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\xi-\sum^{m-3}_{j-1}\int^{\infty}_{0}\langle i\lambda^{k}_{j}K_{m-2-j}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

Step 2: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m-1})
in the viscous boundary layer.

The equations of
[\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}]^{\mathrm{tan}}
and

\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}
can be derived by considering the order

O(\varepsilon^{m-1})
of the viscous boundary layer:

{\mathcal{L}}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m+1}=v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!\!x}\mathrm{d}\partial_{\!\zeta}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}+v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}{\partial}_{\pi^{\alpha}}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}-\sum^{m-1}_{j=0}i\lambda^{k}_{j}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1-j}\,,

(8.95)

the solvability of which is the following system of ODEs:

-{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}}U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}=\sum^{m-1}_{j=0}(\mathcal{F}_{j}-i\lambda^{k}_{j})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1-j}\,,

(8.96)

where
\mathcal{F}_{m-1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})
is defined by

\mathcal{P}\left\{v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\partial_{\!\zeta}B_{m}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})+v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi^{\alpha}\partial_{\!\pi^{\alpha}}B_{m-1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\right\}=(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})\mathcal{F}_{m-1}(U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0})\,.

(8.97)

Note that the linear operator
\mathcal{F}_{1}\,,\cdots\,,\mathcal{F}_{m-2}
have been defined in the previous rounds of the induction. In particular,
\mathcal{F}_{0}={\mathcal{A}}^{\pi}+\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{d}}
.

Projecting the system (
8.96
) on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})^{\perp}
, the
\mathrm{u}\mbox{-}
component and
\rho\mbox{-}
component of which give the equations of
\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}
and
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}
respectively:

-\partial_{\!\zeta}(\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m})=\left\{\nu(2-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}})\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\right\}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})+\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}\left\{(\mathrm{I}-\Pi^{\mathrm{d}})(\mathcal{F}_{j}-i\lambda^{k}_{j})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1-j}\right\}^{\mathrm{u}}\,,

(8.98)

and

\displaystyle-\partial_{\!\zeta}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d})

(8.99)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}(\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi)+\kappa\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}(\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1})+\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}\left\{(\mathrm{I}-\Pi^{\mathrm{d}})(\mathcal{F}_{j}-i\lambda^{k}_{j})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1-j}\right\}^{\rho}\,.

Here we use the notation for a vector
V=(V^{\rho}\,,V^{\mathrm{u}}\,,V^{\theta})^{\top}\,.
Integrating (
8.98
) from
\zeta
to
\infty
gives

\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}=\sum^{m}_{j=2}Y^{\mathrm{b}}_{j}(\zeta\,,P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})\,,

(8.100)

in which the linear operator
Y^{\mathrm{b}}_{2}\,,\cdots\,,Y^{\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}
have been defined in the previous rounds of the induction. This corresponds to
\bf(P^{1}_{m})
.

Next projecting the system (
8.96
) on Null
({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{d}})
, the
\mathrm{u}\mbox{-}
component of which gives that
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\pi
satisfies the ODE

\displaystyle\left\{\nu\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\right\}\mathrm{u}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\pi}(\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1})-\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}\left\{\Pi^{\mathrm{d}}(\mathcal{F}_{j}-i\lambda^{k}_{j})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1-j}\right\}^{\mathrm{u}}\,,

(8.101)

\displaystyle[\mathrm{u}-\tfrac{\nu}{\chi}\partial_{\zeta}\mathrm{u}](\zeta=0)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi+\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}V^{\mathrm{u}}_{j}(P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})\,,

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{u}

\displaystyle=0\,,

the solution of which is

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi=\sum^{m-1}_{j=0}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{j}(\zeta\,,P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})\,,

(8.102)

where the linear operator
\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{0}\,,\cdots\,,\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\mathrm{u}}_{m-2}
have been defined in the previous rounds of the induction. This corresponds to
\bf(P^{2}_{m})
.

Projecting the system (
8.96
) on Null
({\mathcal{A}})
gives that
\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}
satisfies the ODE

\displaystyle\left\{\kappa\partial^{2}_{\zeta\zeta}-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\right\}\theta

\displaystyle=-i\lambda^{k}_{0}\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}(\rho^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}+\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1})-\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}\left\{\Pi^{\mathrm{d}}(\mathcal{F}_{j}-i\lambda^{k}_{j})U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1-j}\right\}^{\theta}\,,

(8.103)

\displaystyle[\theta-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{{\mathrm{D}}+1}\tfrac{\kappa}{\chi}\partial_{\zeta}\theta](\zeta=0)

\displaystyle=-\theta^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1}+\sum^{m-1}_{j=1}V^{\theta}_{j}(P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})\,,

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta\rightarrow\infty}\theta

\displaystyle=0\,,

the solution of which is

\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m-1}=\sum^{m-1}_{j=0}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{j}(\zeta\,,P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-1-j})\,,

(8.104)

where the linear operator
\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{0}\,,\cdots\,,\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b},\theta}_{m-2}
have been defined in the previous rounds of the induction. This corresponds to
\bf(P^{3}_{m})
.

Having (
8.102
) and (
8.104
), go back to (
8.99
) and integrate from
\zeta
to
\infty
, we obtain

\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}=\sum^{m}_{j=1}\widetilde{Z}^{\mathrm{b}}_{j}(\zeta\,,P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})\,.

(8.105)

In particular, by taking
\zeta=0
in (
8.105
), we obtain

-\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m}\cdot\mathrm{n}=\sum^{m}_{j=1}Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{j}(P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})\,,

(8.106)

Thus from (
8.106
) and (
8.94
) we derive the boundary condition of
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}\cdot\mathrm{n}
which will be used in the next step to solve
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}
. This corresponds to
\bf(P^{4}_{m})
.

Under these conditions, the equation (
8.95
) can be solved as
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{m+1}=\sum^{m+1}_{h=0}B_{h}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{j-h})\,,
where
B_{h}(U^{\mathrm{int}})
is defined in (
8.80
). Note that
B_{0}\,,B_{1}\,,\cdots\,,B_{m}
have been determined in the previous rounds of the induction. This corresponds to
\bf(P^{5}_{m})
.

Finally we go back to the kinetic boundary equation (
8.86
) to solve
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}
as

g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{m}=\sum^{m}_{j=1}K_{j}(v,\xi,P_{0}U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})\,,

(8.107)

where the linear operator
K_{m}(v,\xi,U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
is the solution to the linear kinetic boundary layer equation (
6.3
)-(
6.4
) with the source term
s^{\mathrm{bb}}_{m}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
and the boundary source term
h^{\mathrm{bb}}_{m}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
. Note that
K_{1}\,,\cdots\,,K_{m-1}
have been defined in the previous rounds of the induction. This corresponds to
\bf(P^{6}_{m})
. Thus we finish
Step 2
.

Step 3: Order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m})
in the interior.

The order
O(\varepsilon^{\frac{m}{2}})
in the interior part of the ansatz yields

{\mathcal{L}}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m+2}=v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}-\sum^{m}_{j=0}i\lambda^{k}_{j,0}g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-j}\,,

(8.108)

and the solvability condition of which is

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}

\displaystyle=\sum^{m}_{j=1}(i\lambda^{k}_{j}-\mathcal{G}_{j})U^{k}_{m-j}\,,\quad\mbox{in}\quad\!\Omega\,,

(8.109)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}\cdot\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=\sum^{m}_{j=1}(Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{j}+V^{\mathrm{N}}_{j})(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-j})\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\,,

where the vector-valued linear operator
\mathcal{G}_{j}
for
j\geq 4
is defined as

(1,v,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})\mathcal{G}_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})=\mathcal{P}\left\{v\cdot\nabla_{\!x}I_{j}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\right\}\,.

(8.110)

Note that
\mathcal{G}_{1}=0
,
\mathcal{G}_{2}=\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{G}_{3}=0
and
V^{\mathrm{N}}_{j}=0
for
j=1,2
.

Applying Lemma
5.1
to (
8.109
), and recalling in (
8.82
) the definition of
Q_{j}
for
j=1\,,2\,,\cdots\,,m-1
, the formula (
5.20
) gives

\displaystyle i\lambda^{k}_{m}=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2{\mathrm{D}}}}\int_{{\partial\Omega}}(Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}+V^{\mathrm{n}}_{m})(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\Psi^{k}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{\!x}+\sum^{m}_{j=2}\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-j}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}))|U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\right\rangle

(8.111)

\displaystyle+\sum^{m-1}_{h}Q_{h}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-h}\,,\Psi^{k})\,.

The orthogonality condition (
8.85
) implies that the second line of (
8.111
) vanishes. Thus we can define the righthand side of the first line of (
8.111
) as
Q_{m}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{k})
. Thus we verify that
i\lambda_{m}=Q_{m}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{k})
which is completely determined. This corresponds to
\bf(P^{7}_{m})
.

To solve the equation (
8.109
), we need to consider
m+1
cases:

Case 1:

i\lambda^{k}_{h}
is a simple eigenvalue of
L_{h}
for
0\leq h\leq m-1
. No orthogonality condition is needed, and every term is fully determined;

Case j (
2\leq j\leq m+1
):

i\lambda^{k}_{h}
is a multiple eigenvalue of
L_{h}
for
0\leq h\leq j-2
, and a simple eigenvalue of
L_{h}
for
j-1\leq h\leq m-1
.

We only consider the
case m+1
here, i.e. all the eigenvalues
i\lambda^{k}_{h}
are multiple. The other cases are simpler. Taking the inner product with
U^{l,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
, for
l\neq k
,
\lambda^{l}_{0}=\lambda^{k}_{0}
, which is

\sum^{m-1}_{h=1}i\lambda^{k}_{h}a^{kl}_{m-h}=\sum^{m-1}_{h=1}Q_{h}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-h},\Psi^{l})+Q_{m}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l})\,.

(8.112)

If
\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathrm{H}_{2}(\lambda_{2})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{m-1}(\lambda_{m-1})
, then because of the orthogonality condition (
8.84
) for
1\leq h\leq m-2
,

\displaystyle Q_{h}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-h},\Psi^{l})

\displaystyle=Q_{h}(P_{h-1}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-h},\Psi^{l})+Q_{h}(\sum^{h-1}_{\delta=1}P^{\perp}_{\delta}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-h},\Psi^{l})

\displaystyle=i\lambda^{l}_{h}a^{kl}_{m-h}\,.

For
h=m-1
,
Q_{m-1}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1},\Psi^{l})=i\lambda^{l}_{m-2}a^{kl}_{1}+Q_{m-1}(P^{\perp}_{m-2}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1},\Psi^{l})
. Thus, the identity (
8.112
) implies that we need the orthogonality condition that for
k\neq l
,

Q_{m}(\Psi^{k}\,,\Psi^{l})=\int_{\Omega}L_{m}(\Psi^{k})\Psi^{l}\,\mathrm{d}x=0\,,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\!\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{1}\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{m-1}\,,

(8.113)

where the symmetric operator
L_{m}
is defined by
L_{m}\Psi^{l}=i\lambda^{l}_{m}\Psi^{l}
, for
\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{1}\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{m-1}\,.

If
\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathrm{H}_{2}(\lambda_{2})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{m-2}(\lambda_{m-2})\cap\mathrm{H}^{\perp}_{m-1}(\lambda_{m-1})
, i.e
\lambda^{k}_{h}=\lambda^{l}_{h}
for
0\leq h\leq m-2
, but
\lambda^{k}_{m-1}\neq\lambda^{l}_{m-1}
, from the identity (
8.112
), for these
k,l
,
a^{kl}_{1}
can be determined by

a^{kl}_{1}=\tfrac{1}{i\lambda^{k}_{m-1}-i\lambda^{l}_{m-1}}Q_{m}(\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l})\,.

This means that
(P^{\perp}_{0}+P^{\perp}_{1}+\cdots+P^{\perp}_{m-1})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}
is completely determined, but
P_{m-1}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}
is still left as undetermined.

If
\Psi^{k},\Psi^{l}\in\mathrm{H}_{1}\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{m-3}\cap\mathrm{H}^{\perp}_{m-2}
,

Q_{m-1}(P^{\perp}_{m-1}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}\,,\Psi^{l})+Q_{m}(\Psi^{k}\,,\Psi^{l})\\
=(i\lambda^{k}_{m-2}-i\lambda^{l}_{m-2})a^{kl}_{2}+i\lambda^{k}_{m-1}a^{kl}_{1}\,,

(8.114)

from which
a^{kl}_{2}
thus
P^{\perp}_{m-2}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{2}
is completely determined.

Under these solvability conditions, the equation (
8.109
) can be solved as

U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}=P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}+\sum^{m}_{h=1}Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{h}(P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-h})\,,

where
Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{m}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})
is the solution to the following equation:

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{k}_{0})U

\displaystyle=\sum^{m}_{h=1}(i\lambda^{k}_{h}-\mathcal{G}_{h})Z^{\mathrm{int}}_{m-h}(U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0})\,,\quad\mbox{in}\quad\!\Omega\,,

(8.115)

\displaystyle\mathrm{u}\cdot\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=(Z^{\mathrm{b}}_{m}+V^{\mathrm{N}}_{m})U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\,,\quad\mbox{on}\quad\!{\partial\Omega}\,.

Thus,
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}
is determined modulo
P_{0}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m}\,,P_{1}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m-1}\,,\cdots\,,P_{m-1}U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{1}

which are undetermined at this stage. Under these conditions, the equation (
8.108
) is solved as
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{m+2}=I_{0}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m+2})+I_{2}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m})+\sum^{m+2}_{h=4}I_{m+2}(U^{\mathrm{int}}_{m+2-h})
This corresponds to
\bf(P^{8}_{m})
,
\bf(P^{9}_{m})
and
\bf(P^{10}_{m})
.

We can now inductively continue the process, namely
go to the order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m-1})
of the kinetic boundary layer, the order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m})
of the viscous boundary layer, then the order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{m+1})
of the interior, and so on. We should do this at least till the order
N+2
where
N
is the precision of the error in (
7.10
). Note however, that for a given
\lambda=\lambda^{k}_{0}
, we may only need to construct a small
number of the
L_{j}
if after few steps all the eigenvalues become simple, namely if for some
j
all the eigenvalues of
L_{j}
are simple on the
space
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\lambda_{1})\cap\cdots\cap\mathrm{H}_{j-1}(\lambda_{j-1})
.
It is clear that if the eigenvalues become simple for some
j\leq N+2
, then
the orthogonality condition (
8.84
) allows to determine
the eigenfunctions
\Psi^{k}
uniquely.
If the process does not end, then we just need to satisfy the condition
till the order
N+2
which yield a non-unique choice of eigenfunctions.
Also, in this case, we set all the undetermined pieces of the eigenfunction,
namely those left undetermined to be zero.

9.
Proof of Proposition
7.1
: Truncation Error Estimates

In the previous sections, we construct the kinetic-fluid boundary
layers up to any order for
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{2\pi}\chi\varepsilon^{\beta}
. Now we define the approximated eigenfunction and
eigenvalues
g^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}
and

\lambda^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}
by truncation in the corresponding
ansatzs. More specifically,

\displaystyle g^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}

\displaystyle=\sum^{N}_{j=0}\left\{g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j}+g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}\right\}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}+\sum^{N}_{j=1}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{j}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\lambda^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}

\displaystyle=\sum^{N}_{j=0}\lambda^{k}_{j}\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{j}\,.

9.1.
Estimates of
R^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}

Using the eigen-equation (
7.4
), we can easily find that
the error term
R^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}
has the form of

\displaystyle R^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}=

\displaystyle\{(i\lambda^{k}_{0}-v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x})g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{N-1}+(i\lambda^{k}_{0}-v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\pi)\partial_{\pi}[g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{N-1}+g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{N-1}]-v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\partial_{\!\zeta}g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{N}

\displaystyle+\sum^{N-1}_{j=1}i\lambda^{k}_{j}\hat{g}^{k}_{N-1-j}\}\varepsilon^{\frac{N-1}{2}}+``\mbox{higher order terms}"\,,

where
\hat{g}^{k}=g^{k,\mathrm{int}}+g^{k,\mathrm{b}}+g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}
. From the constructions of
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}
,
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}
and
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}
, it is easy to know that

\|g^{k}_{j}\|_{L^{r}(\mathrm{d}x\,;L^{p}(aM\,\mathrm{d}v))}\leq C\,,

for all
j
, and
1<r,p<\infty
, where
g^{k}
stands for
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}
,
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}
or
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}
.

Indeed, both the hydrodynamic and the kinetic parts of
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}
and
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}
have coefficients in terms of the components of
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}
and
U^{k,\mathrm{b}}
. From Lemma
5.1
, the solutions
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{j}
of the equation (
5.16
) can be represented linearly in terms of components of
U^{\mathrm{int}}_{i}
for
0\leq i<j
and the boundary terms of
U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{i}
and
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{i}
for
0\leq i<j
. Note that the pseudo inverse operator
({\mathcal{A}}-i\lambda^{\tau,k})^{-1}
is bounded, and furthermore, the boundary values of
U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{i}
and
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{i}
are linearly in terms of
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{l}
for
0\leq l\leq i
. For
U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{j}
, their components are solutions of second order ordinary differential equations with boundary conditions in terms of
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{i}
and
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq j
. Moreover, the solutions of the linear kinetic boundary layer equation (
6.3
) for
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{i}
are bounded in
L^{r}(\mathrm{d}x,L^{p}(aM\mathrm{d}v))
in terms of
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{l}
and
U^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{l}
for
0\leq l<i
. So all
g^{k,\mathrm{int}}
,
g^{k,\mathrm{b}}
or
g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}
are linearly depends on components of
U^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
, i.e.
\Psi^{k}
and
\nabla_{\!x}\Psi^{k}
which is the eigenfunctions of
-\Delta_{\!x}
with Neumann boundary conditions. From the basic regularity theory of elliptic operator, they are bounded in
L^{r}(\mathrm{d}x;\Omega)
for any
1<r\leq\infty
.

For the term
(v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}){\partial}_{\zeta}g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{N}
,
we integrate over
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
and use simple change of
variable
(y_{1}\,,y_{2}\,,\cdots\,,y_{{\mathrm{D}}-1})=\pi(x)\,,y_{{\mathrm{D}}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
, we can have extra

\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, so all will be in the higher order terms.
Thus, we have the error estimate (
7.10
).

9.2.
Estimates of
g^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}-g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}

The leading order term of
g^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}-g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
is

g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
, so using the expressions above for
\mathrm{u}^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\,,\theta^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}
and a simple change of variable
which will give an extra
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\|g^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}-g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\|_{L^{r}(\mathrm{d}x,L^{p}(aM\mathrm{d}v))}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2r}}\,.

Thus we get (
7.11
).

9.3.
Boundary error estimate

Finally, the boundary error term
r^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}
is

r^{k}_{\varepsilon,N}=-\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{N+1}L^{\mathcal{D}}(g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{N}+g^{k,\mathrm{b}}_{N}+g^{k,\mathrm{bb}}_{N})

from which we can get the estimate (
7.12
). Thus we
finish the proof of the Proposition
7.1
.

10.
Proof of the Weak Convergence in Theorem
3.1
and
3.2

In order to derive the fluid equation with the boundary conditions, we
need to pass to the limit in approximate local conservation laws
built from the renormalized Boltzmann equation
(
2.3
)
. We
choose the renormalization used in
[
27
]
:

\Gamma(Z)=\frac{Z-1}{1+(Z-1)^{2}}\,.

(10.1)

After multiplying
\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})
and dividing by
\varepsilon
, equation
(
2.19
)
becomes

{\partial}_{t}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})\iint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}q_{\varepsilon}b(\omega\,,v_{1}-v)\,\mathrm{d}\omega M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,,

(10.2)

where
\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}=\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\Gamma({G_{\epsilon}})
can be considered as the
L^{2}
part of the fluctuations
g_{\varepsilon}
and
q_{\varepsilon}
is the scaled collision integrand defined as

q_{\varepsilon}=\frac{G^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}G^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}-G_{\varepsilon 1}G_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\,.

(10.3)

By introducing
N_{\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon^{2}g^{2}_{\varepsilon}
, we can write

\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}=\frac{g_{\varepsilon}}{N_{\varepsilon}}\,,\quad\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})=\frac{2}{N^{2}_{\varepsilon}}-\frac{1}{N_{\varepsilon}}\,.

When moments of the renormalized Boltzmann equation

(
10.2
)
are formally taken with respect to any

\zeta\in\mbox{span}\{1\,,v_{1}\,,\cdots\,,v_{\mathrm{D}}\,,|v|^{2}\}
, one obtains the local conservation laws with defects

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\partial}_{t}\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\!x}\!\cdot\!\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\langle\!\left\langle\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})q_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\!\right\rangle\,,

(10.4)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\partial}_{t}\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\!x}(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}+\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\!x}\!\cdot\!\langle\mathrm{A}(v)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\langle\!\left\langle v\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})q_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\!\right\rangle\,,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\partial}_{t}\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\nabla_{\!x}\!\cdot\!\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\!x}\!\cdot\!\langle\mathrm{B}(v)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\langle\!\!\left\langle\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}}-1\right)\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})q_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\!\!\right\rangle\,,

which can be written as

{\partial}_{t}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{A}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}+\widetilde{Q}_{\varepsilon}=\widetilde{R}_{\varepsilon}\,,

(10.5)

where

\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}=(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\,,\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\,,\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon})=(\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\,,\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\,,\langle(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}}-1)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle)\,,

\widetilde{Q}_{\varepsilon}=\left(0\,,\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\!x}\!\cdot\!\langle\mathrm{A}(v)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\,,\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\!x}\!\cdot\!\langle\mathrm{B}(v)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\right)\,,

and the local conservation defect

\widetilde{R}_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\langle\!\!\!\left\langle(1\,,v\,,\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}}-1)\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})q_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\!\!\!\right\rangle\,.

Notice that we do not know if
\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{n}=0
, so
\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
is not necessary in the domain of
{\mathcal{A}}
for every
\varepsilon>0
, thus the notation
\mathcal{A}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
in (
10.5
) is not quite rigorous. However we can show that the weak limit of
\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}
, say,
\mathrm{u}
, satisfies
\mathrm{u}\cdot\mathrm{n}=0
on the boundary, also see
[
23
]
. From the local conservation laws with defect (
10.5
), formally the limit of
\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
will be in the null space of the acoustic operator
{\mathcal{A}}
. In other words, any weak limits of
(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\,,\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\,,\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon})
will satisfy the incompressibility
\nabla_{\!x}\!\cdot\!\tilde{\mathrm{u}}=0
and Boussinesq relation
\tilde{\rho}+\tilde{\theta}=0
.

The term
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{A}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
in
(
10.5
) describes the acoustic waves with propagation speed

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
. As
\varepsilon
goes to zero, the sound waves
propagate faster and faster to make the fluid limit singular. To
derive the incompressible fluid equations, a natural way is to
project the local conservation laws (
10.5
) onto
Null
(\mathcal{A})
and Null
(\mathcal{A})^{\perp}
respectively. First
\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
can be orthogonally decomposed as

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\Pi\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}+\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}

(10.6)

\displaystyle=\left(\langle(1-\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2})\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\,,\mathbb{P}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\,,\langle(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\langle\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\,,\mathbb{Q}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\,,\langle\tfrac{2|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}({\mathrm{D}}+2)}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\right)\,,

in which we call
\Pi\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
and
\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
the incompressible and acoustic parts of
\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
respectively.

By definition of Leray projection in a bounded domain (
5.1
), the boundary conditions of
\mathbb{P}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle

and
\mathbb{Q}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle
are

\mathbb{P}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\cdot\mathrm{n}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbb{Q}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\cdot\mathrm{n}=\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{n}\quad\mbox{on}\quad{\partial\Omega}\,.

To derive the weak form of the evolution equations of

\Pi\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
, we take the test function
Y
in
(
2.24
) as special infinitesimal Maxwellian in the
incompressible mode:

Y^{incom}(x\,,v)=-\chi+w\!\cdot\!v+\chi\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2}\right)\,,

where
(\chi\,,w)\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}\,,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{D}}\times\mathbb{R})
with

\nabla_{\!x}\!\cdot\!w=0
in
\Omega
and
w\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=0
on
{\partial\Omega}
.
Because
\chi
and
w
are independent, the weak form of
(
10.5
) can be written separately as:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Omega}\mathbb{P}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t_{2})\rangle\cdot w\,\mathrm{d}x-\int\limits_{\Omega}\mathbb{P}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t_{1})\rangle\cdot w\,\mathrm{d}x

(10.7)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\mathrm{A}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle:\nabla_{\!x}w\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int\limits_{\partial\Omega}\langle\gamma\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(w\!\cdot\!v)\rangle_{\partial\Omega}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int\limits_{\Omega}w\!\cdot\!\left\langle\!\left\langle v\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})q_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\!\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,,

and

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2}\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\langle\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1\right)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t_{2})\right\rangle\chi\,\mathrm{d}x-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2}\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\langle\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1\right)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t_{1})\right\rangle\chi\,\mathrm{d}x

(10.8)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\mathrm{B}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{\!x}\chi\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int\limits_{\partial\Omega}\left\langle\chi\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1\right)\gamma\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\right\rangle_{\partial\Omega}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int\limits_{\Omega}\chi\left\langle\!\!\left\langle\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1\right)\Gamma^{\prime}({G_{\epsilon}})q_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\!\!\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,.

Identities (
10.7
) and
(
10.8
) are the local conservation laws in
the incompressible modes. It is the starting point of the proof of
the weak convergence to the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations
with boundary conditions in the Main Theorems
3.1
and

3.2
. It has been proved in
[
27
]
the convergence
of the interior terms of (
10.7
) and
(
10.8
) as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
to recover
the weak form of incompressible Navier-Stokes equations. It is only
left to derive the boundary conditions of the limiting equations.

The strategy to recover the boundary conditions in the limit is
basically the same as
[
33
]
except some necessary modifications. For the convenience of the readers, and also because we work in more general collision kernels, we briefly go through the proof here. In
[
27
]
, the author proved
that inside the domain
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}
, the
family of fluctuations
g_{\varepsilon}
is relatively compact in

w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
, and that every
limit point
g
has the form (
3.4
). Lemma 5.1 of

[
33
]
showed that the trace of the limit point
\gamma g

belongs to
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}))
and satisfies

\gamma g=v\!\cdot\!\gamma\mathrm{u}+\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2}\right)\gamma\theta\,,

(10.9)

where
\gamma\mathrm{u}
and
\gamma\theta
denote the fluid traces of
\mathrm{u}

and
\theta
.

We list some key
a priori
estimates from
[
33
]
on

\gamma g_{\varepsilon}
. The first one is from the inside, we generalize
it to the more general collision kernel case considered in this
paper.

Lemma 10.1
.

For all
p>0
, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
,

\gamma\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow\gamma g\quad\!\mbox{in}\quad\!w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(M(1+|v|^{p})|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}))\,.

(10.10)

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as Lemma 5.2 of

[
33
]
, except for some new argument to treat the soft
potential collision kernel case. First, using the function

\Gamma(Z)=\left(\frac{Z-1}{1+(Z-1)^{2}}\right)^{5/3}

in the renormalized formulation (
2.22
) gives

(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x})\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}^{5/3}=\frac{5}{3}\iint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}^{2/3}q_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{2}{N^{2}_{\varepsilon}}-\frac{1}{N_{\varepsilon}}\right)b(\omega,v-v_{1})\mathrm{d}\omega M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,.

(10.11)

To estimate the right-hand side in (
10.11
), we apply the
classical Young’s inequality, namely,

pz\leq r^{*}(p)+r(z)\,,

for every
p
and
z
in the domains of
r^{*}
and
r
. Here the
function
r
is defined over
z>-1
by
r(z)=z\log(1+z)
which
is strictly convex, and
r^{*}
is the Legendre dual of
r
.

\displaystyle\left|\frac{q_{\varepsilon}}{N_{\varepsilon}^{2}}|\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}|^{2/3}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}{G_{\epsilon}}G_{\varepsilon 1}r\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}q_{\varepsilon}}{{G_{\epsilon}}G_{\varepsilon 1}}\right)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}{G_{\epsilon}}G_{\varepsilon 1}r^{*}\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}^{2/3}}{N^{2}_{\varepsilon}}\right)

(10.12)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}{G_{\epsilon}}G_{\varepsilon 1}r\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}q_{\varepsilon}}{{G_{\epsilon}}G_{\varepsilon 1}}\right)+{G_{\epsilon}}G_{\varepsilon 1}\frac{|\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}|^{4/3}}{N^{4}_{\varepsilon}}r^{*}(1)\,,

The second inequality above used the superquadratic homogeneity of

r^{*}
. By the entropy dissipation rate bound, the first term on the
right-hand side of (
10.12
) is bounded in

L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t\,,L^{1}(d\nu\,\mathrm{d}x))
. Since

N_{\varepsilon}\geq 1
and
{G_{\epsilon}}\leq\sqrt{2N_{\varepsilon}}
, the integral of the
second term can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}|\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}|^{4/3}G_{\varepsilon 1}\frac{\overline{b}(v_{1}-v)}{a(v_{1})a(v)}a_{1}M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}aM\mathrm{d}v

(10.13)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\frac{|\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}|^{4/3}}{N^{3}_{\varepsilon}\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon}}}\left(1+\varepsilon|\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1}|\right)\frac{\overline{b}(v_{1}-v)}{a(v_{1})a(v)}a_{1}M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}aM\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}|\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}|^{4/3}\varepsilon|g_{\varepsilon 1}-\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1}|\frac{\overline{b}(v_{1}-v)}{a(v_{1})a(v)}a_{1}M_{1}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}aM\mathrm{d}v\,.

Using the
assumption 3
, namely (
2.7
) and
|\varepsilon\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
, the first term in (
10.13
) is bounded.
Indeed, it is bounded by

C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\frac{|\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}|^{4/3}}{N^{3}_{\varepsilon}\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon}}}aM\,\mathrm{d}v\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\frac{g^{2}_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon}}}aM\,\mathrm{d}v\leq C\,.

The second term in (
10.13
) is bounded as

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\iint\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}|\varepsilon\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}|^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}\frac{|\varepsilon g_{\varepsilon 1}|}{\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon 1}}}\frac{g^{2}_{\varepsilon 1}}{\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon 1}}}\frac{\overline{b}(v_{1}-v)}{a(v_{1})a(v)}aM\mathrm{d}v\,a_{1}M_{1}\mathrm{d}v_{1}

(10.14)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2/3}2\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{4/3}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}}\frac{g^{2}_{\varepsilon 1}}{\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon 1}}}a_{1}M_{1}\mathrm{d}v_{1}\,.

Since the righthand side of (
10.14
) vanishes as
\varepsilon
goes to zero, we
deduce that
(\varepsilon\partial_{t}+v\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x})\tilde{g}^{5/3}_{\varepsilon}
is
uniformly bounded in

L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t\,,L^{1}(M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
. The rest of
the proof of (
10.10
) is the same as that of Lemma 5.2 in

[
33
]
.

∎

The next lemma is the
a priori
estimate of
\gamma g_{\varepsilon}

from the boundary term in the entropy inequality
(
2.27
). The proof is the same as Lemma 6.1
in
[
33
]
with some trivial modification. So we just state the
lemma without giving the proof.

Lemma 10.2
.

Define
\gamma_{\varepsilon}=\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}-\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}
and

\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{\gamma_{\!+}G_{\varepsilon}\leq 2\langle G_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}}\leq 4\gamma_{\!+}G_{\varepsilon}}\,,\quad\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}-\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,.

(10.15)

Then each of these is bounded as follows:

\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}}\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}}{[1+\varepsilon^{2}(\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon})^{2}]^{\frac{1}{4}}}\quad\mbox{in}\quad L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}))\,,

(10.16)

\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}}\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}}{[1+\varepsilon^{2}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}^{2}]^{\frac{1}{4}}}\quad\mbox{in}\quad L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}))\,,

(10.17)

\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\quad\mbox{in}\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}))\,.

(10.18)

Using Lemma
10.1
and Lemma
10.2
, we can prove the
following lemma which describes how to define the renormalized
outgoing mass flux
\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\rho
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Assume that
\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon}\rightarrow\chi\in(0\,,+\infty]
. Then up to the extraction of a subsequence,

\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle^{2}_{\partial\Omega}}

converge in
w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
and have the same weak
limit. Moreover, there exists
\rho\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}))
such that, up to the extraction of a subsequence,

\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\rho\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))\,.

Furthermore,

\rho=\langle\gamma_{\!+}g\rangle_{\partial\Omega}\,.

Lemma
10.3
is nothing but Lemma 6.2 and Lemma 6.3 in
[
33
]
.
The proof is basically the same except some trivial modifications
because we use some different renormalizations. Thus we skip the proof here.

Now it is ready to recover the Dirichlet boundary condition. For the
case
\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\infty
, from (
10.16
) and
(
10.18
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle^{2}_{\partial\Omega}}\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle\quad\mbox{strongly in}\!\!\quad L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x)),

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle^{2}_{\partial\Omega}}\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle\quad\mbox{strongly in}\!\!\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x));

hence, we get

\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle^{2}_{\partial\Omega}}\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{strongly in}\!\!\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))\,.

(10.19)

On the other hand,

\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}^{2}}=\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}-\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\rightarrow\gamma_{\!+}g-\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\rho\,,

(10.20)

in
w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}(x)|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
. Then (
10.19
)
and (
10.20
) imply that

\gamma_{\!+}g=\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\rho

where
\rho
depends only on
(t,x)
. Thus, by (
10.9
) we get the Dirichlet boundary condition

\gamma\mathrm{u}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\gamma\theta=0\,.

Now, we concentrate on the Navier boundary condition case.
Using the previous convergence results, we can take limits in the
conservation laws (
10.7
) and
(
10.8
) to get the weak form of the
boundary conditions. In the weak forms (
10.7
)
and (
10.8
), the limits of the interior
terms have been carried in
[
27
]
, which is
stated in the following lemma:

Lemma 10.4
.

Assume that
\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon}\rightarrow\chi\in[0,\infty)
, then up to the extraction of a sequence,

\mathbb{P}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle
and
\langle(\frac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle

converge to
\mathrm{u}
and
\theta
in
C([0,\infty);w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x))

such that, for all
w\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}})
with

\nabla_{\!\!x}\!\cdot\!w=0
in
\Omega
and
w\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}=0
on
{\partial\Omega}
, for
all
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R})
, and for all

t_{1}\,,t_{2}>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{u}(t_{2})\!\cdot\!w\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{u}(t_{1})\!\cdot\!w\,\mathrm{d}x-\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\sum\limits_{i,j}\mathrm{u}_{i}\mathrm{u}_{j}\partial_{i}w_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(10.21)

\displaystyle+\nu\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\sum\limits_{i,j}(\partial_{i}\mathrm{u}_{j}+\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{i})\partial_{i}w_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\left\langle\frac{\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(w\!\cdot\!v)\mathbf{1}_{|v|^{2}\leq 20|\log\varepsilon|}}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\right\rangle_{\partial\Omega}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\mathrm{d}t\,,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\theta(t_{2})\!\cdot\!\chi\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\theta(t_{1})\!\cdot\!\chi\,\mathrm{d}x-\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\theta\mathrm{u}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\chi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\kappa\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{\!x}\theta\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\chi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

(10.22)

\displaystyle=-\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\left\langle\frac{\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\chi\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{|v|^{2}\leq 20|\log\varepsilon|}\right\rangle_{\partial\Omega}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\mathrm{d}t\,.

where
\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon}=(1-\alpha_{\epsilon})\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}+\alpha_{\epsilon}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}}
.

Proof.

It is the analogue of Lemma 7.1 and Lemma 7.2 of
[
33
]
and the
idea of the proof is the same. Denote by
Y_{\varepsilon}
the test function

(w\!\cdot\!v)\mathbf{1}_{|v|^{2}\leq 20|\log\varepsilon|}
or

\chi(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1)\mathbf{1}_{|v|^{2}\leq 20|\log\varepsilon|}
.
Then
Y_{\varepsilon}
has the property:
Y_{\varepsilon}=LY_{\varepsilon}
, recalling
L
is the local reflection operator defined in (
2.2
). From
(
2.25
), the renormalized form of the Maxwell boundary
condition reads

\gamma_{-}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}=(1-\alpha_{\epsilon})\frac{L\gamma_{+}g_{\epsilon}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}(L\gamma_{+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon})^{2}}+\alpha_{\epsilon}\frac{\langle\gamma_{+}g_{\epsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}(L\gamma_{+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon})^{2}}\,,

(10.23)

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha_{\epsilon})\gamma_{+}g_{\varepsilon}+\alpha_{\epsilon}\langle\gamma_{+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}\,,

\displaystyle=\gamma_{+}g_{\epsilon}-\alpha_{\epsilon}\gamma_{\varepsilon}\,.

Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\langle\gamma\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}Y_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\left\langle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}(\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}^{2}_{\varepsilon}-\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}^{2}_{\varepsilon})}Y_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\right\rangle_{\!\!{\partial\Omega}}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

(10.24)

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\left\langle\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}^{2}_{\varepsilon}}Y_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\right\rangle_{\!\!{\partial\Omega}}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\left\langle\frac{\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}+\gamma^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}^{2}_{\varepsilon})}Y_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\right\rangle_{\!\!{\partial\Omega}}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\left\langle\frac{(\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}+\gamma^{(2)}_{\varepsilon})\varepsilon^{2}(\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon})}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}^{2}_{\varepsilon})}Y_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\right\rangle_{\!\!{\partial\Omega}}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,.

By (
10.18
),

\displaystyle\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\left|\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\int_{{\partial\Omega}}\left\langle\frac{\gamma^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}^{2}_{\varepsilon})}Y_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\right\rangle_{\!\!{\partial\Omega}}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\right|\mathrm{d}t

(10.25)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|\frac{Y_{\varepsilon}}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}^{2}_{\varepsilon})}\right\|_{\infty}\leq C\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|\,.

The
\gamma^{(2)}_{\varepsilon}
part in the last term of (
10.24
) can be estimated as
(
10.25
). For the
\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}
part, from (
10.16
),

\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}}\frac{\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon}}}\quad\mbox{is relatively compact in}\!\!\quad w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(M|v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n}|\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}))

(10.26)

Use the fact that

\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}}\frac{\varepsilon\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon}}(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}^{2}_{\varepsilon})}

(10.27)

is bounded in
L^{\infty}
and goes to 0 a.e. Then by the Product
Limit Theorem of
[
3
]
, the product of (
10.26
) and
(
10.27
) goes to 0 in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)
as

\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Thus we finish the proof of the lemma.
∎

Now, it is ready to recover the Navier boundary condition by taking
limit in the last terms in (
10.21
) and
(
10.22
). As in
[
33
]
, we can deduce that

\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon}\left\langle\frac{\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}(w\!\cdot\!v)\mathbf{1}_{|v|^{2}\leq 20|\log\varepsilon|}}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\right\rangle_{\!\!{\partial\Omega}}\rightarrow\lambda\langle(\gamma_{\!+}g-\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}})(w\!\cdot\!v)\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{\gamma^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g^{2}_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}\hat{g}_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\chi\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1\right)\mathbf{1}_{|v|^{2}\leq 20|\log\varepsilon|}\right\rangle_{\partial\Omega}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\lambda\langle(\gamma_{\!+}g-\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{+}}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}})\chi(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}-1)\rangle_{\partial\Omega}

in
w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}))
. Use
(
10.9
), we finally prove the weak form of the
incompressible Navier-Stokes equations with Navier boundary
conditions:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{u}(t_{2})\!\cdot\!w\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{u}(t_{1})\!\cdot\!w\,\mathrm{d}x-\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\sum\limits_{i,j}\mathrm{u}_{i}\mathrm{u}_{j}\partial_{i}w_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\nu\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\sum\limits_{i,j}(\partial_{i}\mathrm{u}_{j}+\partial_{j}\mathrm{u}_{i})\partial_{i}w_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\lambda\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\gamma\mathrm{u}\!\cdot\!w\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\mathrm{d}t\,,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\theta(t_{2})\!\cdot\!\chi\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\theta(t_{1})\!\cdot\!\chi\,\mathrm{d}x-\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\theta\mathrm{u}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\chi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\kappa\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{\!x}\theta\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\chi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+1}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\alpha\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\gamma\theta\chi\sigma_{x}\mathrm{d}t\,.

Thus we finish the proof of the weak convergence results in the Theorem
3.1
and Theorem
3.2
.

11.
Proof of the Strong Convergence in Theorem
3.1

In the previous section, we proved that the incompressible part of the fluid moments
\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
, i.e.

\Pi\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
converges only
weakly
to solutions of
the incompressible NSF equations. This weak convergence is caused by the persistence of fast acoustic part

\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
, as in the periodic domain

[
27
]
. If
\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
vanishes in some strong sense
as
\varepsilon
goes to zero, we can improve the convergence of
\Pi\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}

from weak to strong. The main novelty of this paper is to prove that in the bounded domain
\Omega
, when
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
, the acoustic part will
be damped
instantaneously
. This damping effect comes from the kinetic-fluid coupled boundary layers. More precisely, we have the following proposition:

Proposition 11.1
.

Let
\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
be defined as (
10.6
). If

\alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
, then

\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))\,,

as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

This proposition is also true for
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(\varepsilon^{\beta})
,
0\leq\beta<frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{1}{2}<\beta<1
. These cases will be treated in a separate paper.

Now we apply Proposition
11.1
to prove the Main Theorem
3.1
, and leave its proof to the next subsection.

11.1.
Strong Convergence in
L^{1}
: Proof of Theorem
3.1

We first show that we can improve the relative compactness of the family of
fluctuations
g_{\varepsilon}
from weak to strong in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
. Indeed,
g_{\varepsilon}
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle g_{\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}+{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}g^{3}_{\varepsilon}}{N_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v\!\cdot\!\mathbb{P}\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}+\left(\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\right)\left(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2}\right)+v\!\cdot\!{\mathbb{Q}}\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}+\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\left(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}+\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}g_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon}}}\frac{g^{2}_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon}}}\,,

where
{\mathcal{P}}
is the projection to Null
({\mathcal{L}})
defined in (
2.11
),
\mathbb{P}
is the Leray projection, and
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P}
.

It has been proved in
[
27
]
that
{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
in
L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(aM\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
, (see (6.41) in
[
27
]
). We can also show that

\mathbb{P}\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathrm{u}\,,\quad\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\theta\,,\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))\,.

(11.1)

Indeed, this convergence is justified in Lemma 5.6 in
[
19
]
. Although the renormalization and decomposition of
g_{\varepsilon}
are different in
[
19
]
and the current paper, the proof of the convergence (
11.1
) can follow the argument in the proof of Lemma 5.6 in
[
19
]
. Furthermore, the Proposition
11.1
yields that

v\!\cdot\!{\mathbb{P}}^{\perp}\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}+\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\left(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}+\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon}\right)\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))\,.

Thus
\mathcal{P}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow g=v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}+\left(\tfrac{1}{2}|v|^{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2}\right)\theta
in

L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. The
key nonlinear estimate in
[
3
]
claims that

\sigma\frac{g^{2}_{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon}}}=O(|\log\varepsilon|)\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{\infty}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(aM\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))\,.

It is easy to see that
\frac{\varepsilon\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon}}}
is bounded, hence

\frac{\varepsilon^{2}g^{3}_{\varepsilon}}{N_{\varepsilon}}\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))\,.

(11.2)

We deduce that
g_{\varepsilon}
is
relatively compact in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))

and that every limit
g
has the form (
3.4
), combining the above estimates.

Next, we can also improve the convergence of the moments of

g_{\varepsilon}
. In
[
27
]
, it was proved that the incompressible
part
(\mathbb{P}\langle vg_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,,\langle(\frac{1}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}|v|^{2}-1)g_{\varepsilon}\rangle)
converge to
(\mathrm{u}\,,\theta)
in

C([0,\infty);w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x))
. We also have

(\mathbb{P}\langle vg_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,,\langle(\frac{1}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}|v|^{2}-1)g_{\varepsilon}\rangle)
converge to
(\mathrm{u}\,,\theta)
in
L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))
.
Now, from Proposition
11.1
, we know that the
acoustic part
{\mathbb{Q}}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle
and
\langle(\frac{1}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}|v|^{2}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle
converge strongly to
0
in

L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))
. So combining this with
(
11.2
), we get

\displaystyle\langle vg_{\varepsilon}\rangle\rightarrow\mathrm{u}

\displaystyle\mbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}))\cap C([0,\infty);w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x;\mathbb{R}^{{\mathrm{D}}}))\,,

\displaystyle\langle(\tfrac{1}{{\mathrm{D}}}|v|^{2}-1)g_{\varepsilon}\rangle\rightarrow\theta

\displaystyle\hbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x;\mathbb{R}))\cap C([0,\infty);w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x;\mathbb{R}))\,.

Furthermore, since now we have
\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathrm{u}
and

\tilde{\theta}\rightarrow\theta
in
L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))
, we can improve the Quadratic Limit Theorem 13.1 in
[
27
]

to

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathrm{u}\otimes\mathrm{u}\,,\quad\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon}\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\mathrm{u}\theta\,,\quad\tilde{\theta}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\theta^{2}\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x))\,,

(11.3)

as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Let
s\in(0,\infty]
be from the assumed bound (
2.7
) on

b
. Let
p=2+\frac{1}{s-1}
, so that
p=2
when
s=\infty
. Let

\hat{\xi}\in L^{p}(aM\mathrm{d}v)
be such that
\mathcal{P}\hat{\xi}=0
and
set
\xi=\mathcal{L}\hat{\xi}
, hence,

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle\xi\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle\xi\mathcal{P}^{\perp}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle=\langle\hat{\xi}\mathcal{Q}(\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})\rangle-\langle\!\langle\hat{\xi}\tilde{q}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\!\rangle+\langle\!\langle\hat{\xi}T_{\varepsilon}\rangle\!\rangle\,.

We know from in
[
27
]
that

\langle\!\langle\hat{\xi}T_{\varepsilon}\rangle\!\rangle\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x))\,,

(11.4)

and

\langle\!\langle\hat{\xi}\tilde{q}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\!\rangle\rightarrow\langle\xi\widehat{\mathrm{A}}\rangle:\nabla_{\!x}u+\langle\xi\widehat{\mathrm{B}}\rangle\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\theta\quad\mbox{in}\!\!\quad w\mbox{-}L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))\,.

(11.5)

Note that

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\xi}\mathcal{Q}(\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})\rangle=\langle\hat{\xi}\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{P}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,\mathcal{P}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})\rangle

\displaystyle+2\langle\hat{\xi}\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{P}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,\mathcal{P}^{\perp}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle\hat{\xi}\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{P}^{\perp}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,\mathcal{P}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})\rangle\,.

It is easy to show that the last two terms above vanish as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. For the first term,

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\xi}\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{P}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,\mathcal{P}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\xi\mathcal{P}^{\perp}(\mathcal{P}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})^{2}\rangle

(11.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\xi\mathrm{A}\rangle:(\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon})+\langle\xi\mathrm{B}\rangle\!\cdot\!\tilde{\mathrm{u}}_{\varepsilon}\tilde{\theta}_{\varepsilon}+\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\xi\mathrm{C}\rangle\tilde{\theta}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,.

Applying the quadratic limit (
11.3
), (
11.6
) can be
taken limit in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x))
strongly. Combining
with convergence (
11.4
) and (
11.5
), we get

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle\xi\mathcal{P}^{\perp}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\rightarrow\left\langle\xi\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{A}:\mathrm{u}\otimes\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{B}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{u}\theta+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{C}\theta^{2}-\widehat{\mathrm{A}}:\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{u}-\widehat{\mathrm{B}}\!\cdot\!\nabla_{\!x}\theta\right)\right\rangle

in
w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x))
. Since

g_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0
in
L^{\infty}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{1}(\sigma M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))
, the convergence above implies (
3.8
). Thus we finish
the proof of the Main Theorem
3.1
.

11.2.
Proof of Proposition
11.1

We will reduce the proof of the Proposition
11.1
to
show that the projection of
\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
on each
fixed
acoustic mode goes to zero in
L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))
.
We know that
\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded in

L^{\infty}(\mathrm{d}t;L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))
, so it can be represented as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\langle\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon},U^{+,k}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}U^{+,k}+\langle\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon},U^{-,k}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}U^{-,k}

(11.7)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2{\mathrm{D}}}\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{2{\mathrm{D}}}{({\mathrm{D}}+2)^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\langle|v|^{2}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\overline{\Psi^{k}}\,\mathrm{d}x\!\!\!\quad\Psi^{k}\\
2\int_{\Omega}\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\!\cdot\!\frac{\nabla_{\!x}\overline{\Psi^{k}}}{i\lambda^{k}}\,\mathrm{d}x\!\!\!\quad\frac{\nabla_{\!x}\Psi^{k}}{i\lambda^{k}}\\
\tfrac{4}{({\mathrm{D}}+2)^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\langle|v|^{2}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle\overline{\Psi^{k}}\,\mathrm{d}x\!\!\!\quad\Psi^{k}\end{pmatrix}\,,

recalling that the inner product
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}
is defined in (
5.2
),
U^{+,k}
and
U^{-,k}
are defined in (
5.7
).

The above summation includes infinitely many terms. To reduce the
problem to a finite number of modes, we need some regularity in
x

of
\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle
and
\langle|v|^{2}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle
. The tool adapted to
investigating this property is the velocity averaging theorem given
in
[
14
]
and the improvement to
L^{1}
averaging in
[
18
]
.

Following the similar argument in the proof of Proposition 11.2 in
[
27
]
, and apply to (
10.2
), we can show that
for each
\zeta\in\mbox{Span}\{1\,,v\,,|v|^{2}\}
and
T>0
, there exists a function
\eta:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\lim\limits_{z\rightarrow 0^{+}}\eta(z)=0
,

\|\langle\zeta\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t,x+y,v)-\zeta\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t,x,v)\rangle\|_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega)}\leq\eta(|y|)\,,

(11.8)

for every
y\in\Omega
such that
|y|\leq 1
, uniformly in
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
. From the classical criterion of compactness in
L^{2}
,
\langle v\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle
and
|v|^{2}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}
are relatively compact in
L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t\,,L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))
which implies that

\sum\limits_{k>N}\int^{T}_{0}\left|\langle\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon},U^{\tau,k}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{N}\|\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}([t_{1},t_{2}];L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))}\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{as}\quad N\rightarrow\infty\,,

(11.9)

recalling from (
11.8
) that
C_{N}\rightarrow 0
as

N\rightarrow\infty
. (
11.9
) implies that, to show

\Pi^{\perp}\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0
strongly in

L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t\,,L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x))
, we need only to prove
that
\langle\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon},U^{\tau,k}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}

converges strongly to
0
in
L^{2}(0,T)
for any fixed acoustic mode

k
. Furthermore, the relation

\langle\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon},U^{\tau,k}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x

implies that the proof of Proposition
11.1
is
reduced to showing that :

Proposition 11.2
.

Assume that
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
and
let
\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}
be the renormalized fluctuation defined in
(
10.3
), satisfying the scaled Boltzmann equation
(
10.2
), and
g^{\tau,k,int}_{0}
(
\tau
is + or -) be the infinitesimal
Maxwellian of acoustic mode
k\geq 1
:

g^{\tau,k,int}_{0}=\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\Psi^{k}+\tfrac{\nabla_{\!x}\Psi^{k}}{\tau i\lambda^{k}}\cdot v+\tfrac{2}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\Psi^{k}(\tfrac{|v|^{2}}{2}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}}{2})\,.

Then, for any fixed mode
k
,

\int_{\Omega}\left\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\rightarrow 0\,\quad\mbox{in}\quad L^{2}(0,T)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\,.

Proof.

We start from the weak formulation of the rescaled Boltzmann
equation (
2.24
) with the renormalization
\Gamma
defined in (
10.1
) and the test function
Y
taken to be
the approximate eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}

constructed in Proposition
7.1
to the order
N=4
,
namely
Y=g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t_{2})g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t_{1})g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x

(11.10)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\langle\gamma\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\gamma g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle R_{\varepsilon}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\!\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\,,

where

R_{\varepsilon}=\Gamma^{\prime}(G_{\varepsilon})q_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{g_{\varepsilon 1}}{N_{\varepsilon 1}}+\frac{g_{\varepsilon}}{N_{\varepsilon}}-\frac{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}}{N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}}-\frac{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}{N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\right)\,.

Define

\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)=\int_{\Omega}\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t)g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Then from (
11.10
)
\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)
satisfies

\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t_{2})-\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t_{1})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\overline{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}}\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}c^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,,

(11.11)

where
c^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)
is:

\displaystyle c^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t)R^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}\langle\gamma\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\gamma g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

(11.12)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle R_{\varepsilon}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\!\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

We claim that the boundary contribution in (
11.12
) is zero as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, i.e.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}
be the approximate eigenfunction of

{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}
constructed in Proposition
7.1
. Then,

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int\limits_{\partial\Omega}\langle\gamma\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\gamma g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}=\Gamma^{\tau,k}_{1}+\Gamma^{\tau,k}_{2}\,,

(11.13)

where
\Gamma^{\tau,k}_{1}
is bounded in

L^{p}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)
for
p>1
, and
\Gamma^{\tau,k}_{2}
vanishes in

L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

We leave the proof of Lemma
11.1
to the section

11.5
.

11.3.
Estimates of
c^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}

We will decompose
c^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)
into two parts: one is
vanishing in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)
, the other is bounded in

L^{p}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)
for some
p>1
. First, taking
N=4
,

r=p=2
in (
7.10
) and noticing the

L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t\,,L^{2}(aM\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x))

boundedness of
\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}
, we have the estimate of the first term in
(
11.12
):

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(t)R^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|R^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\|_{L^{2}(\frac{1}{a}M\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x)}\|\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(aM\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x)}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,.

Second, Lemma
11.1
implies that one part of the boundary term
in (
11.12
), namely
\Gamma^{\tau,k}_{2}
in (
11.13
) will be vanishing in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)

as
\varepsilon
goes to zero. The third term in (
11.12
) is
estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle R_{\varepsilon}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\!\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x

(11.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle R_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{P}}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\!\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle R_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\!\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

For the first term in the right-hand side of (
11.14
), because
{\mathcal{P}}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}
is in Null
({\mathcal{L}})
,, it has the form of

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle\Gamma^{\prime}(G_{\varepsilon})q_{\varepsilon}\zeta\rangle\!\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\langle{\mathcal{L}}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\zeta\rangle\mathrm{d}x\,,\quad\mbox{for some}\quad\zeta(t,x)\in\mbox{Null}({\mathcal{L}})\,.

(11.15)

The second term above is zero, the first term converges to zero
strongly in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
by the
Conservation Defect Theorem (Proposition 8.1) in

[
27
]
.

For the second term in the right-hand side of
(
11.14
), from the calculations in Proposition

7.1
again, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\tfrac{{\mathrm{D}}+2}{2{\mathrm{D}}}}\left(\tfrac{\nabla_{\!x}^{2}\Psi^{k}}{\tau i\lambda^{k}}\!:\!\widehat{\mathrm{A}}+\tfrac{2\nabla_{\!x}\Psi^{k}}{{\mathrm{D}}+2}\!\cdot\!\widehat{\mathrm{B}}\right)+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!\otimes\!\partial_{\!\zeta}\mathrm{u}^{\tau,k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\!:\!{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}+\partial_{\!\zeta}\theta^{\tau,k,\mathrm{b}}_{0}\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\!\cdot\!{\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}\right)

(11.16)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{bb}}_{1}+\mbox{higher order terms}\,.

We decompose
R_{\varepsilon}
into

R_{\varepsilon}=T_{\varepsilon}+(\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1}\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon})+q_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{2}{N^{2}_{\varepsilon}}-\frac{1}{N_{\varepsilon}}-\frac{1}{N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}N_{\varepsilon 1}N_{\varepsilon}}\right)\,,

(11.17)

where
T_{\varepsilon}
is

T_{\varepsilon}=\frac{q_{\varepsilon}}{N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}N_{\varepsilon 1}N_{\varepsilon}}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}+\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1}-\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon})-(\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1}\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1})\,.

When we integrate (
11.16
) over
\Omega
, for the second
term of (
11.16
) which is a function of

(\pi(x)\,,\frac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}})
, we make the change of
variables:

y_{1}=\pi^{1}(x)\,,\cdots\,,y_{{\mathrm{D}}-1}=\pi^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}(x)\,,y_{\mathrm{D}}=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\,.

(11.18)

Then
\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{T}^{*}\mathrm{d}y
, where

\mathrm{T}^{*}=\mathrm{det}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\!x}\pi\\
\nabla_{\!x}\mathrm{d}\end{pmatrix}>0\,.

This extra

\sqrt{\varepsilon}
cancels with the
\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{-1}
in the second term
of (
11.16
). Similarly, for the third term of
(
11.16
), we make the change of variables

y_{1}=\pi(x)^{1}\,,\cdots\,,y_{{\mathrm{D}}-1}=\pi(x)^{{\mathrm{D}}-1}\,,y_{\mathrm{D}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}(x)}{\varepsilon}
,
and consequently
\mathrm{d}x=\varepsilon\mathrm{T}^{*}\mathrm{d}y
. Thus, for
the integral of (
11.16
), the first two terms are the same
order, namely
O(1)
, while the third term is of order
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
, and the rest term is even higher order in
\varepsilon
. By the Flux Remainder Theorem (Proposition 10.1) of

[
27
]
, we have

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle T_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\!\rangle\mathrm{d}x\rightarrow 0\,,\quad\mbox{in}\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)\,,

as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Furthermore, from the Bilinear estimates (Lemma 9.1) of
[
27
]
, we have

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle(\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1}\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1}){\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\!\rangle\mathrm{d}x\leq C\int_{\Omega}\langle a\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}^{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C\,.

The third term in (
11.17
) can be written as

q_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon}}
times a bounded sequence that vanishes almost
everywhere as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Then the
L^{2}(\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t)
boundedness of
q_{\varepsilon}/\sqrt{N_{\varepsilon}}

implies that it times (
11.16
) is relatively compact in

w\mbox{-}L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t;w\mbox{-}L^{1}(\mathrm{d}x))
, then
following from the Product Limit Theorem of
[
3
]
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle q_{\varepsilon}\left(\tfrac{2}{N^{2}_{\varepsilon}}-\tfrac{1}{N_{\varepsilon}}-\tfrac{1}{N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}N_{\varepsilon 1}N_{\varepsilon}}\right){\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\!\rangle\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\rightarrow 0\,,\quad\mbox{in}\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\,.

Now we can decompose
c^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)
into

c^{\tau,k}_{1,\varepsilon}(t)=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\!\langle(\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon 1}\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1}\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon 1}){\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\!\rangle\mathrm{d}x-\Gamma^{\tau,k}_{1}\,,

and
c^{\tau,k}_{2,\varepsilon}(t)=c^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)-c^{\tau,k}_{1,\varepsilon}(t)
,
where
\Gamma^{\tau,k}_{1}
appears in (
11.13
).

The above arguments show that
c^{\tau,k}_{2,\varepsilon}(t)\rightarrow 0
, in

L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)
, and Lemma
11.1
gives that
c^{\tau,k}_{1,\varepsilon}(t)
is bounded in

L^{p}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)
for some
p>1
.

11.4.
Estimates of
\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}

From (
11.11
),
\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the ordinary
differential equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\overline{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}}\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}=c^{\tau,k}_{1,\varepsilon}(t)+c^{\tau,k}_{2,\varepsilon}(t)\,.

(11.19)

The solution to
(
11.19
)
is given by

\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(t)=\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(0)e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\overline{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}}t}+\int^{t}_{0}[c^{\tau,k}_{1,\varepsilon}(s)+c^{\tau,k}_{2,\varepsilon}(s)]e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\overline{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}}(s-t)}\,\mathrm{d}s\,.

(11.20)

From the Proposition
7.1
,
i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}=\tau i\lambda^{k}+i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1}\sqrt{\varepsilon}+i\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau,k}_{1}\varepsilon
, where
\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau,k}_{1}=O(1)
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\overline{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left[\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{Re}(i\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau,k}_{1})\right]t

(11.21)

\displaystyle-i\left[\tau\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lambda^{k}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{Im}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})+\mathrm{Im}(i\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau,k}_{1})\right]t\,.

Using (
11.21
), the first term in (
11.20
) is estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\|\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(0)e^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\overline{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}}t}\|_{L^{2}(0,T)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(0)|\left[-2\left(\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{Re}(i\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau,k}_{1})\right)\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(1-e^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}[\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathrm{Re}(i\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau,k}_{1})]T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}}\,.

To estimate
|\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(0)|
, from

\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(0)=\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}\,,g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x+\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}\,,g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}-g^{k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,,

noticing that
g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\in
Null
(\mathcal{L})
and

\|\langle\zeta(v)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}\rangle\|_{L^{2}(\mathrm{d}x)}

is bounded for every
\zeta(v)\in
Null
(\mathcal{L})
, and the error
estimate for
g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}-g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}
in
(
7.11
), we deduce that
|\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(0)|
is
bounded. Using the key fact that

\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})<0
, we deduce that for any
0<T<\infty
, sufficiently small

\varepsilon
:

\|\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}(0)e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\overline{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}}t}\|_{L^{2}(0,T)}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}}\,.

In order to estimate the remaining term in
(
11.20
)
, we
observe that for any
a\in L^{p}(0,t)
and
1\leq p,r\leq\infty
,
such that
p^{-1}+r^{-1}=1
, we have

\Big|\int^{t}_{0}a(s)e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\overline{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}}(s-t)}\,\mathrm{d}s\Big|\leq C\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})(s-t)}|a(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s\,.

Direct calculations show that

\left\|e^{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})(t-s)}\right\|_{L^{r}(0,t)}=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2r}}\left[\frac{1}{-r\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})}\left(e^{-\frac{r}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})t}-1\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{r}}e^{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})t}\,.

Using the fact
\mathrm{Re}(i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{1})<0

again, we have

\Big|\int^{t}_{0}a(s)e^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\overline{i\lambda^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}}(s-t)}\,\mathrm{d}s\Big|\leq C\|a\|_{L^{p}(0,t)}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2r}}\,.

(11.22)

Now applying
a(t)
in (
11.22
) to

c^{\tau,k}_{1,\varepsilon}
and
c^{\tau,k}_{2,\varepsilon}
, finally we get:

\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0\,,\quad\mbox{strongly in}\quad L^{2}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)\,.

To finish the proof of the Proposition, we notice that

\int\limits_{\Omega}\left\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x=\tilde{b}^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon}+\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\,,g^{\tau,k,\mathrm{int}}_{0}-g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Applying the error estimate (
7.11
) in Proposition
7.1
, we finish the proof of the Proposition
11.2
.
∎
Consequently, we prove the Proposition
11.1
.

11.5.
Proof of Lemma
11.1

Proof.

Using the boundary condition of
g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}
, namely

(
7.3
)
, simple calculations yields that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\partial\Omega}\langle\gamma\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\gamma g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{-}}\gamma_{\!-}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\gamma_{\!-}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})M\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

(11.23)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\left[(1-\alpha_{\epsilon})L\gamma_{\!-}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}+\alpha_{\epsilon}\langle\gamma_{\!-}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}})+r^{k}_{\varepsilon,4}\right](v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})M\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{-}}\gamma_{-}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}\left[\gamma_{-}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}-(1-\alpha_{\varepsilon})L\gamma_{+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}-\alpha_{\varepsilon}\langle\gamma_{+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle_{{\partial\Omega}}\right](v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})M\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}r^{k}_{\varepsilon,4}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})M\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}(\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}-L\gamma_{\!-}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})\mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}(\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}-\langle\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega})\mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}r^{k}_{\varepsilon,4}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})M\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\,,

where the measure

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}=L\gamma_{-}g^{\tau,k}_{\varepsilon,4}(v\cdot\mathrm{n})M\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}
. From the boundary error estimate (
7.12
) in Proposition

7.1
(letting
r=\infty,p=2
), and the fact that
\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}
is bounded in

L^{1}(\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x},L^{2}(aM\mathrm{d}v))
, it is easy to see that

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}r^{k}_{\varepsilon,4}(v\!\cdot\!\mathrm{n})M\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{x}\rightarrow 0\,,\quad\mbox{in}\quad L^{1}_{loc}(\mathrm{d}t)\,,

as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

It remains to show that the first two terms in the righthand side of
(
11.23
) go to zero as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. It again
follows from the
a priori
estimates Lemma
10.1
and

10.2
. The main difficulty is
\alpha_{\epsilon}/\varepsilon\rightarrow\infty
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, since
\alpha_{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{2\pi}\chi\sqrt{\varepsilon}
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}(\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}-L\gamma_{\!-}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})\mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}(\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}-\langle\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega})\mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}

(11.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}(\gamma_{\!+}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}-L\gamma_{\!-}\tilde{g}_{\epsilon})\mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha_{\epsilon}}{\varepsilon}\iint\limits_{\Sigma_{+}}\left(\frac{\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}^{2}}-\frac{\langle\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\partial\Omega}}{1+\varepsilon^{2}\gamma_{\!+}g_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
